palpable. In setting the example of their own lives—the words Reverence for
Life carved into the lintel at the entrance to the hospital—the lives of those who
came to Deschapelles to serve as physicians and health care givers were often
changed forever. Today, even with the incredibly challenging condition of life
in Haiti, those who work here keep the needs of the patients at the forefront of
all that they do.

Brothers in Spirit

Hôpital Albert Schweitzer has provided health care to an increasing
population, now almost 300,000, for almost fifty years. It has saved many lives
through preventive measures and medical care. It has helped the people within
the district improve their economic lot through agricultural assistance and has
provided jobs and education for many people.

Beyond that, and almost as important, it has been a place which has inspired
others to find their own Lambarénés. Dr. Schweitzer said that it was not possible
for everyone to go to Africa and build a hospital but that everyone could, in his
own life, find a way to serve others. Serving others is important, not because
you expect to have your favors returned, but because it makes you a better
person. People who go to Deschapelles say that it changes their lives forever.
No one is quite the same after spending time working under the umbrella of
Reverence for Life in this remote corner of Haiti.

I know that Mom and Larry enjoyed their lives there immensely. Larry
wrote, "For half a lifetime [Dr. Schweitzer's] example has inspired Gwen and
me to greater usefulness—and as a consequence, to greater happiness."

May the example of Dr. Schweitzer's life live on forever, inspiring others to
care for their "brothers and sisters" around the world, through which they might
find true happiness.

Part Two

Schweitzer and Philosophy

CHAPTER FIVE

THE REVERENCE FOR LIFE
AND THE ENVIRONMENT

EDWARD D'ANGELO

Albert Schweitzer has said that the phrase "Reverence for Life" flashed
through his mind while he was on a barge in Africa in 1915, and that this phrase
became the cornerstone for his philosophy of life. What is the meaning of
"reverence?" Reverence for something usually involves a high respect and a
sense of holiness or sacredness. When this is applied to life, it includes
sustaining and cherishing all living things and not destroying them. In this
sense, the principle of Reverence for Life is universal, since all life has value.
According to Schweitzer, the Reverence for Life has three main aspects:
1) The will to live: All living things strive to exist and preserve their
existence by meeting certain physical needs. This is an obvious truth about
human beings and is also true about all other living things. There is a desire for
the continuation of life and an affirmation of the will to live. This entails a
reverence for all life besides my own, since it "longs for fullness and
development as I do myself."1 In other words, humans possess a will to live and
exist among other living things that also have a will to live.

2) The will to self-realization: This is the use of one's power to maximize
potentialities. This involves the growth and development of living things. It
would also include the procreation of a species. However, the realization of
potentiality is contingent on a set of relations outside of the individual living
thing.

3) The will to relatedness: Our existence and the realization of one's
potentiality are dependent upon a set of relations outside of us. As humans, we
are dependent upon our family and other human beings who provide various
services to meet our physical, emotional, educational, economic, social, and
cultural needs. Nonhuman animals and plants are also dependent upon a set of
favorable environmental conditions to survive and continue living. Humans are
not limited to human relations, but experience relatedness to other animals,

Chapter Five

plants, and the rest of the natural environment. Some people also develop a
religious attitude in their relatedness to the universe.

When we subject Schweitzer's principle of Reverence for Life to critical
analysis, some problems arise. The will to live, for example, would not permit
us to kill anything. This is unrealistic, since all animals need to eat other living
things in order to survive. Would this entail vegetarianism? It might, however,
both sentient and non-sentient life have value. It would be purely arbitrary to
claim that it is acceptable to eat an eggplant but not a pig. Additionally, the
killing of buffalo by Native Americans was necessary for survival; all parts of
the animal were used for other purposes besides food. Another problem
presented by the will to live is that it is not present in all human beings in all
stages of life. Some people commit suicide and others suffering terminal
illnesses do not have a desire to continue living.

Schweitzer was aware of these issues. He wrote, "The world offers us the
horrible drama of will to life divided against itself. One existence holds its own
at the cost of another: one destroys another. … But as an ethical being he strives
to escape whenever possible from this necessity."2 Although all life is sacred,
humans sometimes need to decide which of two lives are to be sacrificed
because of the necessities of life. This is witnessed in self-defense situations,
including abortions for mothers whose physical lives are endangered as well as
ectopic pregnancies. Also, saving a human life sometimes requires using
antibiotics to destroy particular microorganisms.

The will to self-realization entails an ethic of creating favorable conditions
for the development of all life. This includes the care and feeding of all plants
and animals whenever it is necessary and feasible as well as rescuing living
things whenever they are in peril. Schweitzer mentions the responsibility of
rescuing an insect that has fallen in a pool of water. However, this viewpoint
might entail a justification of killing a living thing that is no longer capable of
self-realization. A diseased plant or animal that cannot be cured could justifiably
be killed. This would include humans who are comatose and in a persistent
vegetative state (euthanasia). Schweitzer maintains that when "the suffering of a
living creature cannot be alleviated, it is more ethical to end its life mercifully
than it is to stand aloof."3 Schweitzer's view seems inconsistent, since he
believes that the Reverence for Life is an absolute ethic. However, he doesn't
mean that it has no exceptions, but that it "calls for the creating of perfection in
this life. It cannot be completely achieved."4 It is an ideal we pursue in life and a
responsible attitude toward all living things.

Humans are social animals and their existence and self-realization are
dependent upon a multitude of human and environmental conditions.5 Sympathy
is rooted into our mutual dependence on each other. According to Schweitzer,
sympathy is a natural aspect by which we can imagine living things possessing

Reverence and the Environment

certain characteristics that we find in our own lives. "That is the dread of
extinction, fear of pain, and the desire for happiness."6 As humans we can have
sympathy with other living things and assist them whenever it is necessary. This
is the basis of rescuing others. Just as we would want to be rescued from a
burning car while experiencing fear and pain, we can expect others would
experience similar reactions under the same conditions. Schweitzer calls this
sympathy, but actually it is empathy. Empathy is the ability to put yourself in
another person's place. This is the impetus that enables humans to care for and
love others.

Schweitzer begins his theory on an ontological level by observing certain
features of living things and then progresses to a moral level by asserting how
people ought to behave. His principle of the Reverence for Life demonstrates
this by using "is" and "if, then" statements to justify "ought" statements. All
things have a will to live. If you want to preserve your existence and other living
things, then you ought to sustain and not destroy them unless it is necessary for
survival. All living things have a will to self-realization. If you want all living
things to achieve self-realization, then you ought to develop your potentialities
and also assist others to do so. All things have a will to relatedness. If you want
to maximize your relatedness and solidarity with other living things, you ought
to care and love them. But some people are indifferent and apathetic and are
unwilling to accept these goals. Schweitzer's response might be that it is in their
self-interest to do so. Their own will to live, self-realization and relatedness to
others cannot be fulfilled without the concern of others. We live in an
interdependent world and our existence is affected by a system of relations.
Schweitzer's argument begins from an egoist position and it is extended to
altruism. The altruist position is then justified as a means of satisfying the
original egoist position.

The use of Schweitzer's principle of Reverence for Life can have a profound
impact on how humans treat the environment. The Reverence for Life rejects the
notion that humans can use the environment for its own purposes without any
consideration of its consequences for other living things. It accepts the view that
there is a reciprocal relationship among living things. Each species is linked to
many others. A deer depends on plants for food. Plants depend on the action of
soil bacteria for nutrients, and bacteria live on organic wastes dropped by
animals on the soil. This multiplied many times in complex relationships
determines the network of life on earth. In every natural system, what is
excreted by one organism as waste is taken by another as food. Animals release
carbon dioxide and plants use it as a nutrient; they release oxygen which is used
by animals for their sustenance. Nature has taken billions of years to evolve
ecosystems that are compatible with each other.

Chapter Five

The interdependence of living things with the physical environment means
that any change in the system will affect other things within that network. There
is no free lunch, so to speak. For the advantage of automobile transportation, for
example, we pay a price of smog and disease. For the advantage of powerful
insecticides, we pay the price of dwindling wildlife and an unstable ecology. For
the advantage of nuclear power, we risk the danger of radiation. All these gains
are at a cost. The problem occurs when these gains affect the well-being of
living things. The will to life, self-realization, and relatedness is incompatible
with any ecological changes that would hamper and destroy life. We are part of
the environment and have a responsibility to preserve it. In fact, it is in our own
self-interest to do so, since human existence and the quality of life is affected by
our activities in relation to the environment. We also have a moral obligation to
future generations who will hopefully live in a world where the air, water, and
soil is not contaminated with toxic substances.

Schweitzer's views are consistent with a holistic approach to environmental
ethics. Aldo Leopold is one of the foremost supporters of this viewpoint. For
Leopold, "all ethics so far evolved rest on a single premise; that the individual is
a member of a community of interdependent parts. . . . The land ethic simply
enlarges the boundaries of the community to include soil, water, plants, and
animals, or collectively the land."7

The land ethic changes the role of humans from conqueror of the land to a
member of the community. This implies a respect for other members as well as
the community itself. This would be a manifestation of Schweitzer's Reverence
for Life. Sylvan and Plumwood have also suggested a land ethic that respects
other members of the biotic community. They claim that the Native American
environmental attitude provides a good example of limited and respectful use.8
This position would conflict with the unlimited consumerism in some industrial
nations, and would require set limits on the use of certain lands.

The implementation of the Reverence for Life toward the environment
necessitates government policies that are consistent with this principle. This
requires national legislative acts and international treaties to enforce compliance
of certain behaviors toward the natural environment. The Environmental
Protection Agency (EPA) was created in 1970 to implement legislation
enforcing programs that govern air, water pollution, radiation, and toxic
substances. The Clean Air Act, Clean Water Act, and the Toxic Substances
Control Act are some of the acts that have been passed by Congress. There have
been many violations of these acts that have resulted in substantial fines and
prison sentences. However, there are limits as to what can be achieved through
the legal process. Legal actions against corporations and corporate officers can
take many years before a decision is rendered. Individuals can violate federal
and state statutes and not be apprehended. There is also the difficulty of tracking

Reverence and the Environment

all offenders because of the lack of technicians to verify various violations. We
need to develop an ethical attitude within the business community concerning its
behavior toward the natural environment.

An additional problem occurs when the political leadership is willing to
forego its commitment to the environment in favor of the economic interests of
particular corporations. The administration of George W. Bush is a prime
example of this type of leadership. It has allowed electric utilities to continue
spewing tons of mercury, sulphur and carbon dioxide (and other greenhouse
gases) into the air in violation of the Clean Air Act. It has proposed a Clear
Skies bill that would weaken the Clean Air Act. Fortunately, on March 9, 2005,
the Senate Environment Committee rejected the bill. It has also supported oil
drilling in the Arctic National Wildlife Refuge (ANWR) that would destroy a
large wilderness area. Interestingly, on 16 March 2005, the Senate approved a
provision in the 2006 budget that would allow oil drilling in the ANWR.

The assault on the environment is also perpetuated by wars. The use of
depleted uranium weapons, that are radioactive and toxic, in Iraq in 1991,
Bosnia in 1995, and Serbia in 1999 have endangered troops, the general
population, and the natural environment. Massive bombings that have killed
thousands of people and the deaths caused by suicide bombers are a violation of
the Reverence for Life.

How should we proceed in the future to incorporate Dr. Schweitzer's
philosophy? There needs to be a greater cooperation among nations on a
political and economic level to resolve environmental issues. Environmental
problems do not stop at a nation's borders, but affect other nations, e.g., global
warming. Additionally, the world population that is currently about 6.3 billion is
expected to increase to 9 billion by 2050. We need to decrease the growth of the
world population or else experience huge problems of human survival and
environmental degradation. Since government leaders are sometimes not
responsive to these problems, a massive support of environmental groups is
necessary to bring these issues into public awareness. The Sierra Club,
Greenpeace, the National Resources Defense Council, and other organizations
have been instrumental in supporting various environmental projects, and
sometimes victorious in their challenges of governmental policies that have
endangered the natural environment.

We can also learn from countries that are good models of instituting
ecological changes. Cuba has begun to reforest thirty percent of its land, reduce
significantly chemical fertilizers and instead uses diverse organic compounds,
preserve wildlife and several plant species in national parks, and has made a
commitment to biological diversity.

Since we cannot always rely on governments to make the necessary
ecological changes because of their different political and economic interests

Chapter Five

and goals, the Reverence for Life needs to be part of the character structure of
humanity. It should be an integral part of a school curriculum with practical
applications in the community. In 1963, Schweitzer said that a teacher in a
German school was successful in teaching the principle of the Reverence for
Life and that it was also taught in Turkey and Korea.9

The right to a liveable environment is a fundamental human right. Each
person possesses this right, since human existence and the development of
human potentialities are dependent upon a set of relations within the biotic
community. As Schweitzer said, "This then is the nature and origin of ethics.
We have dared to say that it is born of physical life, out of the linking of life
with life. It is therefore the result of our recognizing the solidarity of life which
nature gives us. And as it grows more profound, it teaches us sympathy with all
life. Yet the extremes touch, for this material-born ethic becomes engraved in
our hearts, and culminates in spiritual union and harmony with the Creative Will
which is in and through all."10

In the future, the Reverence for Life needs to be engraved in all our hearts.

Notes

1. Albert Schweitzer, "The Ethics of Reverence for Life," Christendom 1.2 (Winter
1936): 230.
2. Albert Schweitzer, Out of My Life and Thought (New York: Henry Holt, 1948), 188.
3. Albert Schweitzer, Indian Thought and its Development (Boston: Beacon Press, 1936),
83.
4. Schweitzer, "The Ethics of Reverence for Life," 232.
5. Ibid., 237.
6. Ibid.
7. Aldo Leopold, A Sand County Almanac (New York: Ballantine, 1966), 239.
8. R. Sylvan and V. Plumwood, "Human Chauvism and Environmental Ethics," in D.
Manson, M. McRobbie, and R. Routley eds., Environmental Philosophy (Canberra,
Australia: Australian National University, 1980), 178-79.
9. Albert Schweitzer. "Letter from Lambaréné," The Living Age (October, 1963), 70.
10. Schweitzer, "The Ethics of Reverence for Life," 239.

CHAPTER SIX

REVERENCE FOR LIFE AND THE EMERGING
GLOBAL CIVILIZATION

JOHN D. RADIGAN

If one scans the standard histories and textbooks of philosophy and ethics
commonly used in colleges and universities, at least one name—that of Albert
Schweitzer—is conspicuous by its absence. We still occasionally hear him
lauded as one of the great geniuses in human history, a titanic figure on par with
earlier giants like Leonardo da Vinci. But there persists a strange, almost
embarrassed, silence—an uneasy shuffling of intellectual feet—if the topic of
his ethics of Reverence for Life is mentioned in polite academic circles. Though
Schweitzer himself regarded his ethical thought as the most important of his
many stellar contributions in a variety of fields, conventional wisdom among
mainline philosophers has long held that it is too vague, too self-contradictory,
or too overtly mystical to be quite respectable and, consequently, that it has little
or no relevance in our practical hard-headed world.

This essay will make a case for exactly the opposite position. It will show
that Dr. Schweitzer's philosophy of civilization, and in particular his ethics of
Reverence for Life, is one of the great philosophical achievements of the 20th
century and perhaps of world history. It will further show that this radical
reassessment of his thought is valuable not just theoretically but also because of
the central role that an accurate understanding of Reverence for Life can play in
humanizing and civilizing our increasingly globalized planet.

This seemingly outrageous claim rests on three basic facts which it is well to
articulate clearly at the very beginning, as they provide a solid foundation for
the argument to follow. First, Schweitzer's mind was extraordinarily precise and
ruthlessly systematic. Such a mind would be simply incapable of turning out the
kind of intellectual mush that many of his would-be "critics" imagine they have
detected. In point of fact, common charges of supposed "vagueness" in his
thought prove unfounded upon careful examination of its content. Second, in
order to understand Reverence for Life one must connect it, as Schweitzer did,
with civilization. Failure to do this (as in the many cases where it has been

Chapter Six

hijacked into discussions about the environment) results in the distortion and
weakening of a powerful argument that is supreme in its proper context. And
third, Schweitzer never intended Reverence for Life to be merely one more
ethical opinion or theory among many others that one is free to accept or reject.
He was after something far more sweeping and comprehensive than that,
namely, a single ethical idea that contains all others. As we will see in what
follows, he succeeded spectacularly in this regard, creating an ethical genome or
force-field that no moral theory or system can possibly escape.

Let us now proceed to examine some evidence related to each of these points
and to consider the implications of what we find. Concerning the powers of
Schweitzer's mind, first of all, we have in his many books ample evidence of his
apparently limitless capacity for exhaustive detail. One of the more delightful
ways of seeing this for oneself is to spend an hour or so with his monumental
two-volume study of J. S. Bach (i.e., the enlarged German version of 1908, the
basis for the English translation of 1911). Since Schweitzer was a worldrenowned organist, it should come as no surprise that part of his treatment
includes extensive and specific information (in Chapter XIV) on the organ of
Bach's time, registration, phrasing, tempo, ornamentation, and changes of
manual.1 But he goes far beyond this to discuss, for example, the distinction
between the old German arched violin bow, whose hairs were given tension by
the performer's thumb, and the flatter Italian bow with its screw-tensioning and
how this impacts on playing Bach's polyphonic violin passages.2 This is
followed up in Volume II (Chapter XXXV) with a breathtakingly specific
analysis of Bach's orchestra, right down to the mouthpieces of the trumpets,
their effect on the sound produced, and differences from brass instruments of
Schweitzer's day.3 Even further, as a foundation for his revolutionary discovery
of Bach's system of "tone-painting," Schweitzer (in Chapter XX) draws on
Goethe, Schiller, Beethoven, Wagner, Schubert, Berlioz, and others to establish
a theory of art adequate for his purposes.4

This passion for precision and thoroughness persisted all the way through
Schweitzer's life until the day he died. Vivid examples can be found in
reminiscences of people who knew him in his later years, one notable case in
point being Dr. Frederick Franck. Though most famous in recent decades as an
artist and the author of such bestselling books as The Zen of Seeing (1973),
Franck spent some time in the late 1950's serving as a dentist at Schweitzer's
Lambaréné hospital. There he had ample opportunity to observe le grand
docteur (then age 84) at close range, and comments on the remarkable powers
of his mind as follows:

I am sure . . . that his memory is an improvement on the electronic computer. . . .
Whether it is in medicine or politics, theology or music, metallurgy or
pharmacology, if Schweitzer knows a fact in any field he knows it precisely. If

Reverence and The Emerging Global Civilization

you talk about coffee cultures he'll ask 'Which coffee?' and mention three
botanical varieties. He will then inform you where this variety originated and
between which altitudes it will grow or not grow. Before you open your mouth he
has tripped you up. Not pedantically, but simply not understanding that other
people's brains are a bit vaguer and less systematic than his.5

As noted earlier, the point of all this is to emphasize that a mind that worked
in this way would be utterly incapable of churning out the kind of vagueness
and inconsistency that so many have claimed to find in Schweitzer's
philosophy. Such putative fuzzy-headedness, if really there, would be an
anomaly of the first order given everything else we know about the man and his
voluminous writings. We are on much surer ground if we reject such
condescending "interpretations" and stretch ourselves instead to follow the
rigorous, precise, and systematic thinking of one of the greatest minds of all
time working at the top of its game.

One of the first places this rigor and precision makes itself felt is in
Schweitzer's treatment of civilization. Those who miss this point quickly fall
into the trap of believing that Schweitzer's central emphasis on Will-to-Live is
the product of some kind of quasi-mystical "revelation" and as such is too weak
and insubstantial to serve as a basis for any philosophy aspiring to credibility.
Certainly the famous story of Schweitzer's epiphany on the Ogowe River in
1915, when the phrase "Reverence for Life" sprang unbidden into his mind, is
often adduced as evidence in support of this notion. But this completely ignores
what he did with that epiphany as he worked it into his ethics.

Schweitzer's placement of Will-to-Live at the center of his thinking does not
derive from the force of some intuition arriving out of nowhere, but rather from
his systematically addressing the problem of where a reliable source of the
optimism required to drive civilization and to counter the corrosive effects of
pessimism must reside. He carries out this thoroughgoing analysis especially in
chapters 11 and 19 of The Philosophy of Civilization, and concludes that all of
our most
Invention, Social
Revolutions/Historical Forces, and Biological Evolutionary Models—are utterly
inadequate and unreliable in this regard.

candidates—Discovery

common

and

Subsequent events have proven this conclusion to be correct. For example,
the discovery of atomic power and the invention of the computer and the
internet have brought with them as much evil as good in the form of terrifying
nuclear arsenals, identity theft, and sexual predators stalking young children in
online chat-rooms. All or most of the revolutionary movements on which
millions of people pinned their hopes throughout the 20th century, from the
Soviets and their gulags to Hitler's "thousand-year Reich" and its death camps
to Mao's brutal "Cultural Revolution" to Pol Pot and his Cambodian "Killing
Fields," have collapsed or been discredited leaving only a trail of destruction

Chapter Six

and misery in their wake. Meanwhile, we tend to comfort ourselves with the
quasi-biological evolutionary idea that some mechanism in the natural order of
things is, one way or another, gradually bringing about the changes or
adaptations we need independent of our actively taking responsibility for
making them. On the popular level, we hear a great deal of talk about genes
(such as the so-called "God gene") in this connection, while the more
intellectual camp is garrisoned by authorities such as E. O. Wilson and Daniel
Dennett, who are much preoccupied with brain physiology and "design-work"
in their effort to unpack our ethical impulses.6 The glacial pace of any ethical
progress based on this approach, however, suggests that Schweitzer's acid
dismissal of any biologically-based view of ethics as nothing more than
"developed herd-mentality"7 was pretty close to the mark.

The fact is, as we survey the history of the 20th century, that none of these
commonly-accepted bases for optimism has proven adequate. What, then,
remains on which to found any kind of enduring optimism or hope for the
future? Where are we to find a truly reliable form of this essential ingredient for
civilization? Only, says Schweitzer, in the elemental energy of Will-to-Live,
which we can see and feel both in ourselves and around ourselves and which
continuously and imperiously drives us forward.

The crucial importance of this quest for a true source of optimism to the
establishment or restoration of civilization becomes instantly clear the minute
we consider one of Schweitzer's representative definitions:

Civilization, put quite simply, consists in our giving ourselves, as human beings,
to the effort to attain the perfecting of the human race and actualization of
progress of every sort in the circumstances of humanity and of the objective
world. This mental attitude, however, involves a double predisposition: firstly,
we must be prepared to act affirmatively toward the world and life; secondly, we
must become ethical. . . . Civilization originates when men become inspired by a
strong and clear determination to attain progress, and consecrate themselves, as a
result of this determination, to the service of life and of the world. It is only in
ethics that we can find the driving force for such action, transcending, as it does,
the limits of our own existence.8

There is no way that one can have such an affirmative attitude of mind, or
such a commitment to progress, service, and action without a living optimism
that cannot be discredited, shaken, or destroyed. Nothing other than Will-to-
Live, demonstrably, has this kind of durability; and putting our faith in anything
else is to put civilization itself at risk, as we have seen over and over again
throughout history.

It may be tempting at first to downplay or dismiss Schweitzer's way of
characterizing civilization as some, such as Pitirim Sorokin, have done; to them,

Reverence and The Emerging Global Civilization

it seems too simplistic, too vague and fuzzy, too wedded to 18th century
"Western" attitudes about progress and the value of the individual to have much
to offer us. Even as he was writing, Schweitzer himself was aware that his
perspective might seem odd and anachronistic in the context of "modern"
thought. "This moral concept of civilization will strike some people as
rationalistic and old-fashioned," he wrote as early as 1915. "It accords better
with the spirit of our times to conceive of civilization as a natural manifestation
of life in the course of human evolution, but one with most interesting
complications. We are concerned, however, not with what is ingenious, but with
what is true."9

Indeed, if we compare Schweitzer's leading points with the general
consensus about civilization that has emerged in the decades since he wrote, we
find that he was right on target. Arnold Toynbee, for example, writing over a
decade later, emphasized the dynamic quality that distinguishes a civilization
from a "primitive" society and worked out his well-known "challenge-andresponse" model. As he himself put it, "Civilizations, it would seem, grow
through an élan which carries them from challenge through response to further
challenge, and their growth has both outward and inward aspects."10 This idea
was later picked up by Sir Kenneth Clark, who noted the importance of
confidence and vigor and went on to remark that the great enemies of
civilization were fear and exhaustion, "both of which make further forward
movement seem pointless or impossible."11 Another prominent thinker, Herbert
J. Muller, writing in the early 1950s, calls attention to the crucial need for both
the material and the spiritual dimensions in civilization, echoing the same point
Schweitzer had made thirty years earlier. "It would seem obvious," he wrote,
"that material wealth and power are the essential basis of civilization and that
they are essentially only the basis, the means to other ends . . . Yet the values of
mind or spirit remain the 'higher' values, the ends of civilized life."12 And even
more recently (1996), we find Samuel P. Huntington arguing in his book The
Clash of Civilizations and the Remaking of World Order that modernization
(which is different from Westernization) may be emerging as the basis for a new
universal civilization.13 This point is significant in the context of our present
discussion because it was during the 18th century—the very century on which
Schweitzer had focused 50 years before—that this modernization broke out full
force with the Enlightenment and the Industrial Revolution.

All this subsequent discussion is implicit in the way Schweitzer returns to
his great theme of civilization at the beginning of his very last chapter, "The
Civilizing Power of Reverence for Life." As he puts it, "defined from outside
and quite empirically, complete civilization consists in realizing all possible
progress in discovery and invention and in the arrangements of human society,

Chapter Six

and seeing that they work together for the spiritual perfecting of individuals
which is the real and final object of civilization."14

Partial civilizations, of course, abound, just as partial or fragmentary ethical
systems abound. Indeed, perhaps all civilizations that have ever been have fallen
short of this standard in one way or another. Traditional Indian civilization is
partial in its neglect of science and technology; Chinese and Islamic
civilizations are partial because of their reluctance to modify traditional social
structures and to honor the individual more fully; and Western civilization is
partial in its imbalance of material over spiritual. We should especially note here
that Schweitzer is not advancing a particularly "Western" view of civilization
but rather a generic one which includes all the components identified in his
earlier definition, one far more precise and comprehensive than it appears to be
at first glance. It may truly be said that all future discussions of civilization are
in one way or another contained in his general description of it (just as all
discussions of ethics are already contained, as we will see, in the idea of
Reverence for Life). And everything depends on the solid optimism and
unfailing energy provided by Will-to-Live.

But Will-to-Live is crucial in yet another way, for in it we find the
connection or link between civilization and ethics. The intellectual steps that
establish this connection are not in the least esoteric or mystical but plain,
simple, and direct: civilization (as we have seen) depends on optimism for its
continued vitality, growth, and wellbeing; it is therefore imperative, if we wish
to promote and advance civilization, that we maintain and nourish our optimism
to the highest possible degree; the only reliable source of the optimism we need
is (as we have also seen) Will-to-Live; therefore, maintaining and nourishing
our optimism to the highest possible degree means, in effect, maintaining and
nourishing Will-to-Live to exactly that same extent; this leads naturally to our
reverencing this Will-to-Live by enhancing and strengthening it at every
opportunity, to, as Schweitzer wrote, "show[ing] to all will-to-live the same
reverence as I do to my own. There we have given us the basic principle of the
moral which is a necessity of thought. It is good to maintain and to encourage
life; it is bad to destroy life or to obstruct it."15 The weld is seamless, the
conclusion inescapable.

Anyone but Schweitzer would probably have stopped right there and been
satisfied at having arrived at such a far-reaching philosophical insight. But once
again his tremendous capacity and passion for precise detail asserted itself. He
went on, in fact, to work out the specifics implicit in this conclusion and in so
doing mapped out, apparently for the first time, the entire "genome" of a
complete ethics. In carrying out this enterprise his approach was, as usual,
relentlessly systematic, as will become clear to anyone who takes the trouble to
follow his train of thought.

Reverence and The Emerging Global Civilization

In this section I will offer a sort of Ariadne's thread through what for many
may have been a labyrinth, and in so doing will, I hope, make clearer the
tremendous scope and significance of Schweitzer's accomplishment.

Let us begin, as Schweitzer did, with our own Will-to-Live and what it
means to reverence it. This involves, first of all, the whole person—spirit, mind,
and body. These correspond to the three methods of philosophy—Intuitionism,
Rationalism and Empiricism—that the well-known scholar Edwin A. Burtt
described in his 1965 book In Search of Philosophic Understanding (still, in my
view, one of the best introductions to the whole field). Burtt showed that starting
from Intuitionism (the earliest method, realm of religion and revelation), each
successive method provided the means of resolving the contradictions arising
from the one preceding it. Intuitions and mysterious revelations often yield
conflicting or inconsistent perceptions (as illustrated, for example, by the wide
discrepancies between the Bible and the Koran).

Rationalism, the use of reason, helps to resolve many of these; but equally
rationalistic systems can also display dramatic disagreements in their logical
conclusions, a useful case in point being the conflicts between Euclidean
geometry and its various non-Euclidean forms. When rationalist systems lead to
conflicting conclusions, the Empiricist method, which resolves them by appeal
to physical evidence, is very useful. But even empiricism has its limits, as Burtt
astutely shows by posing the question "Can a miracle be a fact?" This leads us
back to issues and concerns associated with Intuitionism and ultimately to
philosophical questions about the presuppositions we bring to the discussion.
The three methods, in fact, are neither separable nor adversarial, as extensive
later work by the American philosopher Ken Wilber has made clear.16 Each one
complements the others and offers strengths that compensate for their respective
inherent weaknesses.

Decades before Burtt or Wilber had arrived on the scene, however, Albert
Schweitzer had built all of these three into his ethical philosophy, calling them
"Mysticism," "Thought" and "Knowledge," respectively. The knee-jerk
negative reaction on the part of many commentators to the mention of anything
mystical in a philosophical treatise has made them unable to perceive that
Schweitzer is merely using alternate names for the same idea. This has led in
turn to no end of unnecessary confusion and meaningless attacks. Western
philosophy has been biased since the time of Descartes toward Rationalism and
Empiricism. But Schweitzer was a professional expert in each of the three
methods, with doctoral degrees
in Theology (Intuitionism), Philosophy
(Rationalism) and Medicine (Empiricism). He was therefore in a position to
question boldly the long-standing assumption that any serious consideration of
the mystical and intuitional was off-limits.

Chapter Six

"Why assume that the road to thought must suddenly stop at the frontier of
mysticism?" he asks pointedly in chapter five of The Philosophy of
Civilization.17 Then in chapter eight he shows by reference to Indian ethics how
flimsy the erecting of a "dividing wall" between philosophical (i.e., Rationalist)
ethics and religious (i.e., Intuitionist) ethics really is, and goes on summarize in
one powerfully compact sentence how the three methods of philosophy all
complement each other in arriving at a comprehensive view of ethics:

The true basic principle of the ethical must be not only something universally
valid [Rationalism], but something absolutely elementary and inward, which,
once it has dawned upon a man, never relinquishes its hold, which as a matter of
course runs like a thread through all his meditation [Intuitionism], which never
lets itself be thrust aside, and which continually challenges him to try conclusions
with reality. [Empiricism]18

If any piece of this, any of the three methods, is missing, we have at once an
incomplete ethics which ignores or short-changes some aspect of our Will-to-
Live. And this further leads directly, as Schweitzer said, to "differing and
mutually contradictory opinions about fragments of the basic principle. The
contradiction lies in their incompleteness."19 Even the most cursory survey of
the confusing ethical wasteland that surrounds us on every side today will
quickly confirm the truth of this assertion.

But more is yet to come. Schweitzer characteristically pushed on even
further and identified (in chapter twenty-four) three additional components that
are also part and parcel of a complete ethics:

In ethics are included the ethics of passive self-perfecting, effected by inward
self-liberation from the world (resignation); the ethics of active self-perfecting,
effected by means of the mutual relations between man and man; and the ethics
of ethical society. Ethics thus form an extensive gamut of notes….In what, then,
does a complete code of ethics consist? In ethics of passive self-perfecting,
combined with ethics of active self-perfecting. The ethics established from the
standpoint of society are supplementary and have to be corrected by the ethics of
active self-perfecting.20

Ethics involves, in other words, attention to self-care and the condition of
one's own inner being, concern for the world and service to others, and a
healthy skepticism about the values our society presents us with, which always
need to be weighed and evaluated based on the degree to which they help and
promote life.

The "extensive gamut of notes" that makes up a complete ethics, then,
consists of six specific "tones" or elements: Passive and Active Self-Perfecting,
Social Ethics, and the three methods of philosophy—Intuitionism, Rationalism

Reverence and The Emerging Global Civilization

and Empiricism. Only Reverence for Life, Schweitzer showed, contains all of
these in their right relation. Any ethical system other than Reverence for Life is
partial, merely a fragment of the whole and is doomed to be merely arbitrary.
Eastern ethics, for example, is partial in that it tends to be intuitionistic and
inward-directed (i.e., focused on Passive Self-Perfecting) with comparatively
little concern for the welfare of society as a whole. Western ethics is partial in a
different way, tending toward the rational and empirical and toward an
outwardly-directed utilitarianism (in other words, toward Active Self-Perfecting
and Social Ethics). But Reverence for Life is like a beam of white light which
contains and unites within itself all the colors of the ethical spectrum, and apart
from which no ethics can exist.21 And this leads to one of the most important yet
hitherto unnoticed features of his ethical philosophy.

In an article which appeared in the July/August, 2000 issue of Natural
History magazine ("The Narthex of San Marco and the Pangenetic Paradigm"),
Stephen Jay Gould suggested a remarkable alternative reading of the familiar
creation story at the beginning of the Book of Genesis. We are accustomed, he
says, to reading this as an account of how the universe came into being by a
series of gradual additions, in much the same way as a painter begins with a
blank canvas and builds up the finished work brush stroke by brush stroke. But
the mosaics on the ceiling of Venice's San Marco do not portray the process in
this way. Rather, as Gould puts it,

These mosaics, at least for the first three days, tell a story about the successive
separation and precipitation of concrete items from an initial inchoate mass that
must have contained, right from the beginning, all the seeds or prototypes for
later realizations—in other words, a tale of progressive differentiation from
unformed potential rather than successive addition, piece by novel piece, in a
sequence of increasing excellence.22

This is exactly how Schweitzer's ethics relates to other ethical systems.
Reverence for Life is the "inchoate mass" that contains "all the seeds or
prototypes for later [ethical] realizations."

We can quickly and easily see this if we look at Reverence for Life from the
perspectives of the various methods of philosophy we have described earlier.
The Rationalist method, as espoused by mainline academic and utilitarian
ethicists such as Peter Singer and James Rachels, for example, emphasizes in
general the twin themes of equal or impartial consideration of interests and the
value or quality of life. Though slightly more specific in some ways than
Schweitzer's terminology, these themes are an easy and natural outgrowth of
Reverence for Life with its emphasis on showing all Will-to-Live the same
reverence as one does to one's own (equal/impartial consideration of interests)
and on regarding all life as sacred (the value or quality of life). These two

Chapter Six

Rationalist themes, in other words, can be described as a "first differentiation"
from the original "inchoate mass" of Reverence for Life and are deeply implicit
in it.

The Intuitionist method, as exemplified, for example, by His Holiness the
Dalai Lama's Ethics for the New Millennium (1999) and Leonard Swidler's
carefully-edited collection For All Life (2002), provides yet more details—a
second differentiation—about each of the major themes emphasized by
Rationalism. On the "equal consideration of interests" side, for example, we
find specifics about economic justice, equal partnership between men and
women, and care for plants and animals. On the "value or quality of life" side
we find reference to peace, nonviolence, and respect for life, as well as on
wisdom and a change of heart or transformation of consciousness. 23 Every one
of these is also already embedded in Schweitzer's ethics, and simply deepens or
elaborates on what he had already put in place.

The Empiricist method, finally, as can be seen in the work of Daniel Dennett
(Darwin's Dangerous Idea, 1995) and E. O. Wilson (Biophilia, 1984 and
Consilience, 1998), takes us once again back to the center or departure point. A
particularly striking example of this is found in Wilson's remark in Biophilia
that "the more the mind is fathomed in its own right as an organ of survival, the
greater will be the reverence for life for purely rational reasons."24 As Wilson
makes no reference to Schweitzer either in his text or in his bibliography, he
appears to have arrived independently (and decades later) at exactly the same
conclusion Schweitzer did, though by a far different route. And a few sentences
later, we find this parallel made even more dramatic in Wilson's discussion of
the dance of "expansion" and "stewardship" that together makes up what he
calls "the conservation ethic." These two dancing partners, however, turn out to
be none other than Passive and Active Self-Perfecting, respectively, under
different names.

Further replications of Schweitzer's Reverence for Life appear wherever we
look in ethics. The more feminist ethics of care, for example, as developed by
Nell Noddings (Caring, 1984), Joan Tronto (Moral Boundaries, 1993), and
others is based on relatedness, caregiving, and responsibility (rather than on the
more "masculine" orientation toward principle and obligation). These connect
directly with Schweitzer's fierce emphasis on these very components in his own
ethical thinking and universalize that thinking even further by illustrating how it
transcends limitations of gender by balancing and harmonizing both masculine
and feminine energies and values. Reverence for Life also embodies a textbook
example of a sustainable worldview,25 which means that it will continue to be a
reliable base for ethics even if our planet experiences a wrenching shift to
conditions of scarcity at some time in the near or distant future.

Reverence and The Emerging Global Civilization

Even this short survey makes clear beyond doubt that Schweitzer's ethics of
Reverence for Life is not simply one more ethical opinion or theory among
many that one is free to accept or reject. Rather it is the single ethical idea that
contains all other ethical ideas just as a seed or fertilized egg contains all the
components of a complete organism. No ethical theory can exist apart from it,
and all bear an organic relation to it. Even more astoundingly, it turns out that
the six components in the "extensive gamut of notes" that make up the complete
ethics of Reverence for Life dovetail precisely, like hand in glove, with the
components of a complete civilization as we sketched them earlier in this essay.
For example, the fact that civilization is not static but dynamic and involves
both material and spiritual progress connects directly with Passive and Active
Self-Perfecting as well as with the three methods of philosophy, where the
material, spiritual, and rational coexist on an equal footing in a delicate and
highly productive relationship. The modernization that may be the material basis
for a new universal civilization is linked to Social Ethics. All this is captured or
summarized well in Schweitzer's closing words on civilization in his last
chapter where he writes:

Three kinds of progress come within the purview of civilization: progress in
knowledge and power; progress in the social organization of mankind; progress
in spirituality. Civilization is made up of four ideals: the ideal of the individual;
the ideal of social and political organization; the ideal of spiritual and religious
social organization; the ideal of humanity as a whole. On the basis of these four
ideals thought tries conclusions with progress.26

Here, the three kinds of progress connect explicitly with the three methods
of philosophy, progress in knowledge, and power involving Empiricism,
progress in social organization involving Rationalism, and progress in
spirituality involving Intuitionism. And the four ideals connect explicitly with
our second triad, the ideal of the individual involving Passive Self-Perfecting,
the ideal of humanity as a whole involving Active Self-Perfecting with its
service to others, and the ideals of political and religious organization involving
Social Ethics. Every part fits. Every part has its place and purpose. No part can
be omitted without detriment to the whole. No essential element is missing.

It is for all the reasons considered here that Schweitzer's philosophy of
civilization and his comprehensive ethics of Reverence for Life deserve to be
regarded as one of the greatest achievements in the history of human thought.
But is all this of any more than theoretical significance? Does it have any
practical implications for us in the early years of the 21st century? It is here that
we forge a link with the theme of this conference, "Albert Schweitzer's
Relevance Today," for what we have seen concerning Reverence for Life and its

Chapter Six

intimate connection with civilization speaks powerfully to our present historical
and planetary situation.

Possibly the most direct route to establishing this point is via Thomas
Friedman's book The Lexus and the Olive Tree: Understanding Globalization
(2000), which contains a wealth of information and insights about this
unprecedented process that is unfolding even as I write these words. Several of
Friedman's observations are germane to the issue of Schweitzer's continuing
relevance in today's world. He notes, for example, that in order to understand
globalization accurately, we must be capable of what he calls "information
arbitrage," i.e., drawing on "a wide net of informants and information and then
knowing how to synthesize it in a way that will produce a profit."27 He goes on
to emphasize that "the traditional boundaries between politics, culture,
technology, finance, national security, and ecology are disappearing. You often
cannot explain one without referring to the others, and you cannot explain the
whole without reference to them all."28 Schweitzer's "organic" multifaceted way
of thinking about ethics and civilization fits this description exactly. It models
for us the kind of broad-based integrative approach that is essential for our age.
And its holistic structure suggests that, unlike less detailed and comprehensive
treatments of the subject, it might offer a degree of positive complexity that is
fully congruent with the complexities we face.

A second key observation in Friedman's book is that emerging global
system is too vast and too complicated for any one individual or group of
individuals (such as the U.N., the W.T.O. or the World Bank) to run in the
traditional top-down style historically characteristic of national governments.
This means that the new system will of necessity have to be regulated from the
bottom up by ordinary people and numerous small institutions networking
together, in other words (to use Friedman's crucial distinction) by governance
rather than by government. He gives examples of this idea in action, from
Kathie Lee Gifford's Fair Labor Association (1999) to a small group of
environmental activists who shaped new fishing-industry behavior by a
grassroots "Dolphin Safe" campaign to the Grameen Bank that makes microloans to impoverished women in developing countries. The success of such
"governance" initiatives depends in part, of course, on the "democratizations"
that Friedman describes early in his book—of technology, of finance, and of
information.29

But it also depends even more fundamentally on precisely the individual and
his or her own sense of responsibility that Schweitzer made the heart and soul of
his ethics of Reverence for Life. In a famous passage from The Philosophy of
Civilization he compares the greening of civilization to the greening of the fields
in the spring, which is brought about by "the millions of young shoots which
sprout up freshly from the old roots. In like manner, the revival of thought

Reverence and The Emerging Global Civilization

which is essential for our time can only come through a transformation of the
opinions and ideals of the many brought about by individual and universal
reflection about the meaning of life and of the world."30 Without this "new
attitude of mind,"31 civilization, whether global or not, will never revive or
flourish. We will continue to live in a world full of the horrific corporate and
governmental excesses described by people like David Korten, John Perkins and
Naomi Klein, a world where the rich get richer, the poor are further crushed by
poverty, war and disease, and the environment continues to be plundered for the
raw materials to feed an out-of-control and ultimately unsustainable consumerist
economy. Here again we see the tremendous relevance of Schweitzer's farreaching thought to the problems before us.

This leads to an important final point. Near the end of his book, Friedman
offers a "matrix" (one of several) to describe the essential components of a
humane global system. As he describes them, these are "a politics of sustainable
globalization, a geopolitics of sustainable globalization (a foreign and defense
policy), a geo-economics of sustainable globalization, and, finally, an ethics of
sustainable globalization (a way of thinking about where values, parenting and
God fit into this system). In other words, it means articulating a new vision for a
new international system."32 The first three of these are widely discussed and
debated in the media and elsewhere; but the fourth, the one concerning ethics,
seems to receive little more than platitudes or self-righteous thunderings. This
may be due in part to the general perception of ethics as something arcane and
abstract (a perception encouraged by many academic treatments of the subject).
It may be due in part to the widespread idea that "it's all relative," that no real
possibility exists of ever arriving at an authentic cross-cultural planet-wide
consensus on ethical values. As long as either or both of these perceptions
remain current, we will never be able to possess the "moral confidence," as
Andrew Sabl has called it, not just to disapprove of the evils we see around us,
but actively to engage our time and energy to oppose and neutralize them. 33 In
other words, it is precisely this moral confidence which must undergird any type
of grassroots governance. If it is present, it will humanize and civilize
globalization. If it is absent, we will find ourselves continuing toward a
planetary future hellish beyond comprehension.

If our conclusions about Schweitzer's ethics of Reverence for Life are
correct, that means we have already in hand the complete and universal values
base on which we can confidently build a truly humane new global order. We
need not seek it or construct it or endlessly debate it. It has been given to us and
is inescapably present in every ethical system the world over, just as the same
nutritional elements are present in every one of the planet's disparate cuisines.
Even further, Schweitzer's precise characterization of the way in which this
complete ethics interlocks exactly with the requirements for a complete

Chapter Six

civilization provides us also with the template or pattern for a truly civil society,
one that has shifted from being centered on a love of money to being centered
on a love of life itself. David Korten, near the end of his book When
Corporations Rule the World, writes that making this fundamental shift in
values is what "defines the great work of our time."34

If this is true, nothing could be more relevant today than the monumental

philosophy that Albert Schweitzer bequeathed to us.

Schweitzer's beloved Goethe included in Faust a couplet which may serve

us as a fitting conclusion:

Was glänzt, ist für den Augenblick geboren,
Das Echte bleibt der Nachwelt unverloren.
[What glitters is born for the moment;
The genuine endures from age to age.]

A century later, this sentiment was echoed by Schweitzer himself in The
Philosophy of Civilization, where he wrote "Nicht auf das, was geistreich,
sondern auf das, was wahr ist, kommt es an." ["We are not concerned with what
is ingenious, but with what is true."]35 The ethics of Reverence for Life is
neither glittery, merely clever, nor ephemeral. It is the genuine article, the truth
which will last down the ages and on which so much now depends.
