CHAPTER SEVEN

SCHWEITZER, TEILHARD, AND THE HUMAN
FUTURE

BENJAMIN B. PAGE

It may seem somewhat odd at first glance to discuss Albert Schweitzer and
Pierre Teilhard de Chardin in the same paper, under the same title.1 One a
world-famous missionary doctor, as well as a theologian, philosopher, musician,
and educator, with several books to his credit and a Nobel Peace Prize in
recognition of his work; the other a rather unusual combination of Jesuit priest
and paleontologist who spent much of his life studying the earliest origins of
life, whose books lay unpublished—banned by the church he loved—and who
was known only to a few close colleagues at the time he died.

However, there are interesting convergences, to use a Teilhardian term—
between these two figures. The thinking of both, although ordained clergymen,
went well beyond what was typical of their respective churches, for which each
suffered misunderstanding, even controversy; both were committed to reflective
thought, to reason and science, whether in application or research; each spent
much of his life and work far from the Europe of his formative years, the one by
choice in equatorial Africa, the other more or less in exile in China. Most
importantly for present purposes, each was profoundly disturbed by the state of
European civilization and the future of humankind. Each became a prophetic
voice crying out the need for radical change in human consciousness if, for
Schweitzer, civilization was to be "restored,"2 for Teilhard if we were to "build
the earth," the only alternative to our perishing.3

The story of how Schweitzer hit upon the expression Reverence for Life—
or, rather, it hit him—while travelling up the Ogowe River in Gabon, is well
known. It is important, however, to recall the context in which this happened.
Since his earliest university days, he wrote, he had been concerned over what he
saw as the declining state of "civilization"—at the time (turn of the nineteenth
century) the adjective "European" would have been considered tautological. At
first he had planned a book on this theme and what he saw as its dire likely
consequences. The First World War seemed a confirmation of all Schweitzer

Schweitzer, Teilhard, and the Human Future

had feared, and made such a book unnecessary. He refocused. He would write
something "constructive . . . a work dealing with the restoration of civilization."
He now saw the problem of European civilization as stemming from "the tragedy
[that] . . . the original bonds uniting an affirmative attitude toward the world
(weltanschauung) with ethics [were] . . . loosening and finally breaking apart...
European humanity is being guided by a progress that is merely external and has
lost its bearings, [thanks to the fact that its understanding of] the ethical was not
really founded on thought"—i.e. on principles arrived at by reason. What was
needed was a vision of a civilization built not on the mere accretion of science and
power, but rather one that "cares most of all for the spiritual and ethical
development of the individual and of humankind."4 Schweitzer wrote of having
spent many long hours and sheets of scribbled notes trying to arrive at a concept
that would effect the union of ethics with metaphysics, with a life-affirming
world-view, when he chanced to see the hippopotamuses in the river, closer to
his canoe than was comfortable.5

Schweitzer considered the philosophy of Reverence for Life his greatest
contribution—"the ethics of love raised to the level of universality. . . .
Reverence and compassion for all that is called life; the connecting link [discovered
by reason], between love for God and love for humankind: love for all creatures,
reverence for all being, a compassionate sharing of experience with all of life, no
matter how externally different from our own."6 But, for Schweitzer, Reverence for
Life meant more. The book that eventually emerged from his quest for a way to
restore Europe, Philosophy of Civilization (1923) makes clear what following—
living—the ethic asks of us.7

The "ordinary ethics" of Europe, Schweitzer contends, had become a confusion
of abstraction, compromise and relativism, in large part because they start from
principles such as Descartes' famous cogito, which mistakenly take something
other than awareness of one's own experience of the will to life as basic, or, like
Kant's and most other Western moral philosophy, limits its concern to human life.
Schweitzer's concern is with all of life: "The ethics of reverence for life makes
only the promotion and maintenance of life to rank as good. All destruction and
injury to life, under whatever circumstances they take place it condemns as evil." A
person is "truly ethical" only when he or she "obeys the compulsion to help all life
which he is able to assist and shrinks from injuring anything that lives." This
imperative holds, regardless of such secondary considerations as effect given the
larger picture of all of the destruction of life by life that occurs in nature, whether or
to what degree any particular life is of use, benefit or value to us or society, or
whether a life in any way "deserves" our sympathy; regardless, too, of what public
opinion might think of our actions on behalf of life.8 Schweitzer is explicit in his
contention that no form or level of life is in any way superior to any other; all,
including that of the human species or of any individual human being, are equally

Chapter Seven

valuable and needed instances of life, of the universal will to life. For the truly
ethical person, "Life as such is sacred."9 Ethics, for Schweitzer, consists in
"responsibility without limit toward all that lives; . . . in my experiencing the
compulsion to show to all will-to-live the same reverence as I do [to] my own."10 It
is almost as though Schweitzer has reworked one of the forms of Kant's categorical
imperative: every instance of life, whether in yourself or another, must always be
treated as an end in itself and never as a means only.

And yet nature presents the "ghastly drama" of life divided against itself:
life, to live must consume other life. While this "puzzling and horrible law" may
be a necessity of life, the fact that it is necessary in no way diminishes the evil
of it. Nor does its necessity absolve thinking beings—us—from "the guilt we
incur" when we kill, even if to eat or to feed our young.11 Of us, however—of
life that has become conscious of itself—Reverence for Life demands even
more: we are constantly confronted with the question of to what extent can I
ethically devote myself and whatever I own to my own self-maintenance—or, by
extension, to that of my family, my country or my religion? It requires each of us to
decide "how much of my life, my possessions, my rights, my happiness, my time,
and my rest I must devote to others and how much I may keep for myself . . .
Whatever more than others you have received in health, natural gifts, working
capacity, success, a beautiful childhood, harmonious family circumstances, you
must not accept as a matter of course," Schweitzer stressed. "You must pay a price
for them. You must show more than average devotion of life to life."12

The upshot is that the philosophy of Reverence for Life places us, insofar as
we are a conscious, thinking form of life, in a situation where, in everything we
do we must decide between, on the one hand, taking upon ourselves the guilt we
incur by acting—even if out of necessity in the maintenance of our own will to
live—in ways that diminish other life, or, on the other hand, remaining ethical
even at the cost of diminishing our own life or well being, our own pleasure,
food supply, health . . . Nor does Reverence for Life provide any rules or
guidelines. Each of us has to decide this on his or her own, in each situation in
which we find ourselves. No one can tell us what we must do, nor has anyone
any ground on which to judge our choice or condemn our action. Thus
Schweitzer's quest for a philosophy capable of restoring civilization in the end
places us in a situation not unlike that in which we are placed by the
existentialist ethics of his cousin Jean-Paul Sartre13—with the all-important
"addendum" that none of us is alone in this universe: we are part of something
that goes well beyond us whether as individuals or as a species; part, as it were,
of life "life-ing."

While Schweitzer was concerned with life, Reverence for Life, and the
restoration of civilization, Teilhard focused on matter, consciousness, and the
future of evolution. Writing initially from the front lines during the First World

Schweitzer, Teilhard, and the Human Future

War and then in the years between it and the Second, Teilhard had come to
believe that in and through us—humankind—and our science, invention, and
technology, evolution had reached a crucial point of decision. We can affirm a
faith in the future, i.e. decide to push evolution—consciousness—forward
toward unity, unity initially in "building the earth;" ultimately in "the systematic
arrangement and exploration of the universe understood as the true country of
mankind." Or—what Teilhard considered "the most besetting temptation of our
time"14—we can continue to do nothing, to keep on consuming—wasting—
earth and its energies and resources in "useless wars," "perverse refinements,"
and "a restless search for pleasures, especially those of a material character."
"The time is close at hand," Teilhard believed, "when mankind will see that
precisely in virtue of its position in a cosmic evolution which it has become
capable of discovering, and criticizing, it now stands biologically between the
alternatives of suicide and worship."15

An adequate explication of Teilhard's thinking would require more time and
space than is available here. Suffice it to note that just as Schweitzer had found
it necessary to critique "ordinary ethics," Teilhard had to do the same with
regard to "ordinary science." In the methods of reduction and analysis typical of
most scientific research, the emergence of life and of consciousness from
inorganic matter become enigmatic at best, explicable only as random—hence
meaningless—chance…or the result of divine intervention. Instead, Teilhard
started from the basic fact of the existence of life and of consciousness, and set
out to discover the conditions necessary to bring them about.16 His conclusions:
consciousness, thought, or spirit, at least in rudimentary or potential form,
pervades—is coextensive with—the universe.17 It has been present in all things
including in matter—the raw material of Creation, of life—at least since the
beginning of space-time. Evolution is thus not random; it is closer to the notion
of entelechy, the gradual manifestation of inherent potential. Evolution in other
words has direction, as can be seen by comparing life today with the fossil
remains Teilhard studied as a paleontologist. Evolution is always moving—
"rising" —toward greater and greater complexity, organization and synthesis,
sublimation, convergence and eventual unity. Consciousness, Teilhard stressed
"is born within and as a function of matter."18

Where evolution is happening most profoundly today is at the level of
human consciousness. Despite the continuing appeal of notions of individualism
or nationalism, or of particular religions or philosophies, consciousness,
Teilhard wrote in the 1930s, has already reached the international phase.19
Through our science, the spirit of research, and their practical application in
technology, communications, and virtually every other area of human activity,
we are weaving a delicate membrane of consciousness—Teilhard's
"noosphere"—embracing and drawing into itself the entire planet.20 We are

Chapter Seven

becoming aware of ourselves as what we have always been, if but dimly seen: a
planetary species; aware of ourselves as the shapers of the future of evolution,
our own as well as that of the planet and ultimately of the universe. For
Schweitzer we are life become conscious of itself; for Teilhard we are
evolution—the evolution of the planet, of the universe—become conscious of
itself. Thus, where Schweitzer saw human life as only one of the vast number
of totally equal forms in the great web of life, different from the others only in
that in us life, since conscious, was burdened by the guilt incurred from
participating in the necessary evil of the "ghastly drama," Teilhard saw human
life as primary but differently than this notion is usually understood. We
represent not the pinnacle or goal, but rather the leading edge of evolution, of
consciousness; the tip of the arrow flying in the direction all else in creation is
also moving. "The human phenomenon is the supremely characteristic form of
the cosmic phenomenon."21

Like Schweitzer's, Teilhard's vision of the place of humankind in the larger
picture imposes on us the burden of conscious choice. However, where
Schweitzer's Reverence for Life places each of us, individually, in the nearly
perpetual agony of ethical decision-making, the decision Teilhard envisages
must be made by humankind acting consciously as a whole, as a species.
Moreover, insofar as we are the leading edge of consciousness, our decisions are
also "on behalf of" all of creation. The issue is far too consequential to be left in
the hands of personal, national, economic, or even religious interests. "The
human mass [must] gather itself together as one [and decide] once and for all
[whether it is] really worth our while to yield--even [as would be necessary] to
devote ourselves passionately—to the forging ahead of the world."22

However far-fetched this may sound, Teilhard finds reason for hope. On the
one hand, he suggests, "the mass of mankind" is "dissatisfied" with the world as
it is: "Everyone wants something larger, finer, better for mankind." On the other
hand, he finds that "scattered throughout the apparently hostile masses" there
are," elements that are only waiting for a shock in order to reorient themselves
and unite." What is needed is "an appeal" that "responds to their inner needs,"
much as the notions of freedom, democracy, and the rights of man did some 250
years ago, something that would propel them—us—toward a "real," if at first
only "relative unanimity . . . to the extent to which all the world is finally at one
in recognizing that the function of man is to build and direct the whole of
earth."23 The more he looked at the world as a scientist, Teilhard insisted, the
less he could see "any possible biological result" other than its "active and
conscious unity," the only fully natural and real form of which is the gradually
dawning "Sense" or "Spirit of Earth, the passionate concern for our common
destiny, which draws the thinking part of life ever forward."24 He concludes:

Schweitzer, Teilhard, and the Human Future

The resources we enjoy today, the powers and secrets of science we have
discovered, cannot be absorbed by the narrow system of individual and national
divisions which have so far served the leaders of the world. The age of nations is
past. [It is in this sense that the] task before us now, if we would not perish, is to
shake off our ancient prejudices, and to build the earth.25

Thus far, nothing has been mentioned of God, Jesus, or religion in this
presentation of the visions of Schweitzer and Teilhard, each of whom, after all,
was not only a theologian, but also an ordained minister, the one in the Lutheran
Church, the other in the Roman Catholic In the case of Schweitzer this is not
inappropriate; for him religion is largely a personal matter. Schweitzer rarely wrote
of God. He rejected the notion of a God who rules or even watches over us or
creation. What is important in Christianity is "the spirit of Jesus," not religious
dogma or stories—the spirit of love, expressed primarily by unlimited service to
life or to human need. Jesus, Schweitzer wrote at the end of his book on him,
comes "unknown and nameless" to those who respond to his call to take up "the
tasks He has to carry out in our age. To them He will reveal Himself in the
fellowship of peace and activity, of struggle and suffering, till they come to know
as an inexpressible secret, who He is."26 The whole of his own life, Schweitzer
insisted, was a response to this call; he went to Africa in order to live rather than
talk about what answering it meant. It would apparently be in and through
individuals' personal beliefs and acts. However, if God or religion is to play a role
in Schweitzer's project for "restoring Civilization, in other words, could be restored
through personal appreciation of a universal philosophy that brings together ethics
and a positive, life-affirming world-view that did not depend on a supreme being or
higher power, and that had been arrived at not by revelation or theological teaching,
but by rational thought.

For Teilhard, too, religion was intensely meaningful in his personal
consciousness. In his case, however, it was a Catholic understanding of God and of
Jesus, more than a personal call to service, that was primary. As the present paper
suggests, it is possible to present Teilhard's thinking and vision without specific
reference to religion; he himself insisted that his major work be read "solely and
exclusively as a scientific study."27 However, while separating the science from his
theology and philosophy can be done with results that may be provocative for
many who are less interested in religion, doing so leaves out what was central to
Teilhard.

For Teilhard, the movement toward human unity inherent in evolution can
avoid becoming distorted by personal, national or even religious self-interests only
if empowered by the vision, the lure, of a yet greater unity, "something tangible for
the possession of which it would be infinitely good to lay down one's life."28 For
Teilhard, in other words, that which religion calls God is an integral element in the
process of evolution, the center toward which everything is converging; evolution's

Chapter Seven

ultimate goal. If we are as yet unable to see this, it is not because God is hiding
himself or testing us, but rather because so far, with but few recorded exceptions,
the consciousness of the bulk of humankind is not yet sufficiently evolved.29
Moreover, for Teilhard, God is not only the Omega of creation; God is also the
Alpha, the beginning. Matter and the universe made of it, are not only God's
creation; they are God, the Word made flesh; Creation as the great Incarnation, in
concentrated form, as it were, in Jesus. The words "This is My Body" spoken at the
moment of transubstantiation refer to the bread immediately present on the altar
and to the universe. The act of transubstantiation is a reminder that the bread and
the universe are what they have always been—the body of a cosmic Christ.30 It is
from this perspective that Teilhard finds no conflict between science and
Christianity, that he can see research as "the highest form of adoration," that he can
suggest that "perhaps, impelled by the necessity to build the unity of the world we
shall end by perceiving that the great object unconsciously pursued by science, is
nothing else than the discovery of God."31 And it is in this sense that in and through
us, evolution now stands between the alternatives of suicide and worship.

There are many points of comparison and contrast in this all too brief sketch.
The more important questions relate to what, if anything, the visions of
Schweitzer and Teilhard might offer to us today. To what extent does
Schweitzer's or Teilhard's diagnosis of, or response to the world of his day offer
insights or questions that might help us in defining and responding the needs of
our world? How necessary is religion or the feeling of relationship to someone
or something that goes beyond our immediate situations and interests, beyond
our own lives? To what extent might Schweitzer's philosophy and ethics of
Reverence for Life become the kind of catalyst Teilhard believes so urgently
needed if we are not to perish? To what extent might it be suggested that the
Internet and today's processes of "globalization" represent a fulfillment of
Teilhard's concept of a "nooshpere" or of the need he saw to move beyond
nations and ethnicities toward human unity? Teilhard in his day saw various
forces contending for "possession of the earth—democracy, communism,
nationalism, and fascism—of each of which he was sharply critical.
Nonetheless, he felt that, while antagonistic towards each other, each also
contained positive constituents, by virtue of which they were "unwittingly
converging toward a common conception of the future," their very conflict
representing "a crisis of birth [rather than] signs of death." To what degree
might this offer a useful perspective from which to evaluate and respond to the
forces, interests, and currents seeking to possess the world today? It is to
questions such as these that I hope this contribution can draw attention and
discussion.

As for my own view, I find a valuable, even necessary complementarity in
the thinking of the two men. Like so many of the Biblical prophets they both

Schweitzer, Teilhard, and the Human Future

somewhat resemble. Schweitzer and Teilhard begin in a note of urgency about
what each saw as the situation facing us as a whole people, a species, and facing
us precisely because of the dimension of consciousness that is so much more
fully developed in us than (yet) in other forms of life or (for Teilhard) matter.
From here, Schweitzer moves into a deep search for individual, personal moral
purpose and life-meaning. Teilhard thrusts us into a future with a vision of a
better world with global—or at least species-wide—meaning, but that calls for
radical change in our collective assumptions and ways of understanding and
acting; he gives fresh depth and passion to the ancient hope, expressed in the
closing words of a hymn that was—but is no longer—in the hymnal of the
church where I first learned it,

Now, even now, once more from Earth to Sky

peals forth in joy Man's old, undaunted cry
Earth shall be fair, and all her people one . . .32

In this Teilhard is, of course, far from alone. Several writers have found
parallels, for example, between Teilhard and another prophet who at first glance
makes an odd pairing—Karl Marx. Both were committed to the concept of
evolution—and hence also to its corollaries. Humankind and human-built
societies, nations, or social and economic systems also evolve, increasingly as a
result of the further evolution of the consciousness we embody and the decisions
and actions we undertake, even if we are as yet not often fully aware of their
larger relationships and consequences. There are other attributes in which
Schweitzer also shares: interest in wholeness and relationships—in the larger
context with a vision that transcends boundaries and disciplines; a personal life
as well as a philosophy founded on a unity of theory and practice; a
commitment to his vision so deep as to bar compromise and preclude the
possibility of academic or—for Schweitzer and Teilhard—ecclesiastical careers
and entail for all three various degrees of aloneness, professional criticism,
ostracism, and even exile.33

Yet there is always a problem in prophetic visions. Emphasizing global
categories like hope, unity, the future, or all of humankind risks losing sight of
the immediate, the present, the individual. It can provide fertile ground for the
emergence of elites claiming special knowledge of what that future should be
and special competence for bringing it about; it can encourage an ethos of
sacrifice at least by some, now—voluntary or otherwise—in the name of the
posited better future for all. The more noble or inspiring the goal, the greater the
likelihood of such concomitants.34 Here Schweitzer's vision can offer a vitally
needed balance: his emphases on Life, on the needs and conditions of life here
and now, on the moral dimensions and demands of every concrete decision in
every specific moment, and on direct personal responsibility especially for

Chapter Seven

those decisions or actions that damage the life-prospects of others—plant,
animal, or human. Overall, however, I find Schweitzer's view to be rather static
in contrast to Teilhard's forward thrusting dynamism, his focus too much on the
immediate. I find more appealing the all-embracing aspects of Teilhard's
vision35 and the prospect of real collaboration—community—with others, rather
than individual commitment worked out on one's own. But this is a question not
of exclusion but of proportion. Both visions are needed—but if I had to, I would
say that my commitment would be to Reverence for Life—in the context of the
further evolution of The Cosmic Phenomenon.

CHAPTER EIGHT

"A BOUNDLESS ETHICS": REVERENCE FOR LIFE
VERSUS DIVINE DOMINION

TIMOTHY T. DANSDILL

The Necessity of (Re)Thinking Reverence

No edited collection of essays devoted to Reverence for Life—the central
legacy of Albert Schweitzer's life and thought—has appeared since 2002, which
saw the release of Reverence for Life: The Ethics of Albert Schweitzer for the
Twenty-First Century, edited by Marvin Meyer and Kurt Bergel. That volume
grew out of a conference held at Chapman University in Orange, California, in
1999, titled "Albert Schweitzer at the turn of the Millennium," and was brought
to fruition and release after the events of September 11, 2001. Referring back to
those events in the preface, Meyer, Director of the Schweitzer Institute at
Chapman, writes: "If ever there was a time for reflection upon Albert
Schweitzer and reverence for life, it is now" (ix).

David Ives, Director of Quinnipiac University's Schweitzer Institute,
determined in 2004 that by honoring Jane Goodall's work to understand and
preserve the ever-more threatened lives and habitat of our closest primate
relatives, chimpanzees, we could also, in effect, reference and "reverence"
Schweitzer's life and legacy. Quinnipiac's 2005 Conference, "Reverence for
Life Revisited: Albert Schweitzer's Relevance Today," and now this edited
collection, have recapitulated, with important variations, the motivation and
matter of the Chapman conference and its edited collection. The legacy of
Schweitzer's ethic of Reverence for Life is one that demands continuous
reflection and re-description, as we are now faced with an apparently permanent
"global war on terrorism." The entire world continues to reel, paralyzed by
outbreaks of genocide across the European and African continents, flummoxed
by massive stockpiles of nuclear weapons, indifferent to ever-mounting
evidence of global climate change and massive ecological destruction.

'A Boundless Ethic'

In the opening of The Decay and Restoration of Civilization, Schweitzer
announced: "It is clear now to everyone that the suicide of civilization is in
progress" (3). Writing in the 1920s, after the devastation of World War I,
followed by the Great Flu Epidemic, Schweitzer had reason to be pessimistic
about humanity's prospects, of course, and he lived long enough to see even
greater death and destruction: from World War II, through the ever-looming
threat of nuclear weapons, and the Korean conflict. He died as the Vietnam
conflict began to expand. He continued to argue that our collective drive toward
suicide stems from "an absence of thinking which is characterized by a
contempt for life." For Schweitzer, the practice of a new form of individual,
subjective "reflection" would not only provide the kind of thinking that would
prevent "contempt for life," but once perfected, it would found and foster his
notion of "Ethics: . . . the activity of man directed to secure the inner perfection
of his own personality" (94). From this inter-subjective achievement of
perfection through reflection, civilization would enjoy an ethic of Reverence for
Life.

Schweitzer's deepest scholars and newly minted followers alike will
reproduce magnificent passages in which he presents the enduring dualism of
material and spiritual life, of animalism and rationalism, of the philosophical
and religious fission-like release in holding together an optimistic and
pessimistic world view with a new ethical law of binding energy—reverence.
The more careful among them will then remind us that Schweitzer does not fuse
these dualisms as much as he boldly (and quite enigmatically) confuses them
with a call for a new kind of non-rational rationality, a subjective state of
enthusiasm that is at the same time a rigorous act of intellectual "reflection." We
are tirelessly reminded of what he himself argued endlessly for: that this
contemplative practice, which is inherent, yet generally unavailable, is what
makes humans a special species, and it will drive out contempt for life based on
ignorance, in favor of Reverence for Life, based on "rational reflection."

Let's allow Schweitzer to summarize his project. I have taken two passages
from what is arguably his most important attempt to codify the metaphysical,
material, and moral grounds for the ethic of Reverence for Life, Civilization and
Ethics. The first is from the Introduction. I will reproduce the second later in my
discussion below.

Every conviction which possesses real value is non-rational and enthusiastic in
character, since it cannot be the product of knowledge about the universe, but
arises from the reflective experience of the will-to-live, in virtue of which we
leave behind all mere intellectual knowledge of the world . . . We must all
become religious as the result of reflection. This rational reflection must become
the ruling force in our lives (xviii). . . . [I]t is the disposition to reverence for life
which alone is capable of creating a new concept of law (xix).

Chapter Eight

Throughout
three major works—Indian Thought and Its Development;
Christianity and the Religions of the World; and Civilization and Ethics—
Schweitzer makes it clear that only one model of an "ethical personality" has
ever existed for our securing this inner "religious" perfection that would make
possible authentic reflection, thinking, a new concept of law, and thus
Reverence for Life: Jesus Christ. Buddha is no slouch, of course. He and Christ
"have this in common, that their form of ethics . . . is not an ethic of action but
an ethic of inner perfection," and both are "governed by a principle of love."
But Schweitzer insists that only the ethical personality of Jesus "commands
active love: with the Buddha it does not get so far" (Indian, 113). Now, fairminded rationalists, secular and evolutionary humanists, will pause a long time
after studying Schweitzer's groundwork and implementation plans for his ethic
of the Reverence for Life. Even some very judicious Schweitzer scholars and
proselytizers of his philosophy have concluded that his premises about Christ's
necessity are puzzling, if not disturbing, and that whatever the rightness of his
Christ-based conclusion, his presumption of the human potential for a boundless
ethics through a universal mode of reverential reflection is dubious. Here is
Meyer, writing toward the end of his "Affirming Reverence for Life" on the
necessity of a Christ(ian)-driven Reverence for Life as equal to, or in excess of,
the absence of thinking and of human contempt.

In spite of the best attempts of Schweitzer and [James] Brabazon [Schweitzer
biographer], I still do not think a strong case is made for reverence for life as a
necessity of thought . . . Further, even if necessity of thought is not judged to be a
logical necessity, few thinkers other than committed Schweitzerians buy into the
necessary relationship Schweitzer poses between rational and non-rational
thought, nor do ethicists feel compelled to draw the same conclusion as
Schweitzer. Reverence for life remains a powerful, appealing ethical option, but
it does not appear to be a necessity of thought" (34) [brackets and italics added].

The purpose of this essay is not to dismiss Schweitzer's legacy of reverence, nor
even to argue in the direction that Meyer leads us for pondering that legacy (an
ethics of what he calls "reciprocity"), as we begin a new century that will mark
what many believe to be the final perfection of our "civilized" suicide. My
purpose is to reflect on the much older—truly mythical— moral mandate found
in The Old Testament Book of Genesis, the one that has been trans-codified
through the "ethical personality" of a "God" which is recognized as the "Father"
of law, the "ruling force" in Jewish, Christian, and Muslim lives alike. This
archetypal ethic is familiar even to atheists and evolutionary humanists. I am
referring, of course, to the ethic of Divine Dominion, which can be found in the
Priestly version of Genesis.1

'A Boundless Ethic'

And God made the beast . . . and cattle . . . and everything that creepeth upon the
earth: and God saw that it was good. And God said, Let us make man in our
image … and let them have dominion . . . over all the earth (1:24-26)

This mandate from God—what I am calling the Ethic of Divine Dominion—
was irrational and ir-reverential from the moment of its expression. That is, God
creates all non-human, yet still "divine" life, calls it "good," then puts it all
under the unreflective control of his divine spitting images whose destiny of
domination is driven by guilt, shame and punishment for disobeying his will
after giving them free will. The Ethic of Divine Dominion is, as Steven Best
describes it, not only dysfunctional but dangerous:

The old geocentric and anthropocentric stories are false, limited, dysfunctional,
and dangerous, wholly unsuited for the destructive power of a technologically
to be superseded by
advanced civilization. Homocentric dramas need
cosmological narratives that situate human life in the larger evolution of the
universe (http://www.drstevebest.org/papers/phiecosoc/h-identity.htm).

To his profound credit, Schweitzer was suspicious of humanity's
"optimism," for he had seen too much suffering, death and destruction, even by
1915, when he had his flash of revelation about Reverence for Life. That
occurred while watching, from a safe distance, a hippo mother, (the most
dangerous of animals vis a vis human presence), herd her young on an African
river. Nevertheless, in Civilization and Ethics, in a Chapter titled "The Crisis of
Civilization and its Spiritual Origin," Schweitzer endorses the ethic of Divine
Dominion in order to make his case for the boundless ethics of Reverence for
Life, with its activist reflection on a Christ-like perfection of service and selfsacrifice.

The impulse to strive after progress in all fields and in every direction comes to
man from an optimistic view of the world, which asserts that the world and life
have real value in themselves, and thus carries in itself the necessity of
developing existence, in so far as we can influence it, to its highest degree of
value. . . . This leads to the lordship of the spirit over the forces of nature, to
completion of the religious, social, economic and practical association of men,
and to spiritual perfection of the individual and of society (8) [emphasis added].

If ever indeed there were a time—ever once again!—for reflecting on the
boundless relation between human ignorance, contempt, and Schweitzer's ethic
of reverence that would somehow deliver us from "the lordship of the spirit," it
is now. This essay would have us rationally reflect on the possibility that
Reverence for Life might be beyond human capacity—beyond the bounds, as it

Chapter Eight

were, of an even greater legacy—the ethic of Divine Dominion. The Genesis
passage should now represent to us the actual performance and legacy of that
hominid creature which diverged from an ancestor that also gave rise to the
great apes and which flourished after the great extinction of species, such as
Tyrannosaurus Rex. Their dominion of the earth would have made the corporate
scribes of Genesis tremble before giving human beings that divine mandate, had
they
theology, but of modern
paleontology, instead.2

insight not of primitive

the reflective

The unbounded ethic of dominion that the Old Testament "God" has been
recorded as mandating for his final act of creation —"with its irrepressible
belief in the dynastic rule of a "dominant" species over all nature"— stands in
high, and some would argue, irremediable, tension with the ethic of Reverence
for Life that Schweitzer has elsewhere described as "a boundless ethics which
will include the animals also."3 In both honoring and doubting the ethical
potential of Schweitzer's irresistible Reverence for Life as a boundless ethic, I
am attempting to suggest that his ethics are no more or less problematic than
those which he analyzed and found wanting. Unfortunately, nowhere in his
magisterial overview and critique of the philosophical and religious foundations
of civilization do we find anything like an overt or sustained awareness and
analysis of the ethic of Divine Dominion. Why did Schweitzer overlook the
immovable force of humanity's self-arrogating, expedient ethic of "Divine
Dominion"? Is it possible that he would not dare to fully acknowledge it as
truth, especially given the crushing intellectual conclusion of Genesis in which
God, after destroying the earth and repopulating it under a covenant with Noah,
turns in one of the great statements of resignation of all time (and true to its
word, this Creator-Destroyer goes on to wipe out only selected living things.)

Yahweh said in his heart, "I will not again curse the ground any more for man's
sake, because the imagination of man's heart is evil from his youth; neither will I
ever again strike everything living, as I have done" (8:21).

To top it off, he grants his "evil" creation, made in his own image, yet

another license for dominion, but this time "with a vengeance," as it were:

The fear of you and the dread of you will be on every animal of the earth, and on
every bird of the sky. Everything that the ground teems with, and all the fish of
the sea are delivered into your hand (9:2).

Before I close with a little known correlation between Kant's closing
reflection on the limits of human respect and duty and Schweitzer's very curious
conclusion to Decay and Restoration of Civilization, some reflection is due on
Jane Goodall's work to advance Schweitzer's legacy in the real terms of our

'A Boundless Ethic'

"genetic" communion with—not dominion over—all that creepeth over the
earth. Steven Best reminds us that Goodall's legacy is one equal to, if not
greater than Schwietizer's: "Until Jane Goodall's work, the identity of Homo
sapiens still had some security: only we were Homo faber and Homo loquens;
only we could make tools, use tools, and linguistically interact; only we lived in
behaviorally complex communities." It would be the most supremely sacred of
ironies if Goodall were to receive the Nobel Peace Prize for her work with
chimpanzees as a way to demonstrate the arrogant imbecility and contemptuous
pseudo-divinity of human dominion; that our inferior humanoid others might
teach us something about evolutionary humanism, peace and communion.

First of all, it is not a well-known fact that among Goodall's many honors is
the Albert Schweitzer Medal in 1987 sponsored by The Animal Welfare
Institute from the only veterinarian ever to serve in the U.S. Senate, John
Melcher, who said that Goodall's work is "only matched by its first recipient,
Albert Schweitzer." In her acceptance speech, Goodall related the story of a
formerly captive circus chimpanzee—named "Old Man"—who feared and hated
humans, but who saved a young (hu)man who tended to Old Man and other
chimps in their new habitat. The young man accidentally slipped and fell while
bringing food, and frightened a baby chimpanzee, which brought on the wrath
of its mother and other females. Old Man pulled the females away, enabling the
young man's escape. Goodall asked this question in her acceptance speech:

If a chimpanzee—one who has been abused by humans—can reach across the
species barrier to help a human friend, then surely we, with our greater capacity
for compassion, can reach out to help chimpanzees, to help all the non-human
creatures with whom we share this planet, can't we?

Second, Goodall's determination to save the habitat and core "humanity" of
these creatures (who along with bonobos "make love" more often than any other
animal species), is truly indomitable. One can sense in the title of her most
famous book, In the Shadow of Man, that though chimps were not mentioned
within the direct shadow of Divine Genesis, if the myth of dominion is to
persist, they must have had a seat near the front of Noah's ark. Moreover, one
of Goodall's most outstanding concerns in every keynote address she gives to
open such conferences as ours, at Quinnipiac University, is that the "imbecilic
behavior" of human primates is making it impossible not only for its non-human
primate cousins to survive, except as laboratory slaves and zoo-convict species,
but that this imbecility—read, Schweitzer's sense of our contemptuous
ignorance—is reducing us, as she says, "to yahoos soiling our own nest." And
yet for all of her deeply informed, and therefore righteous, indignation with
human dominance and trespass upon the rest of the natural world, I can't help
but to be struck by the construction of one of her "Four Reasons for Hope"

Chapter Eight

described in her essay in this volume. Goodall believes that human imbecility as
contempt for life can be somehow mitigated by what she calls "the indomitable
human spirit" (16) (Recall that domicile; domain; lordship; even the etymology
of Property, take root in the semantic field of "dominion.")

What I tried to establish in my 2005 Schweitzer conference talk and slide
presentation on "Divine Dominion" is that human indomitability, whether cast
in a biological, technological, or essentially a theological light (as in "spiritual
lordship"), is not a reason for hope at all. The meaning of "indomitable" as a
state of being impossible to defeat or frighten, especially when attached to the
"human project" on any plane, should frighten all but the most optimistic or
vicious. As a species, humans appear to be utterly determined, given our
evolved—or "God-Given" (it matters little)—will, to dominate the earth. This
drive for dominion is not only frightful; it is perhaps the reason for repeatedly
reflecting at length on the legacy and relevance of what Schweitzer has
variously described in Indian Thought and Its Development as "a boundless
ethics," and in "Religion and Modern Civilization" as "the highest rationality."
The possibility that Goodall's notion of humanity's hopeful indomitability
and Schweitzer's boundless ethic of Reverence for Life are finally linked and
fatally limited by what I am calling the very highest rationality of all—"The
Ethic of Divine Dominion"—will not please theists in general, and might
discomfit many followers of Schweitzer who find in his "ethical mysticism" a
truly exemplary recapitulation of an activist Christianity that will aid in the
necessary thinking we will all need to do toward realizing something like the
"cosmological humanism" Best has outlined. As a fully collapsed Catholic,
philosophically trained skeptic, and evolving cosmological humanist, however, I
am neither interested in pleasing nor in outraging my readers who either
consider
themselves followers of "God" or who revere Schweitzer's
"reverence." In reflecting on the possibility that the ethic of Reverence for Life
is pre-dominated, if not completely contaminated, by the "ethical" precedence
of Divine Dominion, I am merely following Schweitzer's
legacy of
philosophical skepticism. For, after all, we must remember that Schweitzer
properly thought of himself as a philosopher who also happened to follow the
teachings of Jesus Christ. Note this passage from a letter to his wife, Helene:

Basically I am a philosopher—but I let myself be caught by him, the greatest, the
most divine of all philosophers, in whom the most sublime thoughts leads back to
the most simple. Because of this obedience he will forgive my heresies (in Meyer
32).

I have always been impressed by Schweitzer's "heretical" commitment to
methodological rationality, as demonstrated in this statement from Christianity
and the Religions of the World. It is why I trust that by putting Schweitzer's

'A Boundless Ethic'

own ethic of reverence to the same test to which he put Christianity—"as
something that stands to reason"—I will not be dismissed as an atheist-cynicnihilist.

From my youth I have held the conviction that all religious truth must in the end
be capable of being grasped as something that stands to reason. I, therefore,
believe that Christianity, in the contest with philosophy and with other religions,
should not ask for exceptional treatment (21).

Perhaps I put too fine a provocative point on my argument, but I am
beginning to wonder if we shouldn't begin to approach Schweitzer's ethic of
Reverence for Life as a mode of reflection and action that, if it does indeed
stand to reason, then we all need to reflect on this necessity of thought: that in
the contest with the philosophy of cosmological humanism, Schweitzer's ethic
of reverence must not only not ask for exceptional treatment, but that it must be
questioned for its unwitting endorsement of the ethos of divine dominion.

Cosmological Humanism and Non-Human Reverence

As we have seen, Schweitzer never intended for his rational re-valuation of
Christianity as an activist ethic of love for all of creation to be mistaken for the
full measure of the "reflection" that would be required of all humans to come
into lasting communion with itself and the world it has thus far dominated with
suicidal consequences for all concerned. Toward that end, in Out of My Life and
Thought, he observes that, "Christianity cannot take the place of thinking, but it
must be founded on it (276). And, in his Memoirs of Childhood and Youth, he
writes:

I was convinced—and I am so still—that the fundamental principles of
Christianity have to be proved true by reasoning, and by no other method.
Reason, I said to myself, is given us that we may bring everything within the
range of its action, even the most exalted ideas of religion (60).

Earlier in that wonderful book, his uncompromising devotion to reason as
both a rational and non-rational method for coming to an ethic of Reverence for
Life, finds its way into his remarkable insight into the ultimate function and
purpose of prayer as a reflection upon our non-human duties and
responsibilities.

It was quite remarkable to me—this was before I began going to school—why in
my evening prayers I should pray for human beings only . . . I used to add
silently a prayer that I had composed myself for all living creatures. It ran thus:

Chapter Eight

"O, heavenly father, protect and bless all things that have breath; guard them all
from evil, and let them sleep in peace" (40).

I will no longer trouble Schweitzer's rationalist ethic of prayer with the
existence of a "God-Father" who mandated that his children not protect and
bless all things, but to instead subdue and dominate them. The fact is, as we
have already partially understood, given his lifelong attachment to philosophical
skepticism and rational reflection, Schweitzer's youthful reverence for all of life
through a more boundless ethics of prayer was destined for synthesis with the
highest achievement of Enlightenment Rationalism. In particular, Schweitzer
was quite influenced by Immanuel Kant's critiques of "pure" and "practical"
reasoning as the theory and practice of respect and duty not only for humanity,
but also for the purpose of reflecting on the insignificance of human moral law
confronted by an unspeakably infinite cosmos that is utterly indifferent to the
human spectacle and its speck of dominion—divine or otherwise. At the end of
his "Critique of Practical Reason," Kant dramatically reduces his exposition to
this great dualism:

Two things fill the mind with ever new and increasing admiration and awe, the
oftener and the more steadily we reflect on them: the starry heavens above and
the moral law within . . . The former view of a countless multitude of worlds
annihilates as it were my importance as an animal creature, which after it has
been for a short time provided with vital power, one knows not how, must again
give back the matter of which it was formed to the planet it inhabits (a mere
speck in the universe). The second, on the contrary, infinitely elevates my worth
as an intelligence by my personality, in which the moral law reveals to me a life
independent of animality and even of the whole sensible world, at least so far as
may be inferred from the destination assigned to my existence by this law, a
destination not restricted to conditions and limits of this life, but reaching into the
infinite.

We see here the lineaments of Schweitzer's ethic of Reverence for Life—the
almost excruciating tension in human consciousness of believing itself both
divinely derived and yet at the same time "a mere speck in the universe." Let us
now savor
tension—what Schweitzer called "the
antagonism"—in his ultimate view of life as an ethic of reverence, when near
the end of Decay and the Restoration of Civilization, he precisely resonates
Kant's reflection:

the same exquisite

Mere reflection about the meaning of life has already value in itself. . . . How
much would already be accomplished towards our salvation from our present
circumstances if only we would all give up three minutes every evening to gazing
into the infinite world of the starry heavens and meditating on it, or if in taking

'A Boundless Ethic'

part in a funeral procession we would reflect on the enigma of life and death,
instead of engaging in thoughtless conversation as we follow behind the coffin!
(102-3)

Between a Kantian and a Schweitzerian approach to the antagonistic doubleconsciousness of the human as a mere biological speck in a cosmological
mystery play that might not, in fact, reverence the human at all, we feel
ourselves not so much under an unbearable antagonism between irrelevance and
dominance, as that we begin to reflect on a non-human destiny. That destiny
privileges humility over indomitability. Schweitzer's genius was to merge
Kant's pure reason as an act of reverence with Christ's perfect sense of selfsacrifice for a salvation larger than humanity's. Not until I accepted the
invitation to write this essay, did I begin to reflect seriously on my own worldview as one who no longer believes in any anthropocentric conception of the
divine, as one who stopped praying to anything years ago. That reflection—still
based largely on my devotion both to Kant and to Schweitzer as important
ethical philosophers—has found some new expression in Steven Best's call for a
"cosmological humanism."

There is a desperate need for a new consciousness, for new cosmopolitan
identities, in the broadest and most literal sense of the term. Human beings must
begin seeing themselves not as citizens of one nation or another, but of the earth,
indeed, of the cosmos itself. Accordingly, human identity can only be properly
perceived in the context of cosmology and new ecological stories.

Best means we need to dispense with the dominion narrative of "Genesis," at
the very least, and recast, at least for "Christian" consumption, Jesus' "Sermon
on the Mount" to reflect our ideological and ecological crisis toward civilized
suicide. But I think we also need to craft new narratives as news and evidence
comes in from the latest probes of the comets that once struck the earth—gave it
biological life, and wiped it out in at least two massive extinction epochs. And,
of course, I believe that Albert Schweitzer, despite his unconsciousness of the
lasting deleterious effects of the divine dominion ethic, should have the last
word as to what new reflective—educative and active—possibilities await in his
evermore attractive ethic of Reverence for Life. Here he is, at his most tentative
and tender, near the end of his Decay and Restoration of Civilization, opening
himself to the moral law within, unable to position himself as the questioning
speck.

Perhaps we shall have to look elsewhere than we have done hitherto for the real
basis of that theory of the universe which carries with it affirmation of the world
and of life. Previous thought imagined that it could deduce the meaning of life
from its interpretation of the universe. It may be that we shall be obliged to resign

Chapter Eight

ourselves to abandon the problem of the interpretation of the universe and to find
the meaning of our life in the will-to-live as this exists in ourselves (105).

We have already seen that far from being a necessity of thought,
Schweitzer's Reverence for Life might only approach such necessity once the
Ethic of Divine Dominion is made less necessary as the justification for human
transcendence at the expense of all other life; that it is revealed for what it is: a
"myth" in the worst sense, an ignoble lie that continues to enable such
dangerous pieties as "the lordship of the human spirit" and "the indomitable
human spirit." But perhaps we can see in Schweitzer's "Perhaps . . ." the will to
become part of a much larger cosmological story whose boundless ethics would
keep us here—no more and no less than the primates we are—utterly dependent
on our shared animality, in awe, not afraid, of the indomitable stars of which we
are made. At the very least, Schweitzer's rationalist ethic of rigorous
cosmological reflection upon our human decay and possible humanist
restoration binds us all, (the core meaning of "religion"), to educating ourselves
in a far more boundless ethics of reverence than either he—or "God"—was able
to understand as a final necessity of thought.
