Notes

1. There's no need for reminding readers of the "cutting room floor" reality of this sacred
Word of God document, perhaps, but one never knows, and so, as Ian Stuart so
succinctly reminds us: "Scholars tell us that there were originally at least two creation
stories – the "J" or Yahwist version and the "P" or Priestly version. The Yahwist is the
older of the two, written before 700 BCE during the dominance of the Tigris-Euphrates
valley by Assyria. The Priestly document was composed during the time when the
Hebrews were captive to the Babylonians in the 6th century BCE. These two versions
were combined—not without an element of confusion—in the final edition of Genesis we
have today." It is also important to understand that the Yahwist version does not mention
"dominion" or the injunction to "subdue the earth." Instead, we read: "And the Lord God
took the man, and put him into the garden of Eden to dress it and keep it" (Genesis II 15).
Obviously there is a world of rhetorical and, in the Schweitzerian sense of ethics, a
reverential difference in the injunctions to dress and keep, versus dominate and subdue.
John Black's book looks at these linguistic differences through the lenses of exegetical
and ecological analysis and concludes that the tension between thoughtless exploitation
and reflective, respectful stewardship has been with us ever since. It is significant that
only the ecologist, Black, among the three outside texts I consulted on Dominion as the
fundamental ethics of humanity, references Schweitzer's legacy: "Respect for the life of
plants has not been part of the western ethic, though there is a certain magnificence and
appearance of permanency about trees that catches the human imagination. By contrast,
man's awareness of his biological relationship to the rest of the animal kingdom is apt to
intervene in the process of dominating nature, as seen, for instance in the nature teaching
of St Francis, in Schweitzer's principle of 'Reverence for Life', in evolutionary
humanism or as an essential component in the rise of the modern conservation
movement" (38-9).
2. Paul Semonin, in his "Empire and Extinction: The Dinosaur as Metaphor for
Dominance in Pre-Historic Nature," concludes that our celebration of T-Rex and other
dominant carnivores of yore should lead us to reexamine "the roots of our obsession with
violence and domination in the natural world, which has turned us into the most
psychologically disturbed and self-destructive of the species." Although he does not link
"the roots of our obsession with . . . domination" to the myth of Divine Dominion, he

Chapter Eight

does remind us that, "In trying to grasp the powerful myths underlying scientific
civilization's fascination with "violent" prehistoric creatures, the current dinosaur craze
should alert us to the dangerous arrogance of our imperial view of the natural world, with
its irrepressible belief in the dynastic rule of a "dominant" species over all nature."
3. In researching the precedents and pervasiveness of what I am calling "the ethic of
Divine Dominion," I read through three very different books whose shared purpose is to
perform close readings of this problematic moment in the history of civilization. The
Character type of this "God," an authoritarian creator-figure, which can be found across
any number of scrolls preserved from the anonymous morass of ancient ("sacred")
literatures, speaks forth a mandate that has managed to control the discourse of much of
the world's ethics, politics, economics ever since—Schweitzer's ethic of reverence not at
all withstanding. Matthew Scully's study, Dominion, looks at the suffering of animals in
light of this ethic of divine dominion. He is a political conservative, a former speech
writer for President George W. Bush, and a self-described believer in God, whose piety
and faith are not particularly well-defined or liable to close examination. As a
professional ecologist writing at the beginning of the ecology movement in 1970, John
Black in his The Dominion of Man, looks carefully at the competing versions of Genesis
in which divine dominion is first set forth. (He wonders why the older Jahwist version,
which stressed "stewardship" rather than dominion, did not win out over the newer
version, which I am basing my argument upon.) As a practicing Christian who refuses to
read these texts with skepticism, lest he fall into atheism or cynicism, Black at least
makes a fascinating case for pushing the older version of Genesis that implies an ethic of
sustainability and a custodial ethic toward the earth and its creatures, if not into outright
"reverence." Finally, Lewis Petrinovich's Darwinian Dominion is an exquisite blend of
the latest findings from evolutionary biology, (his field of expertise), the tradition of
utilitarian justice (Kant to Bentham to Rawls) as applied to the theory of animal rights
(Tom Regan) and its activist movement (Peter Singer). Unlike with Scully and Black,
Petrinovich strikes no tone of religiosity or sentimentality, never mind a sorrowful or
righteous indignation with the problem of dominion. Read together, these three books
would help all Schweitzerians better understand the limits of reverence.

CHAPTER NINE

ALBERT SCHWEITZER'S DERIVATION OF ETHICS
AND ITS ROLE IN CONTEMPORARY ETHICS

CHRISTOPHER WEAVER

Advances in medical knowledge and technology, in addition to the ever
growing pharmaceutical industry, have driven our society to seek new methods
of research and drug testing in an effort to find better ways to treat disease in the
human body. Since scientists often lack the most fitting subjects for this
research (i.e., humans), due to ethical considerations, they turn to animals as
subjects. However, this use of animals has led to explosive controversy, with
animal rights activists destroying labs and threatening scientists on the one hand
and scientists performing vivisections and lethal dosage 50% tests on the other
hand. Often the level of debate does not rise above mudslinging and shouts and
screams between the two sides.

It was Albert Schweitzer who first stood up to question the utility of animal
research in his day. He argued that many of the experiments being done on
animals were not practical and were merely being used for demonstrative
purposes. Almost always, the phenomena being demonstrated to students in
these animal experiments were already well-known (Schweitzer, 2002). I argue
that by revisiting the philosophy of Schweitzer, instead of solely continuing
with our present impetus of scientific analysis, we will be much more capable of
transcending the current level of debate over ethical issues encompassing animal
research. This will be accomplished in part by gaining a new perception of our
world that will lift us out of the quagmire of dualities which have been created
by the singular use of analytic and rational methods.

By revisiting Schweitzer's philosophy based upon Reverence for Life, we
recognize a derivation of ethics that differs from our own derivation of ethics
today. Modern scientists, beginning with Descartes, have generally used
metaphysical theories and observations in order to mold their ethical values and
actions. However, Schweitzer argues that this is flawed. As we observe the
metaphysics of the world, we find that nature is inherently chaotic, destructive,
and grows and develops on the evolutionary basis of the survival of the fittest.

Chapter Nine

Therefore, ethics need to be formulated in another way, as Schweitzer explains,
by connecting reason with the heart (Schweitzer, 2002). Furthermore, I argue,
Schweitzer's words logically extrapolate to a more contemporary paradigm shift
based upon a balance between reason and empathy. In this shift, reason is an
understanding that penetrates to the depths of things and attempts to embrace
the wholeness of reality (Schweitzer, 2002), but the vague concept of the heart
is replaced with a more precise definition of empathy. The intellectual empathy
can be focused and distilled from the metaphorical connotations usually
attributed to the heart.

The current perception of nature is rooted in the Newtonian scientific
method and framework. This method is based upon observation and analysis, in
which we are detached observers of the world and remain outside of what we
are studying; essentially it represents a disconnected existence from all
surrounding life. In contrast to this, Schweitzer argues that we must be reverent
of all life. However, Reverence for Life is not attainable by pure analysis; as we
would be isolated from that life, and would be incapable of becoming reverent
of it. Schweitzer alludes to this as he defines Reverence for Life as an "ethical
mysticism." By connecting reason with empathy we are capable of seeing and,
more importantly, experiencing Reverence for Life. In doing so, we have thus
attained and will continually experience the ethical mysticism characteristic of
Schweitzer that will enable us to transform the issue of animal research.

However, there are many obstructions in the pathway from the current
debate on animal research to reaching the transformation brought upon by
Reverence for Life. I am convinced that limitations in current methods of
thinking and language used to address this moral issue stall the debate of animal
research. Furthermore, the problems of thinking and language stem from a
common root: a failure to perceive the interdependency of everything that
exists, more commonly expressed today as the unity of life. In this paper, I focus
on certain areas of endeavor
to perceive
interconnectedness, which are areas that have particular relevance to the issue of
animals in research. By revealing this failure in relation to thought and
language, there can be hope of finding a way to a greater understanding of the
effects of our actions in relationship to the all-encompassing biosphere, and how
those actions reflect upon our perceived need for animal testing and research.

impacted by a

failure

First, I claim that there is a failure to openly acknowledge and accept the
interdependency between mind and body; this failure results in a false analogy
between humans and animals based only upon metabolic similarities. Such
failure can lead to neglect of important differences that exist between species.
These differences can result in problems when findings of animal research are
extrapolated to humans.

Schweitzer's Derivation of Ethics

Secondly, I claim that there is a failure to acknowledge the need for intuitive
or empathic thought processes. This failure can manifest in at least two ways: 1)
the use of language that obscures the relationship between fact and value, and 2)
a dramatic failure in perception based upon an inappropriate neglect of empathy.
The result of these failures, which may act as a dense surrounding fog, can
conceal factors that may be morally relevant to the issue of the use of animals in
research.

Finally, I suggest that we might achieve the sort of perception and
understanding needed to adequately address issues raised by the use of animals
in research by finding a balance between two kinds of thought, reason and
intellectual empathy. This is a form of perception that transcends subject/object
dichotomies and thus allows for harmony, and thus also wholeness; such
balance may help to provide groundwork so that opponents can find a basis for
common discourse. If these two kinds of thought are brought into balance,
opposites need no longer be polemical and distinct as they once were. There can
be a unity of opposites or a synergism that can help to construct or develop a
new perception that recognizes the interrelatedness and interdependency of all
forms of life. I argue that if such perception is allowed to be a part of our
experience and play an integral role in how we understand the ethics of using
animals as research subjects, we will be much less likely to engage in the sorts
of abuses that have inspired the anger of animal rights activists; and we may be
led to question the effects of language on our perceptions.

that

there

is a failure

First, I claim

to fully acknowledge

the
interconnectedness between mind and matter, and more specific for this
argument, mind and body. The basis for this present dichotomy is a result of the
principles of the Cartesian duality, and the near universal acceptance of these
principles has resulted in the use of animals for scientific research. The method
of analytic thinking, pioneered by René Descartes, is the process of taking a
complex system, or a whole, and separating it into pieces to understand the
properties of the pieces. It was further reasoned that by understanding the
properties of the parts, one can understand the behavior of the whole. The
extension of these principles led to Descartes' vision of nature as one of duality,
in which the mind was entirely separate from matter. Thus, the material
universe, including living organisms, was a machine in which the behavior of
the whole could be understood solely through the process of analysis. This
process of analysis was applied to biology, and it was believed that the body, or
matter, could be purely understood through causality in the disciplines of
chemistry and physics. The body was believed to be purely mechanical, merely
a more complex clock in which the parts exist as a chain of cause and effect.
Therefore, the mechanisms of the body could be studied in similar animal
species, such as dogs, cats, primates, and rats. The ideas that the body is a

Chapter Nine

machine and consciousness and emotional states are separate from the body, and
thus have very little or no effect upon physiological processes, are assumed by
some who engage in animal research today.

The Cartesian theory of duality no longer has much universal relevance.
However, I want to acknowledge that Descartes' principles have been and still
are very useful. The studies of the mechanisms of the body which are the result
of his principles have been very successful and pragmatic, as they are
continually contributing to the understanding of disease mechanisms and the
production of vaccines. However, it has been a mistake to ignore consciousness
and its effects on physiology. That effect is often profound. Take, for example,
the change in the emotional state of a human being, such as when a person
becomes anxious. The heart starts beating faster and therefore if cardiac output
is to be maintained, the heart compensates with a reduction in stroke volume.
This in turn affects the volume of blood traveling to the brain, and therefore an
alteration in blood flow to the brain changes neurophysiology. The modification
in brain function alters the nerve impulses sent to visceral organs and muscles
which act upon this change in the body. An organ could produce an enzyme or
similar molecule and that molecule will enter the blood, and act upon a
mechanism in the body, which will in turn affect brain function. This process is
one example of many mind and body processes in which one substantially
changes the other.

Such changes imply that mind and body are integrally connected, through
their material nature. Thus, scientists have begun to seek to understand the mind
using a variant of the analytic method, and in doing so, have shown that the
mind and matter are made of the same material substances. Therefore, the split
between mind and body is no longer consistent with current understandings of
reality, and thus cannot be universal.

So if the level of consciousness affects physiology as stated above, and
animals do not have the same level of consciousness as humans, then research
conducted on animal species in search of cures for human diseases is not
universally suitable and may not be practically used to the degree it is now.
Since species differ in consciousness, as well as anatomy and physiology,
research done on nonhuman animals may not always provide a valid basis for
conclusions regarding humans. In other words, there may be some research
done on mice, rats, and dogs that cannot be applied to humans.

I am not implying the outright use of humans in research. I am merely
suggesting that better data might be drawn from human cell cultures and tissue
samples, without needing to sacrifice animal life in an effort to extend human
life. However, I believe that the reason animal testing is still widely used lies
much deeper in human consciousness than these generally well-known
observations; I believe the answer is found in language and thought processes.

Schweitzer's Derivation of Ethics

Secondly, I believe that there is a failure, especially in the scientific
enterprise, to acknowledge the need for intuitive or empathic thought. This
failure manifests itself in at least two ways.

One is the use of scientific language which clouds the relationship between
fact and value. Language plays a vital role in the blind acceptance of animal
research as language is a symbol for reality. In order to convey meaning and/or
communicate about perceived phenomena, we substitute the perception of the
phenomenon with a word, in which that word, and any word, is a symbol for
reality. We assign meaning to that word and therefore that perception can be
shared with others. From this, Descartes saw value separate from fact, which is
an expression of his mind/matter duality. Since the Cartesian paradigm of
thought is so deeply ingrained in our society, we have tended to believe that
scientific facts are independent of what we do, and therefore independent of our
values. However, scientific fact emanates from observation, which includes
human perceptions, values, and actions. If scientific fact emerges from these
things, and furthermore, quantum theory shows that the observer has a direct
effect on the object being observed, then the human values and perceptions, and
thus consciousness, cannot be separated from scientific fact. Thus, the
consciousness of both the subject and object in the experiment are integral parts
of the scientific method, and should not be overlooked. Furthermore, since
many early scientists had adopted Descartes' principles regarding reality, and
language is used as a symbol to convey meaning about reality, then the language
used by scientists reflect Cartesian principles of reality.

The second way this manifests itself is in the way science has focused on
matter to the exclusion, until only recently, of the mind. Thus, the language used
by some scientists can be void of the mind. The language lacks value and
emotion and is demonstrated today, in which scientists use the phrase "bilateral
lid suture" of the cat to refer to sewing a cat's eyelids shut, "thermally injured"
is compared to burned or scalded, and "enculeation and visual deprivation"
refers to cutting the eyes out of mice. As a result of the scientists' use of
morally detached and technical language, animal rights activists' fire back with
emotion laden responses and actions, which usually stem from anger. With this
present state of affairs, no progress will ever be made as scientists will continue
to perform "vivisections" (cutting into live animals), detached from the moral
implications it has, while animal rights activists continue to destroy labs,
simultaneously causing a possible worse situation for the animals while
engaging in unnecessary destruction.

The failure to recognize the need for intuitive thought expresses itself as an
inappropriate neglect of empathy. The neglect of empathy is caused by the
limitations in the current method of accepted thinking, which tends to involve
pure analysis. As Bergson points out, analysis is based upon moving around an

Chapter Nine

object and observing the object, in which the observer is outside and detached
from the object (or at least has that perception). Since analysis is based upon
movement around an object, the observation is thus dependent upon the point of
view. Once a point of view is taken, analysis continues as the reduction of the
object into elements that are already known. We continue to move around the
object, in successive points, in the hopes of gaining a complete understanding of
the object. However, this process is merely a translation or a development into
symbols taken from those successive points of view of the object (Bergson,
1903).

Science is mistaken when it confuses these symbols for reality, as symbols
are not reality; they are only imitations of reality constructed from elements that
are already known to us. Furthermore, as scientists may have different points of
view around one object due to the process of analysis, this is the reason for the
development of differing theories of the nature of one object, in which all
theories are valid. But, in fact, the scientists are all looking at the same object; in
much the same way that two cameras placed at different sides of one fish tank
will produce different, but complementary images of the same fish.

This was seen somewhat recently in string theory of modern physics in
which five separate string theories were found to be only parts of a greater and
more comprehensive single theory, known now as M theory. As long as the
observed concepts are only looked at side by side, they will only give us an
artificial reconstruction of the object, and even in some cases, different artificial
reconstructions of the same object due to false perceptions. The process of
analysis can be coupled with our reasoning capabilities to develop contexts that
may appear absolute, but they are not absolute, they are only relative. They are,
in fact, biased because they are artificial reconstructions composed of elements
that are already known to us. We have a tendency to invent the contexts and
relationships to fit our own bias or values, rather than discovering them. This is
the manifestation of the dichotomy between fact and value, as our values or
beliefs may have an effect on what we find when we are observing nature.
When we believe in something, our mind will seek to find it in our perceptions
of nature, and in this way, fact and value can be become interwoven.

This overall method is currently attempting to understand the systems theory
in which all living beings are members of ecological communities bound
together in a network of interdependencies. However, if persistent analysis is
used, this perception will definitively be thrown out. This perception of the
connections in all of life is one of contexts. But by taking contexts and wholes
and attempting to understand them through analysis, the whole is broken apart.
So, the contexts in the whole, which are like connecting strands binding
everything together, are severed as well. The dissolving of the connections
change how the parts function, so the part being looked at in isolation is

Schweitzer's Derivation of Ethics

different from that if it was contained in the whole and all connections were
present. Since this method of thinking is relative, the moral arguments
regarding animal research are at a standstill. This leaves little room for the
evolution of thought which will allow the argument to transcend its current
static state. The argument is in a static state because analysis has the potential to
ossify dynamic forms, putting them into static states to study them. Since
empathy is a dynamic and changing process of emotion, it cannot be solely
understood with the use of analysis, as the use of analysis would halt the
dynamic process and change the parts being looked at in isolation. Therefore,
with the current method of thinking, empathy is inappropriately neglected
because it is not compatible with our current impetus of the unbalanced rational
thinking process.

Finally, I suggest that we may achieve the sort of perception and
understanding essential to addressing the issues raised by the use of animals in
research by, as Schweitzer has argued, finding a balance between two kinds of
thought, reason and empathy. A balance between rational and empathic thought
is a synergism that creates a new perception of reality, which may be the basis
for a common discourse. It also recognizes the interdependency of all life, in
which an absence of this recognition can lead to lack of appreciation for the
value of life, and that lack of appreciation progresses towards self-defeating
behavior.

With solely using analysis, we perceive all life as being separate and
independent, and this is further accelerated by the appearance of a subject/object
dichotomy. The perception of this dichotomy is the root of the failure to
recognize the interconnections of all life. The process of observing the world
and rationalizing the life around us puts other living beings as objects, while
humans observe them believing to be separate from them. If we allow empathy
to come into balance with reason, it allows that dichotomy of what we are
observing and the observer to dissolve. Empathy is a mode of thinking in which
we enter the object in the sense of identifying with the object, and therefore are
connected with it. Empathy is much like how we relate to characters in a novel.
The author can describe many traits of a character and can have the character
speak and act, but not until we place ourselves inside that character do we
experience the actions and emotions that the character is experiencing and the
author is attempting to convey (Bergson, 1903). Schweitzer summarizes this
beautifully, as when empathy is used, life has devoted itself to life, and for at
least a moment, the division of life against itself has ceased (Schweitzer, 2002).
It is with this method of thinking that the subject/object dichotomy ceases to
exist and we are capable of pursuing the interconnections of all life in their
contexts, rather than viewing them as separate, isolated parts. If we were to use
both analysis and empathy we would understand the parts in isolation, but more

Chapter Nine

importantly, we would first understand the contexts and relationships of those
parts, and how they interact with each other. There is a synergy in the contexts
of parts that can only be understood if we were to use empathy as we would be
experiencing those connections rather than developing artificial contexts. Thus,
empathy can be a method of thinking to discover entirely new relationships and
ideas, rather than building those relationships from previously known elements.
This is precisely how Einstein discovered his theory of relativity; he imagined
himself riding upon a photon of light and the subsequent observations and
perceptions of his surrounding reality in this thought experiment led him to his
special theory of relativity. The discovery of new relationships and ideas will
manifest itself as a growth in the level of consciousness. This growth will alter
the perception of reality, and when the current perception of reality falters, it is
replaced with the perception of what is being experienced. What is being
experienced is the unity and dependency of all life which is discovered through
empathy. This new perception of relationships and unity allow the moral
argument to evolve, much like the method of thinking did, into a balance or
unity, which may be the common discourse.

The new perception of reality reexamines the current actions of humanity
and how these actions relate to our need for animal research in the first place.
By continually using reason and perceiving the world as subjects and objects,
we view animals and the environment at our disposal. So, we tend to harm both
the environment, since we perceive it as being disposable and also other animals
because we perceive them as lesser beings than us. But the earth's biological
diversity is crucial to the continued vitality and growth of both agriculture and
medicine, and maybe even to life on earth itself. However, many thousands of
plant and animal species have been rendered extinct or nearly extinct by
humans. It has been reported that two of every three species is estimated to be in
decline (Hinrichsen and Robey, 2000).

I believe that our increasing need to improve the quality and/or quantity of
life is a result of our demeanor and actions toward life and the environment. By
unnecessarily destroying and harming life and the environment, in addition to
the onset of synthesized chemicals, increased emissions, and the dependency
upon oil and petroleum-based products, we are indirectly harming ourselves as
it is shown with the increase in cancer and other diseases throughout the world.
For example, air pollution has drastically increased in recent years and its
effects killed three million people alone in 2000 (Hinrichsen and Robey, 2000).
Also, a Harvard study showed that the level of pesticides and PCBs
(polychlorinated biphenyls) that are highly resistant to both chemical and
biological degradation was higher in the breast tissue of women with cancer
than women without cancer. Furthermore, the study stated that most scientists

Schweitzer's Derivation of Ethics

agree that approximately 80% of all cancers are caused by environmental
factors. Essentially, cancer is an environmental disease (Harvard, 1999).

By harming ourselves, we have to do research on animals to find cures and
methods of treatment for the growth of disease. Thus we continue the process of
destruction, by harming animals to help humanity. If we no longer view our
reality with a subject/object distinction we are consciously aware of the
interconnectedness and unity of all life. This conscious awareness is not an
observational awareness, but a direct experience in the relationships and
interconnections of the entire biosphere. If we have an experience in these
relationships, then we will, as opposed to should, act in the reverence of all
living beings.

In conclusion, theory and experience must coincide in order for any ethics to
be developed. Schweitzer concisely states this point in his well-known
aphorism, "my life is my argument." As long as our actions and arguments are
not in harmony, we are outside and detached from what we are studying or
experiencing, whether we perceive it or not. Empathy is needed to heal the
tearing of the fabric of our world, in which the tearing is the result of analysis,
which produces a cleavage between subject and object. Empathy coupled with
reason is the path to Reverence for Life: the direct awareness and experience
brought about by empathy transforms oneself to Reverence for Life. Thus
Reverence for Life is the bridging of subject and object back together, and it
results in the harmony between our life and our arguments. Furthermore, the
bridging of subject and object reunifies the world that was torn apart by
analysis. We are capable of reconnecting with life and becoming part of the
process of life rather than being entirely separate from it. By using empathy to
enter with life, it is a way of healing for the mind and even physical processes
(since mind and body are indivisible) that may suggest an alternative to some
pharmaceuticals. Therefore, by being connected to life we don't find the same
need to develop medicines that need to be tested upon animals. Thus, empathy
and Reverence for Life not only manifests itself by reducing the need for animal
research in not harming the environment, but it also is a process of healing that
forms balance and harmony within the person. More importantly, it promotes
harmony with the surrounding environment that permeates through all of life.

Part Three

Schweitzer Today

CHAPTER TEN

ALBERT SCHWEITZER: THE INFLUENCE
OF HIS LIFE AND IDEAS ON THE EASTERN
SLAVIC COUNTRIES

SERGEY A. KIZIMA

After the collapse of the Soviet Union, communist ideas lost their influence
on the population of the newly independent Slavic states, and new ideas and
authorities became necessary. Albert Schweitzer happened to be one such
authority. His multi-faceted personality and interests provided excellent answers
to the many questions that the communist ideology had left unanswered. In this
article, I investigate how this implementation took place through teaching and
scientific activities; in religious and ecological life; in social work and health
spheres. I also describe how the life and activities of Schweitzer are described in
the mass media of Eastern Slavic countries, how Albert Schweitzer's personality
has permeated the emerging cultures, and who will carry on his legacy and
mission in Russia, Ukraine, and Belarus.

Methods

In my research I used two methods: analysis of written sources and
interviews. The interviews were treated as secondary sources because they
were conducted in only one of the Eastern Slavic countries (Belarus). My
attempt to find interview subjects was telling in and of itself: out of 23 people I
approached for an interview (among them heads and vice-heads of different
state bodies, professors in many disciplines), sixteen did not know who Albert
Schweitzer was, and five knew about him and his ideas but refused an interview,
as this was for a conference in the United States—Belarus currently has poor
political relations with the United States. Only two people, out of the 23 asked,
agreed to be interviewed.

Chapter Ten

The Use of Albert Schweitzer's Ideas in Teaching
and Scientific Activities

The Head of the Institute of Philosophy at the Academy of Science of
Russia, Academician V. Stepin, asserts that Albert Schweitzer's ideas are the
foundation for creating optimistic scenarios for human civilization. Unlike other
representatives of the Western cultural tradition, Schweitzer was able to
demonstrate the unity of theoretical concepts and their ethical realization in his
own life. This is important because this position has something in common with
Oriental tradition, and we should use Oriental and Western traditions together to
create this optimistic scenario (Shul'gin, 2004: 13). A. Gusejnov, editor of one
of Russian translations of Albert Schweitzer's editions, points out that
Schweitzer's spiritual transformation is similar to that of Augustine and is an
illustration of man's potential to transcend his own life (Gusejnov). In a
teaching manual on philosophy issued in Moscow, Schweitzer is described as
"the greatest humanist of modernity," his life as "life-exploit, life-legend," his
personality as "phenomenon of the European culture" (Kuznecova, 2004: 456).
U. Rotenfel'd asserts the need for people like Schweitzer in today's world,
because his life sets an example of a new way of life in society, patterns of
behavior not found prior to this societal transformation post-Glasnost. He
combined personal aims with highly moral social actions (Rotenfel'd). In V.
Kochergin's opinion, the importance of Schweitzer's life primarily lies in the
example it sets for young people. He was a real humanist, he dedicated his life
to others and we should encourage the young to follow his example (Kochergin,
2005).

Not all the scientists who devote their time to researching Albert
Schweitzer's ideas find them to be applicable to present realities. For example,
in the teaching manual "Philosophy of Crisis" issued in 2003 in Moscow, there
is a special chapter "Albert Schweitzer: Culture Crisis of Philosophy of Life—
Crisis of Culture." But nothing is said about how the implementation of his
ideas can be useful for Russia, which is constantly in crisis (Sidorina, 2003: 82-
90). And not all scientists are so kind to the personality of Albert Schweitzer.
For example, in one edition (Dictionary of Culture of the 20th Century), he is
called a typical example of a schizoid-autist, like Ludwig Wittgenstein (Rudnev,
1997). Regrettably, the author forgot to demonstrate his own psychoanalysis
examination results. It could be a helpful source in understanding why he tries
to find roots of humanistic orientation in psychiatric problems.

In the practice of teaching, Albert Schweitzer's ideas are a part of special
courses, usually at philosophical and medical faculties of state universities.
According to post-Soviet specifications, non-state institutes and universities are
mainly aimed at training effective market professionals, and less care is taken

Schweitzer's Influence on Eastern Slavic Countries

with the humanitarian component of education. Typically, humanitarian
disciplines are concentrated in the Department of Humanities (philosophy,
ethics, culture studies, religious studies), while special humanitarian courses are
not welcomed by administrators—all efforts are concentrated on the adaptation
of future specialists for market economy. State universities, on the other hand,
have a long tradition of humanitarian curricula, even in the education of
technicians. One such course, devoted to the crisis of European culture and its
impact on the philosophy of the 20th century, is offered by the philosophy
faculty at the Russian State Humanitarian University. The course also includes
Schweitzer's reflections on the necessity of overcoming the crisis in culture.
Another special course, "Modern Foreign Social Philosophy," is taught at
Rostov State University. It considers the theme "Social-Philosophical Views of
Albert Schweitzer" as one of six main topics. The lecturer of the Department of
Humanities of the Specialized Scientific Study Center for Physics, Mathematics,
Chemistry, and Biology Education at Novosibirsk State University, Alexander
Anikin, uses tests on Schweitzer's works in his teaching practice.

The special course "Nature of the Morals," taught at the Philosophical
Faculty of Moscow State University by lecturer A. Skvorcov, includes a study
of Schweitzer's view on culture and ethics. A course in bioethics is taught at
Ivanov State University in the Department of Physiology of Man and Animals.
Included in the syllabus is the statement: "A. Schweitzer—founder of modern
Bioethics." "Ecological Ethics," an optional course for ordinary high schools (8-
11 forms) in Russia, assumes the study of Schweitzer's ethical views (Boreiko).
The analysis of Schweitzer's ethical views exists even in the Russian Single
State Examination Test on Social Sciences. F. Rudinskij, Co-Head of Expert
Council of Ombudsman of Russian Federation, mentioned in 2001 that while
teaching human rights it is necessary to discuss Schweitzer's "noble deeds"
(Rudinskij, 2001). At the same time, the course "Methods of Studying Culture,"
necessary for students involved in studies for specialized "cultural studies" at all
Russian universities, only includes Schweitzer's book as a recommended source
but unfortunately doesn't assume the study of his ideas in the syllabus of the
course.

Albert Schweitzer's teachings are also discussed in PhD dissertations
defended by scientists of the Eastern Slavic countries. Some of them are fully
devoted to analysis of his ideas and their adaptability to post-Soviet practice.
They are as follows:

Cvetkov Igor'. Theological and Philosophical Views of Albert Schweitzer.

Moscow: 2000.

Posokhova Ludmila. Ethical Views of Albert Schweitzer in the Context of

Modern Problems of Humanization of Education. Sochi: 2003.

Chapter Ten

There are also a number of PhD dissertations in which Albert Schweitzer's

views have been mentioned:

Grigorash V.V. The Formation of Conscience as Moral Quality of Person
in the Process of Ethical Education of Senior Pupils. Moscow: 1994.
Guseva N.A. The Principle of Humanization and his Realization in the

Educational Process in the Modern School. Brjansk: 1994.

Kozlova O.N. Humanism in Education: Socio-Cultural Problems of

Realization. Moscow: 1994.

Korosteleva A.A. The Formation of Spiritual Values of Senior Pupils.

Moscow: 1992.

Reaction and Interpretation of Albert Schweitzer's Ideas
by Means of Different Theological Teachings

In the Eastern Slavic countries it is commonly thought that Albert
Schweitzer is far from being a real Christian. One dictionary states: "For liberal
theology, and later for Schweitzer, understanding of Christianity as mainly, or
exceptionally, a moral doctrine." (Culturologija: 159) This is a typical view of
Schweitzer. The Orthodox priest Alexander Men', a renowned cleric in Eastern
Slavic countries, wrote of Schweitzer: "Of course, I can't think of him either as
a Christian writer or a philosopher. For me he is a religious thinker, but very far
from Christianity. He is closer to Tolstoy, to Stoics whom he loved so much.
But I respect his moral position, his fight for humankind" (Men', 1999). This
respect of A. Men' to Albert Schweitzer is clearly visible in numerous
quotations he uses in his works and even in the epilogue that was published in
translation of "Verfall und Wiederaufbau der Kultur.
the Russian
Kulturphilosophie. Erster Teil," issued in 1993 in Moscow. A. Men' also cited
Schweitzer to demonstrate that everybody can be saved. While speaking about
Schweitzer as a non-Christian thinker, he practically calls him a saint: "And our
century, black, disgusting, full of hate, destruction and evil, at the same time is
also a holy century. No one century could give such people as Mother Maria,
Albert Schweitzer, Maximilian Kolbe, and Mother Theresa . . . Our time is the
time of martyrs, saints, and heroes" (Men', 1999).

At the same time it is necessary to remember that Alexander Men' was a
very unusual orthodox priest, and his ideas were not supported by the traditional
Russian church. For example, there is a tradition of teaching the course "Ethics
and Axiology in Religion" in Russia: students in theology workshops are
supposed to compare thoughts of different Western specialists in ethics,
including Albert Schweitzer, with Orthodox tradition because "it makes obvious

Schweitzer's Influence on Eastern Slavic Countries

futile attempts to transfer Western system of moral and social values onto the
Russian soil" (Ehtika I Aksiologija v Religii).

In the interview of Orthodox Abbot, Father Pavel Boyankov done for this
article, he characterizes Albert Schweitzer as a "typical Protestant with liberalmodernist views." His Reverence for Life postulate recalls pantheism of early
times. The Orthodox Church need not adopt anything from his teachings; it has
enough ideas of its own. The decline of Western Christian churches (both
Protestant and Catholic) forced active people to leave in an attempt to find
realization of their abilities somewhere, far from the "civilized world." The
main difference between Western Christianity and Orthodox, according to the
abbot, can be seen through the comparison of life and deeds of Albert
Schweitzer with life and work of Saint Ljuka Vojno-Yaseneckij, canonized by
Russian Orthodox Church in 2000. His life at many points is similar to the life
of Albert Schweitzer and at the same time is different. Similarities:

(cid:120) Period of life: Schweitzer—1875-1965; Saint Ljuka—1877-1961;
(cid:120) Profession—both were doctors and worked in hospitals;
(cid:120) Both had highly acknowledged works in theology and science;
(cid:120) Both were repressed—Schweitzer during the First World War got in a
French camp, Saint Ljuka during Stalinist repressions got in the GULAG
camp;

(cid:120) Both received the highest possible awards for their time and place—

Schweitzer the Nobel Peace Prize, and Saint Ljuka the Stalin Prize.

The main difference is that Schweitzer left the Protestant church where he
was a priest, while Saint Ljuka became a priest during cruel repressions against
Orthodox Church when priests were killed by thousands (Boyankov, 2005).

As mentioned earlier, the priest Igor' Cvetkov defended his PhD dissertation,
"Theological and Philosophical Views of Albert Schweitzer" in 2000 in
Moscow. His attitude toward Schweitzer is not any more favorable than that of
Pavel Boyankov. He mentions, in particular, that Albert Schweitzer is not a
theologian, because in his study of Christ he was not interested in Christianity
but in the person of Christ; he is not a historian, because he did not use any
scientific system when he worked with sources; he is not a philosopher because
he didn't try to create a system from the world around him; and he is not a sage
because communists from the USSR and GDR used him and his reputation for
their own aims (Cvetkov).

Such an attitude on the side of many Orthodox priests does not mean that
Albert Schweitzer's ideas are futile from the viewpoint of the Orthodox Church.
Partial understanding of this rejection can be found in protest of the Orthodox
Church against intensive agitation of ideas of Catholicism and Protestantism at

Chapter Ten

its territory after the collapse of the Soviet Union. After the end of the
Communist regime, the Orthodox Church found itself exhausted and weak after
seventy years of repressions from atheists. However, instead of help from other
Christian confessions, the church found itself facing rich and powerful
competitors. Rejection of ideas of such an influential Protestant as Albert
Schweitzer by Orthodox priests is a part of the battle for the minds of old.

In E. Balagushkin's research on the revival of paganism in Russia, the ideas
of Albert Schweitzer are mentioned as an important source for different
branches of new Russian paganism. So, in "The Teacher Ivanov's System,"
(Sistema Uchitelja Ivanova) there is a thesis that "it is necessary to feel pity for
all alive and value it." In the "Church of Last Precept," (Sinkreticheskoe
Hristianstvo Vissariona), Schweitzer's postulate about the Reverence for Life is
a part of the attitude toward the environment. At the same time, Balagushkin
remarked that for new pagan cults it is common to have a small ethical
component, unlike Christianity, for which ethics is the essence, according to
Schweitzer (Balagushkin, 1999).

In the Eastern Slavic countries, a number of organizations also work on the
fringes of religious/secular activity. One such group is the International Creative
Organization "Self-Creation" (Mezhdunarodnaja Tvorcheskaja Organizacija
Samosozidanie). Their aim is to involve all people in the problem of
improvement of consciousness, free them according to the scheme of Albert
Schweitzer. But the final aspiration is to instill in each individual a personality
similar to God, filled at the same time with Spirit of Reason (Makarcev).

Albert Schweitzer's Ideas and Environmental Problems
in the Eastern Slavic Countries

Albert Schweitzer's ideas are very popular among environmental activists in the
Eastern Slavic countries. During special meetings and conferences, love and
benevolence toward nature is a common theme (Efremov, 2001), (Khen, 1997).
Yet some scientists think that Schweitzer got tangled up in a trap of subjective
illusions because he considered thinking of life as the absolute, while most
ecologists will not share this opinion (Shatalov, Olejnikov, 1997).

U. Kurikalov maintains that Schweitzer's teachings are the best argument
against critiques of Christianity that claim it has an anti-ecological focus. On the
contrary, this understanding can help find an answer to ecological crisis. The
Christian cosmism, especially in countries with orthodox population, can be
helpful to develop necessary ecological consciousness (Kurikalov, 1998). A.
Arkhangelskaja speaks of the importance of Schweitzer's ideas in the
development of the new ecological culture for the 21st century. The importance
of this lies in understanding the necessity of interplay between Oriental and

Schweitzer's Influence on Eastern Slavic Countries

European cultures (Arkhangelskaja). P. Bachinskij from Dnepropetrovskij
National University in the Ukraine, stated that Schweitzer's ideas formed the
basis for the newly created and rapidly expanding field of bioethics
(Bachinskij). R. Karpinskaja maintained the necessity of understanding the
Reverence for Life principle. Sociobiology treated this principle too narrowly,
in the mainstream of evolution and biological interpretation, and as a result the
conception lacks the idea of necessity to work all life in order to increase selfconsciousness, which was originally stated by Schweitzer (Karpinskaja, 1996:
5-40). R. Karpinskaja came to be famous not only because of her numerous
scientific works, but also because of the introduction in 1993 of the concept of
"Bio-philosophy" to describe the field of knowledge that contains synthesis of
biological and philosophical knowledge, developed by A. Schweitzer, V.
Vernandskij, and T. de Chardin (Karpinskaja, 1993), (Shatalov, Oleinikov,
1997). Some scientists even believe that the Reverence for Life postulate can
become the main basis of "Biophilosophy" (Khen, 1997).

Zazdravnov A. and Zazdravnova O., from State Agrarian University and
National University of Radio-Electronics in the Ukraine, came to the opinion
that the best strategy for the survival of mankind is co-evolution, closely tied
with the necessity to recognize the value of all forms of life, as it was proposed
earlier by Schweitzer. This point of view on communication and interaction is
the last bastion of the humanism that can protect human being in the flow of
permanent changes of the world (Zazdravnov A.P., Zazdravnova O.I.).

A. Nikolskij argued that millions of lives are needlessly destroyed every
minute. Schweitzer's ideas, and especially the Reverence for Life principle, is
becoming especially important. It is necessary to fight with the tendency of
"poetic" perception of killing, especially as regards hunting. Russian tradition
has a well-developed esthetics of hunting that turns this uncivilized action into a
favorite sport for the elite (Nikolskij, 1999).

S. Mamontov singled out two people whose names should be known by
every educated man and woman in the field of Christian ecology – Saint Francis
of Assisi and Albert Schweitzer. He, like V. Stepin and A. Arhangelskaja,
emphasized the special importance of Schweitzer's work, which has enriched
both Oriental and Western traditions (Mamontov, 1999).

The Implementation of Albert Schweitzer's Ideas in Social
Work and Health Spheres

The Russian magazine "Ethics' Education" has noted that as society is going
through moral decay, social relations and value priorities fall out of harmony.
Thus, it is necessary to find a basis to restore the moral forces of society and the
ethical landmarks for the education of children. Ethics should be introduced in

Chapter Ten

schools for this aim, with Albert Schweitzer's relation to man and life as the
main values to be taught in the ethics course (Nravstvennoe Vospitanie v
Shkole).

Schweitzer's ideas can have a positive social impact through meetings and
discussions on the topics from his books. To that end, the Department of
Biochemistry of Nizhegorodskoj State Medical Academy regularly conducts
literature meetings devoted to the life and the ideas of Schweitzer. The
association "REMA" conducted a conference for readers of one of Schweitzer's
books in 2005. Similarly, In Voronezh (Russia), the Voronezh Center of the
World Academy of Medicine, named in honor of Albert Schweitzer, has been
opened on the campus of Voronezh State Medical Academy.

Schweitzer's ideas are also being implemented in Carskoe selo (Russia),
where the project of a German enterprise "Albert Schweitzer" is being realized.
It is called "Albert Schweitzer in Pushkin" and the main idea is to create a
system that will offer aid to orphans. A social building named after Albert
Schweitzer has been proposed at this site, to launch this project.

Interpretation of Life and Ideas of Albert Schweitzer
in the Eastern Slavic Countries Mass Media

The mass media of Eastern Slavic countries contains mostly positive articles on
the life and the ideas of Albert Schweitzer (these articles are, as a rule,
published in magazines that try to demonstrate their civil position and interest in
the fate of society). The articles usually contain discussion of Schweitzer's life
and the ethical choices that he made as a young man, and then implemented for
the rest of his life. The authors try to inspire readers to follow his way of
humanism and service to humankind. For example, an article appeared in the
magazine "Novuj Akropol" by D. Zubov under the title "Genius of Humanity."
The article contains factual errors about Schweitzer's life. The author insisted
that Schweitzer's initial decision to change his life so radically was made when
he was just 29 years old, but he only got public recognition at the end of his life
which is blatantly untrue; still, the article is still very optimistic and inspiring.
The author concludes: "The life of a great man is always an example. The nature
generously gifted talents to him. Few can play Bach with such outstanding
ability as he did, write such deep books. . . . But it is easy for everybody to start
loving people; feel reverence for life like Schweitzer" (Zubov).

Exploitation of Schweitzer's Fame

It is a pity, but there are always people who try to exploit the reputations of
really great men. In 1999, the mass media of Turkmenistan announced that

Schweitzer's Influence on Eastern Slavic Countries

Father of all Turkmen, President Niyazov, was awarded an international prize
named in honor of Albert Schweitzer, and founded by the Academy of Medicine
of Poland to award heads of states and public figures for outstanding
achievements in the development of humanism in society and building of the
national model of democracy. It was also noted that this prize was the second
best-known award of its kind in the world (to the Nobel Prize). For many
people, this came as a shock; Turkmenistan is anything but a democratic
country, and its strictly authoritarian government has been accused of flagrant
and numerous human rights violations.

It was later learned that this action was merely a show organized by
Turkmenistan's president. Money was paid to the owner of a small private
enterprise "Academy of Medicine" registered in Poland, a specialist in sexual
pathology Kazemir Imelinskij, to come and to deliver this "prize," which was a
complete fabrication invented by himself (Ehsenov, 2002). Traces of activities
of this enterprise can be found in many republics of the former Soviet Union.
For example, two university rectors in Uzbekistan also became laureates and
academicians of this academy (V oblasti Zdravoohranenija), as have rectors in
Russia and Ukraine. On the website of one of the Russian universities at which
Mr. Imelinskij bestowed his award, he's falsely mentioned as a laureate of the
Nobel Peace Prize (Severnuj Medicinskij Gosudarstvennyj Universitet).

The People Who Are Considered Continuers of Albert
Schweitzer's Mission in the Eastern Slavic Countries

Albert Schweitzer has become a popular figure to use when comparing the work
done by others who are walking in his footsteps. Some late-Soviet and post-
Soviet authors were trying to find local people who behaved like him or had a
similar point of view. Even the Russian poet Alexander Block is mentioned as a
person who "found the way that the prominent philosopher of the XX century,
Albert Schweitzer, will call later as 'Reverence for Life'" (Drozhneva, 1999:
58). In the late Soviet times, the academician Andrei Sakharov was often cited
as an "heir" of Schweitzer (Rotenfel'd). Sakharov, an eminent Soviet nuclear
physicist, dissident and human rights activist, won the Nobel Peace Prize in
1975 but was not allowed to leave the Soviet Union to receive it. He died in
December 1989.

The next man to continue Schweitzer's mission was the above-mentioned
Orthodox priest Alexander Men'. His moral influence on the people is often
considered the equal of Schweitzer's. As written in one article, people that tried
to find moral perfection came to both of them (Naumova, Belova, 2005).
Alexander Men' was killed by an unknown murderer in September 1990.

Chapter Ten

Jurij Bandazhevskij is another figure who might be considered to be
continuing the work of Schweitzer (Posokhova, 2003: 96). A talented musician,
he became a doctor, wrote more than 240 scientific works, and came to help
people who live near Chernobyl, the nuclear power plaint in Ukraine that in
1986 suffered the worst accident in the history of nuclear power; more than half
of the radioactive fallout released by the plant landed in Belarus. He was rector
of Gomel State Medical Institute from 1990 up to 1999. In 2001, he was
sentenced to 8 years for bribery. According to Amnesty International,
Bandazhevskij's real crime was the attention he focused on the attempts by the
Belarusian government to return people to districts that had been contaminated
by radiation. He repeatedly insisted that this was a danger to public health
(Bandazhevskij).

Conclusion

Albert Schweitzer's ideas and personality became an important part of
intellectual and cultural life in the Eastern Slavic countries. His Reverence for
Life postulate formed the basis of bioethics and bio-philosophy. Scientists of the
Eastern Slavic countries consider his ideas as an important part of the scenario
for survival of society. Teachers use his teachings in their educational activities.
Mass media inspires people to follow his moral feats. At the same time, some
people underestimate the importance of his life and personality. Perhaps, this is
a necessary attribute to mark any genuine greatness.

It is an important sign that out of the three people who were perceived as
continuers of his mission in the Soviet Union and the eastern Slavic countries,
two are dead (one of them killed) and the third is in prison.

CHAPTER ELEVEN

PRODUCING CHARACTER
IN A CONSUMING SOCIETY

SUZANNE S. HUDD

.

For the past several years, my research efforts have been focused on
character education, the instruction of core ethical values and cultivation of
good conduct in elementary and secondary school children (McClellan, 1999).
Character education programs are increasingly being incorporated in public
school curricula across the country. Perhaps the best example of a "typical"
character education curricula is "Character Counts!". The "Character Counts!"
program contracts with hundreds of educational organizations throughout the
United States. Schools can purchase curricular materials, posters and a various
types of educational aids to facilitate instruction around a core set of values.
There are a variety of alternative programs similar to "Character Counts!," each
emphasizing a unique list of virtues.

As an alternative to these short lists, a number of schools offer character
education using indirect methods (Benninga, 1991). In these programs, the
teacher does not review a values list. Instead, the discussion is oriented to values
clarification through the resolution of moral dilemmas. The term character
education has also been associated with programs in service learning and
volunteerism which emphasize community experience coupled with classroom
instruction. With each of these methods, the goal of character education is not to
supplant, but rather to support moral development that occurs primarily at
home. Character educators stress the importance of engaging parents and
community leaders to ensure successful character development.

Over the last few years, schools in 48 states have introduced programs in
character education (Howard et al., 2003). Public support for the addition of
character education to school curricula is the strongest it has been since the
1950's (McClellan, 1999). So much has our support of school-based character
instruction grown that the federal government tripled funding for character
education, to nearly $25 million in the No Child Left Behind Act of 2001. Why
are schools being mandated to teach character? Can character really be taught?

Chapter Eleven

An often-cited survey of 12,000 high school students conducted by the
Josephson Institute of Ethics (2002) suggests that children are more likely to
steal, cheat, shoplift, and lie to their teacher and parents than they were only a
decade ago. While more recent survey data indicate that there has been a decline
in such behaviors, the percentage of students who engage in these activities is
still seen as a cause for such concern (Josephson Institute for Ethics, 2004).
Character educators contend that we are experiencing a national "crisis of
character" that necessitates the inclusion of formalized curricula in character in
public schools. Among the trends they identify are: rising youth violence,
growing disrespect for parents, teachers and authority figures, the deterioration
of language and increased levels of "self-destructive" behavior such as
premature sexual activity, substance abuse and suicide (Likona, 1996;
Josephson Institute for Ethics, 2002).

While my earlier work has emphasized the potential effects of our cultural
imperative for efficiency on character development, it has recently occurred to
me that the phenomenon of consumerism presents a perhaps even greater
deterrent to the formation of character. In contemporary character we seem to be
focused on production: creating in our children an understanding, awareness and
appreciation of what character is and what it requires such that they will
reproduce it in society at large. In this paper, I wish to address what I see as an
important contradiction: our emphasis on producing character in a consumeroriented society. Specifically, I will consider whether Schweitzer's work and
life offers insights that may enable us to reconcile this inconsistency.

My interest in applying Schweitzer's work to my research has evolved
mainly from my personal experience with the Schweitzer Institute. Last year, I
spent a week with a group of about a dozen Quinnipiac University students in
Nicaragua on a trip sponsored by the Schweitzer Institute. Our goal for the
week-long visit was to construct an addition on an elementary school. While we
were there, I had the opportunity to observe many of Schweitzer's ideals in
action in the behavior of our Quinnipiac students and I found myself wondering
how each of these talented young people had come to realize the value of
personal sacrifice—or as they might describe it: "why I chose to mix cement in
the hot sun on my spring break."

In speaking about his decision to go to Africa, Schweitzer observed: "only a
person who, thanks to his own efforts or the devotion of friends is free from
material needs can nowadays take the risk of undertaking such a personal task
(1933, 89)." He wrote these words in 1933, when material "needs" were no
doubt defined quite differently. My guess is that the students who I
accompanied to Nicaragua did not see themselves "free from material need." I
know from personal experience, having made the decision myself to spend a
year teaching school in Central America at the age of twenty, that it can be

Character in A Consumer Society

difficult to let go. When I began working on this paper, I was curious to see
whether Schweitzer's work might offer me insights as to how these students
came to disregard their personal interests and instead were motivated to pursue
self-sacrifice in our culture of self-satisfaction.

I will spend my remaining time first, briefly describing the phenomenon of
materialism in our society. I will then consider what Schweitzer had to say
about materialism and its effects on mankind. I will conclude by considering
how Schweitzer's work might inform the production of character in our society
of consumption.

The Consumer Culture in Which We Seek to "Create"

Character

We live in a material society in which many of us judge our worth, and the
worth of others based on extrinsic factors and
labels. Contemporary
consumerism is distinguished by the extent to which children and youth have
been "swept up" in the movement. The pervasiveness of consumerism has
affected both the behavior and beliefs of our young people. By the age of ten, a
child will typically visit stores nearly 270 times per year, on average (Sutherland
and Thompson, 2003). What were defined as "luxuries" only a couple of
decades ago are now perceived as expectations. Roper routinely asks adults to
identify the essential elements that comprise "the good life." While in 1975,
38% of respondents indicated that "a lot of money" was essential to the good
life, by 1994, nearly 63% of survey respondents felt this way—a 25% increase.
For about half of the material items on this list, the greatest increases in the
share who consider them important to the "good life" were among 18 to 29-year
olds (Anonymous, 1996). "Young adults pursue a more materialistic version of
the American dream. . . . they are more likely than baby boomers to see money
as the most important reason to work." (Crimmins et al., 1991)

Schweitzer had strong views on the effects of materialism, which was an
evolving trend in his lifetime. While he acknowledged that progress, as
evidenced in materialism is an essential element in modern civilization (1933),
he stressed the importance of coupling materialism within an ethical foundation.
He noted:

Civilization which develops itself on the material, and not in a corresponding
degree on the spiritual side, is like a ship with a defective steering gear, which
becomes more unsteerable from moment to moment and so rushes on to
catastrophe. (Civilization and Ethics, 1929: 2)

Chapter Eleven

Amidst our "hyperconsumption" the balance between the material and
spiritual has been lost in contemporary America; Americans frequently incur
high levels of debt to obtain their material desires (Ritzer, 2004). Ironically,
overspending has been described as a coping mechanism for dealing with the
everyday pressures produced by excess consumerism (Schor, 2004).

Schweitzer attributed the imbalance between material and spiritual to the
ability of organizations and corporations to control thinking. In this observation,
he identified perhaps one of the most detrimental effects of materialism, the loss
of self confidence. In Out of My Life he argues:

Corporate bodies do not look for their strength in ideas and in the values of the
people for whom they are responsible. They try to achieve the greatest possible
uniformity. . . . in this way they hold the greatest power. . . . From every side in
the most varied ways it is hammered into [man] that the truths and convictions he
needs for life must be taken away from the associations that have rights over him
(225).

Contemporary marketers rely on brand loyalty as a mechanism to control
consumption in such a way that it is guided by associations—that is, the label
placed on the product rather than the quality of its taste or appearance is the
driving force behind the purchase. Marketing researchers have found that 90 %
of all requests from children are made with brand names, and that brand
preferences develop as early as 20 months of age (Sutherland and Thompson
2003).

Consumerism alters our consciousness in many ways (Ritzer, 2004).
Interactions centered on commodities become less personal. We have witnessed
this phenomenon in our own classrooms. When our students look to "consume"
knowledge rather than learn, they think less about the process and focus more
on the product: What is my grade? Why must I take Research Methods when
I'm not going to pursue a career in research? More and more, throughout
various settings in which they find themselves, our young people are
experiencing their lives as a series of "transactions." And so they ask: What can
I get from this experience, as opposed what can I give?

This trend—of choices guided by associations—is perhaps best exemplified
in the statistics on youth volunteerism. The number of youth pursuing volunteer
experiences has risen to record levels. In 2004, nearly 29.4 percent of teenagers
volunteered. A closer look at these statistics reveals an interesting trend,
however: 36% of young people in twelfth grade who intend to apply to college
volunteer while only 19% without plans to further their education do so (Trends
Databank and Volunteerism, 2004). It seems that volunteering is now seen as an
essential prerequisite to create another association: admission to college. In a
recent New York Times article (Steele, 2005), students offered various reasons

Character in A Consumer Society

for pursuing community service including: self development, maturity, the
acquisition of language skills, going to exotic places and learning how other
people live. Notably absent from the list is the notion of sharing talents, or
giving to those less fortunate. One parent was quick to observe: "They have to
get out of their environment and reflect on those things that money doesn't buy.
It's not their fault they come from Fairfield County." Thus, it appears in this
case, volunteerism is truly about the volunteer.

Schweitzer (1933) noted that: "Material dependence has an effect on [the]
mind, so that we finally believe that [we] are not qualified to come to [our own]
conclusions (225)." I find it somewhat ironic that while we live in an era of vast
consumer choice, our freedom of choice is increasingly constrained. Rather than
making choices based upon individual preference, what our young people
choose is often defined by larger forces: marketers and consumer trends. Even
the decision to volunteer can now be orchestrated through organizations that
market a "vacation slash volunteer experience" in a developing country for
young college-bound adults.

What does all of this have to do with character development? Character is
often described by character educators as "what you do when no one is
looking." Consumerism has created an environment in which we are constantly
looking at one another; where all that matters is the appearance. Little time is
spent examining what is under the surface, what is inside the person. One
rationale used by students who violate academic integrity standards is:
"Everyone does it." It is consumerism that has trained them to respond to
external cues rather than to their internal moral compass.

Conclusion

What can we do, then, to encourage the production of character in our
consumer society? Contemporary America is dominated by an appreciation for
what is visible. Appearances matter. Our pursuit of material satisfaction,
however, leads us away from our internal lives, which, according to Schweitzer
(1939), play an important role in the development of one's life philosophy, their
character. In his words:

We do not have enough inwardness, we are not sufficiently preoccupied with our
own spiritual life, we lack quietness: and this is not only because in our exacting,
busy existence it is difficult to obtain, but because, ignoring its importance, we
do not take pains to secure it, being too easily contented with our living our lives
as unrecollected men who merely aim at being good (43).

Schweitzer (1929) argued that "the mere setting up of lists of virtues and
vices is like vamping on the keyboard and calling the ensuing noise (25)." Much

Chapter Eleven

of what is offered through school-based character curricula seems to resonate
with his statement. At face value, character education offers our children a set of
ideals to live by. Unfortunately, they spend much of their time in a world that
often contradicts what they have been taught.

Our emphasis on formal character education programs suggests that we fear
that in the absence of a defined list of values or a mandated community service
requirement, our young people will not innately practice character. Some have
criticized contemporary character education for this "fix the kid" orientation
(Kohn, 1997). The writings of Schweitzer support this critique. As he (1931)
notes, "the knowledge of life which we grown-ups have to pass on to the
younger generation [is] . . . [g]row into your ideals, so that life can never rob
you of them (100)." Schweitzer's words admonish us that we need not lack faith
in our children, but rather we should direct our mistrust at the world around
them which often prevents them from sustaining their ideals.

Schweitzer speaks of character as a give and take process. His advice to
young people, as evidenced in the following words, reflects this sentiment:
"Impart as much as you can of your spiritual being to those who are on the road
with you and accept as something precious what comes back to you from them"
(1931, 92). This statement holds appeal in our consumption-oriented society:
there is something to be had. The dilemma, however, lies in the fact that what
will be returned is intangible. In our material world where brand recognition
predominates, we do not emphasize intangibles. Thus, our children may be
inadequately prepared to either recognize or value them. Schweitzer's words
encourage us to consider how we as adults might better teach our children to
recognize and value intrinsic over extrinsic rewards.

Character development is ultimately about teaching our children to
recognize a "good" (and my play on words is intentional here) that is valuable,
but unseen. Rather than celebrating the "A" my daughter ultimately received on
her final exam in Science last May, we chose to celebrate the hard effort she put
into studying for the exam—before we knew what the outcome would be. The
evening after the test, during dinner, we celebrated the process, the hard work
that went into preparing, and a best effort. In doing this, we encouraged her to
recognize the job had already been "well done," as well as to recognize that the
reward was in the process, not in the product.

The Quinnipiac students who I accompanied to Nicaragua clearly share this
appreciation for intangibles. In the words of one student: "While I was there, I
realized I don't take time to sit back and enjoy life—it's not the American way.
The people in Nicaragua may not have anything, but they have everything." The
phrase "you get more than you give," floated among the volunteers. The fact
that they could see what they had received, although it was intangible, suggests
that they understood something very important. Our students arrived in

Character in A Consumer Society

Nicaragua with bags full of supplies. The bags were emptied while we were
there, but the students left with something much greater.

Character often evolves in fits and starts: it sometimes arises where it is
least expected, because of its absence. Schweitzer (1933) tells us: "I often had to
recognize that the need to 'do something special' was born of a restless spirit.
Such people wanted to dedicate themselves to larger tasks because those that lay
nearest to them did not satisfy them (88)." A restless spirit is one with the
capacity for defying what is expected: one who is not satisfied with what is
taken for granted or, as a sociologist might describe it, "normative." In our
material culture, where conformity and convention rule—where everyone is
watching, always—we are not likely to produce such restlessness. While we
continue to acquire more, it is often more and more of the same (Ritzer, 2005).
When our restlessness is born from the constant pursuit of "keeping up with the
Jones," character disappears.

Here, Schweitzer's message is quite counterintuitive for character educators:
in order to foster character, we may need to tolerate and reward restlessness. So
much of formalized character education is about producing and reproducing the
right behavior, that we have lost sight of this fact. Character often requires that
someone go against the grain—for example, standing up for a friend who is
being made fun of by a group of children. In our consumer society, where the
goal is to "fit in" and conform, the occurrence of such acts becomes less likely.

In my leisure reading this summer, I re-read a book that has been
inspirational to me in my own parenting. The title of the book, Mittenstrings for
God: Reflections for Mothers in a Hurry had attracted me during my former
days as an administrator, when time seemed at a premium. Interestingly, I have
learned that time has become even harder to find as a faculty member. While the
solutions to administrative problems are constrained by various forces such as
budget and staff limitations, the solution to the problem of creating character,
the focus of my research, is open-ended. Kenison emphasizes the importance of
open-ended reflection in the lives of children:

There is a tendency in our culture to encourage children to look critically at the
world or to understand it on an intellectual level before they have had a chance to
embrace it and know it in a purely spiritual way. Wisdom begins in wonder
(186).

We need to provide our children with the time and space, the freedom to
wonder and speculate about what character means before they will come to
appreciate it. In this regard, formalized character education can be seen as a
constraint—an imposed way of thinking about character that may ultimately
limit the outcome. During our trip to Nicaragua, I saw both wisdom and wonder
in our Quinnipiac student delegation. The wonder of observing something new,

Chapter Eleven

and the sense of wisdom that a place which offered so little in a material sense,
could provide them with so much more in other ways.

Formalized character education offers us a similar opportunity. In Out of My

Life and Thought, Schweitzer (1933) reminds us that:

Great values are lost at every moment because we miss opportunities, but the
values that are turned into will and action constitute a richness that must not be
undervalued. Our humanity is by no means as materialistic as people claim so
complacently. Judging by what I have learned about men and women, I am
convinced that far more idealistic aspiration exists than is evident. Just as the
rivers we are much less numerous than underground streams, so the idealism that
is visible is minor compared to what men and women carry in their hearts,
unreleased or scarcely released. Mankind is waiting and longing for those who
can accomplish the task of untying what is knotted and bringing the underground
waters to the surface (90).

Thus, Schweitzer reminds us here that character education is really not about
teaching character, but rather about exposing it—providing opportunities for
what is already there to become evident. We need to be careful in our
classrooms, and our daily lives with children to provide opportunities for
inwardness, and to tolerate restless. In character education, we must offer our
children what consumer culture does not: opportunities for silence and
independent thinking. In teaching character, it will be necessary to defy the
norms of our consumer culture in order to combat its effects.
