CHAPTER TWELVE

NUCLEAR WEAPONS AND REVERENCE FOR LIFE

JONATHAN GRANOFF

Albert Schweitzer places our lives in clear context:

Man can hardly recognize the devils of his own creation. Let me give you a
definition of ethics: It is good to maintain and further life. It is bad to damage and
destroy life. By having reverence for life, we enter into a spiritual relation with
the world. By practicing reverence for life, we become good, deep, and alive.1

This sense of values is universally threatened by the practices of only several
states. Our sense of the unity of the human family and our obligation to this and
future generations is thus threatened as never before in human history. Dr.
Schweitzer knew this risk well and committed himself deeply to the abolition of
nuclear weapons.

thousand mounted on

There are now over 27,000 nuclear weapons in the world. Russia and the
United States possess over ninety percent of them. The US has about 10,000
nuclear weapons—some as battlefield "tactical" nukes, some in storage, and
several
ICBMs
(Intercontinental Ballistic Missiles). The Russians have over 15,000 nuclear
weapons—again, several thousand of them on fueled, hair-trigger-alert missiles.
From the moment the early-warning systems cry danger (real or cyber-glitch),
the US government allows itself less than 23 minutes before launch keys are
turned in retaliation; the Russian government allows itself less than ten. Launch
on warning continues as if the Cold War had not ended.

fueled, hair-trigger-to-launch

As Dr. Schweitzer said: Man is a clever animal who behaves like an

imbecile.

The nuclear weapons exploded in Hiroshima and Nagasaki were the
equivalent of 15,000 and 12,000 tons of TNT (trinitrotoluene), respectively.
Bombs in the megaton (equivalent to a million tons of TNT) and multiple
megaton range are now in the arsenals, some in excess of twenty megatons

Chapter Twelve

(equivalent to 20,000,000 tons of TNT). A five megaton weapon would
represent more explosive power than all the bombs used in World War II and a
twenty megaton bomb more than all the explosives used in all wars in the
history of the world.

Over time, through accident or design, human fallibility will cause the
unacceptable use of these weapons. Additionally, the possession by some states
is the strongest stimulant to others to acquire them. The willingness to use them
represents an affront to any minimum sense of the Reverence for Life.

In a stunning assertion of militarism and US unilateralism over international
cooperation and the rule of law, the Bush Administration promotes funding
steps toward the development of new nuclear weapons. Some of these weapons
are called "mini-nukes" and are ready to be integrated into conventional war
fighting plans while others are modifications of existing weapons designed for
new targets. This aspiration represents an enormous shift in the basic rationale
for the weapons.

Historically, nuclear weapons have been maintained to ensure that nuclear
weapons would not be used. If each nuclear-equipped party has enough to inflict
unacceptable damage to the other, even after being hit with a nuclear attack,
then neither would use their arsenal. Now, the US wants to develop weapons to
be used with no reference to preventing threats from nuclear weapons states. It
is a repudiation of a taboo regarding the use of unimaginably horrific devices
and an undermining of treaty commitments under the Nuclear Nonproliferation
Treaty (NPT), with 188-member States, to negotiate the elimination of nuclear
weapons.

The reality of such contemplated use of nuclear weapons has yet to
adequately dent the public debate. Like the response to the banality of evil that
made possible the Holocaust's Final Solution, today's world remains numb to
the banality of nuclear weapons.

Until now, there has been an international consensus that the proliferation of
nuclear weapons posed a paramount threat to the security of the world. President
Bush has changed this presumption. In his 2003 State of the Union address he
said, "The gravest danger facing America and the world is outlaw regimes that
seek and possess nuclear, chemical and biological weapons." The emphasis has
shifted from the weapons themselves to concern for regimes. The policies that
arise from this shift are dramatic. The focus has shifted from control and
elimination of the weapons through international law to regime changes based
on US unilateral decision making. This is very hazardous to international order.
Its consequences have been seen in the war in Iraq.

Encouraging nuclear development in India, which refuses to join the Nuclear
Nonproliferation Treaty (NPT), while denying such development to Iran
replaces setting universal legal standards with an ad hoc regime based on who is

Nuclear Weapons and Reverence for Life

seasonally friendly to the US. Iran might have a terrible government with
antiquated ideas about the role of religion and the state and deeply offensive
attitudes toward the rest of the world. But, with respect to nuclear weapons, it
promotes creating a nuclear-free zone in the Middle East, is a member of the
NPT, and publicly renounces the weapons. It has stated it is willing to allow
intrusive inspections if they are based on universal norms. We must prevent Iran
from getting nuclear weapons. But this will be difficult to achieve without a
moral foundation. The issue remains that the weapons themselves are horrific
regardless of whether the nation which has them is a friend or foe. If we return
to our legal commitments we can enforce legal duties upon others with far
greater effectiveness.

If the U.S. and Russia demonstrated active fulfillment of disarmament
commitments they would have the strongest argument for stopping any new
proliferators. Such commitment could easily be achieved by simply de-alerting
the weapons, removing the war heads from the delivery vehicles and taking
them off launch on warning. Additionally, we could make the agreements of
cuts in the arsenals, the Moscow Treaty verifiable and irreversible. We should
also legally strengthen the International Atomic Energy Agency's capacity to
inspect and negotiate a treaty banning the creation of new fissile material for
weapons purposes. These steps would give tremendous credence to efforts to
stem proliferation.

to fulfill

than working

Tragically, rather

treaty commitments for
nonproliferation and disarmament, the U.S. Nuclear Posture Review of 2002
calls for proliferation; U.S. National Security Strategy emphasizes that the
United States will take anticipatory preemptive action. Since the Nuclear
Posture Review calls for incorporating nuclear weapons into conventional war
fighting capabilities, we now have a doctrine that can rationalize a pre-emptive
use of a nuclear weapon.

It is easy to understand why the rest of the world is so afraid. North Korea's
response should come as no surprise. Iran's response should come as no
surprise. We should all be afraid of the situation.

Mohammed ElBaradei, Director of the International Atomic Energy Agency

(IAEA), and winner of the 2005 Nobel Peace Prize, stated the situation clearly:

These are double standards. On the one hand, the US says that the proliferation of
nuclear weapons must be fought. On the other, it perfects its own arsenal. This is
not acceptable… The US Administration demands from other states not to have
any nuclear weapons, while it fills its own arsenals . . . If we do not give up such
double standards, we will have even more nuclear powers. We are at a turning
point now.2

Chapter Twelve

The steady, albeit slow, progress in building peaceful relations based on the
rule of law has been stopped. Fear and uncertainty have been emphasized.
Various arguments are used to rationalize this intentional process: fighting
terrorism in the wake of 9/11, eliminating despotic rulers, demonstrating
commitments to stop the proliferation of weapons of mass destruction. But,
actions, such as instituting a war without clear U.N. Security Council
authorization, speak louder than doctrines.

The extent of the hazard has not been grasped and political arguments have
not swayed the hearts of the people nor the decision makers. Worse, the
irrationality of the quest to address the problem by building more weapons of
mass destruction remains inadequately challenged. What must we do?

The first step toward a solution is a sober acknowledgement of the problem
and a profound commitment to change. It is imperative that our goal of
obtaining the universal elimination of nuclear weapons be founded on strong
moral positions. Since fear can cloud reason, we must awaken an activism based
on an equally strong motivation. That is why spirit matters.

Dr. Schweitzer stated: "One truth stands firm. All that happens in world
history rests on something spiritual. If the spiritual is strong, it creates world
history. If it is weak, it suffers world history."

Without a strong inner capacity I do not believe one can soberly even
evaluate the seriousness of nuclear weapons. It is simply too frightening for the
everyday mind. Neither reason nor imagination is sufficient to grasp the
magnitude of what we have created. The International Court of Justice, in its
landmark opinion in 1996 on the Legality of the Threat or Use of Nuclear
Weapons stated: "The destructive power of nuclear weapons cannot be
contained in either space or time. They have the potential to destroy all
civilization and the entire eco-system of the planet." The Court unanimously
concluded that there is a legal duty to negotiate the global elimination of nuclear
weapons.

Nuclear weapons exemplify a thoroughly modern dilemma where the means
of pursuing security undermine the end of obtaining security. As Thoreau said,
"Improved means to unimproved ends."

The Mayor of Nagasaki pleads with us to understand the human dimensions

of one relatively small atomic bomb:

The explosion of the atomic bomb generated an enormous fireball, 200 meters in
radius, almost as though a small sun had appeared in the sky. The next instant, a
ferocious blast and wave of heat assailed the ground with a thunderous roar. The
surface temperature of the fireball was about 7,000 degrees C, and the heat rays
that reached the ground were over 3,000 degrees C. The explosion instantly
killed or injured people within a two-kilometer radius of the hypocenter, leaving
innumerable corpses charred like clumps of charcoal and scattered in the ruins

Nuclear Weapons and Reverence for Life

near the hypocenter. In some cases, not even a trace of the person's remains
could be found. A wind (over 680 miles per hour) slapped down trees and
demolished most buildings. Even iron-reinforced concrete structures were so
badly damaged that they seemed to have been smashed by a giant hammer. The
fierce flash of heat meanwhile melted glass and left metal objects contorted like
strands of taffy, and the subsequent fires burned the ruins of the city to ashes.
Nagasaki became a city of death where not even the sound of insects could be
heard.

After a while, countless men, women and children began to gather for a drink
of water at the banks of the nearby Urakami River, their hair and clothing
scorched and their burnt skin hanging off in sheets like rags. Begging for help,
they died one after another in the water or in heaps on the banks. Then radiation
began to take its toll, killing people like a scourge of death expanding in
concentric circles from the hypocenter. Four months after the atomic bombing,
74,000 people were dead and 75,000 had suffered injuries, that is, two thirds of
the city population had fallen victim to this calamity that came upon Nagasaki
like a preview of the Apocalypse.3

When Mahatma Gandhi heard of this horror he pondered: "What has

happened to the soul of the destroying nation is yet too early to see."

The psychologist Robert Jay Lifton has described part of the cost to the
small handful of nuclear weapons states as "a collective form of psychic
numbing." Our readiness to unleash, in short order, devices that will rapidly
transform cities
into concentration camp-like ovens numbs our moral
sensitivities. These weapons are ovens with wings. Even George Kennan, the
distinguished American diplomat who originated the Cold War containment
policy toward the Soviet Union, not associated with moral admonitions, warns
us:

The readiness to use nuclear weapons against other human beings—against
people we do not know, whom we have never seen, and whose guilt or innocence
is not for us to establish—and, in doing so, to place in jeopardy the natural
structure upon which all civilization rests, as though the safety and perceived
interests of our own generation were more important than everything that has
taken place or could take place in civilization: this is nothing less than a
presumption, a blasphemy, an
indignity of monstrous
dimensions—offered to God!4

indignity—an

This expression of human arrogance hides a fundamental weakness, a failure
of respect for the power of love, God's greatest gift to us. That power is denied
by this threatening violence. This ultimate violence is idolatry without
boundary, exalting human ideas and force above the creator's gift and the very
life of the creation.

Chapter Twelve

The violence of threatening to unleash the Apocalypse represents an
immorality of vast proportion and calls us to an affirmation of faith of vaster
proportion. We cannot be passive in pursuing this capacity. Dr. Martin Luther
King's words burn through the haze of today's daily news reports:

The fact that most of the time human beings put the truth about the nature and
risks of nuclear war out of their minds because it is too painful and therefore not
'acceptable,' does not alter the nature and risks of such war... (i)f modern man
continues to flirt unhesitatingly with war, he will transform his earthly habitat
into an inferno such that even the mind of Dante could not imagine…I do not
wish to minimize the complexity of the problems that need to be faced in
achieving disarmament and peace. But I think it is a fact that we shall not have
the will, the courage, and insight to deal with such matters unless in this field we
are prepared to undergo a mental and spiritual reevaluation – a change of focus
which will enable us to see that the things which seem most real and powerful are
indeed now unreal and have come under the sentence of death…It is not enough
to say "We must not wage war.' It is necessary to love peace and sacrifice for
it…we have inherited a big house, a great 'world house' in which we have to live
together – black and white. Easterner and Westerners, Gentiles and Jews,
Catholics and Protestants, Moslems and Hindu, a family unduly separated in
ideas, culture, and interests who, because we can never again live without each
other, must learn somehow, in this one big world, to live with each other… This
means that more and more our loyalties must become ecumenical rather than
sectional. We must now give an overriding loyalty to mankind as a whole in
order to preserve the best in our individual societies. This call for a worldwide
fellowship that lifts neighborly concern beyond one's tribe, race, class, and
nation is in reality a call for an all embracing and unconditional love for all."5

Developing this love is not only a personal path to salvation but a necessity
for human fulfillment and survival. As King said, "When I speak of love I am
not speaking of some sentimental and weak response which is little more than
emotional bosh. I am speaking of that force which all the great religions have
seen as the supreme unifying principle of life. Love is somehow the key that
unlocks the door which leads to ultimate reality."6 Our choice is Dante's hell or
a door to ultimate reality.

The mystery that placed the power of destruction in the binding forces of the
atom has placed the healing power of love in our hearts and further gifted us
with both the courage and wisdom to use that power effectively. King's stand is
correct: "I refuse to accept the cynical notion that nation after nation must spiral
down a militaristic stairway into the hell of nuclear annihilation."7 With faith
that we can be guided by and have confidence in our love of life, should we not
commit to cause our country to disavow its unlawful, immoral policy of failing
to negotiate the elimination of nuclear weapons. Will you join this call from the
conscience of humanity?

Nuclear Weapons and Reverence for Life

We have the choice to respond to this call of conscience. With the help of
each, which is a gift in our hands to choose, and the help of God, which is a gift
surely granted, we can and will become the change we want to see. We can
change our government's conduct to reflect our human values. The policies that
diminish the threat to use nuclear weapons and lead to their elimination have
actually already been painstakingly negotiated in the NPT. They include a
Comprehensive Test Ban Treaty, deep cuts in the arsenals, reduction of the
operational status of the weapons, diminished role of the weapons in policy
planning, and negotiations leading to elimination. We don't need to reinvent a
treaty.

The commitments under the NPT are an excellent foundation, but they are
certainly not enough. For example, the entire budget for all inspections under
the IAEA is less than $90 million per year for the whole world, while the US
alone spends more than $97 million per day on our nuclear arsenal. The need for
changing economic allocations is obvious.

Compared to what might arise in the future, addressing nuclear weapons is
now relatively manageable. Only three countries—Israel, Pakistan and India –
are not in the NPT, and only five countries are nuclear weapons States under the
treaty—U.S., U.K., China, France, and Russia. Additionally, nuclear materials
leave a footprint that can be found and because everyone is afraid of terrorists
the public could be mobilized to support steps toward elimination. If we do not
move quickly to curtail this capacity for self destruction that science has given
humanity with this obviously hazardous technology, how do we intend to
constrain and control the next generation of weapons of mass destruction? It is
time that this technology be subject to law and morality. Nothing less than our
humanity and future is at stake.

We need to further awaken the public and generate the political will to fulfill
our existing commitments. We need to compel our political leaders to take clear
morally coherent positions and commit to work for abolition. At a minimum, we
must demand that every candidate for national office describe their aspirations
and plans to reduce and eliminate the possession and threat to use nuclear
weapons in every other country in the world.

level of cooperation

And we cannot ignore that there are numerous threats to humanity which
require a new
to address effectively. This new
understanding must comprehend the world as a living whole where every life is
understood to be part of the web. Dr. Schweitzer stated: "Until he extends his
circle of compassion to include all living things, man will not himself find
peace." This spiritual admonition now converges with the practical necessity of
working in a new spirit of global cooperation. This is the first time in human
history where the spiritual guidance of the wise fully reinforces the practical
analysis of the worldly realists.

Chapter Twelve

As long as several countries claim a right to excesses in weapons and the
right to threaten everyone else with annihilation, the necessary cooperation for a
sustainable future will not be obtained. Nuclear weapons threaten all life and for
that reason they are an excellent place to begin working together. Working to
abolish this unacceptable risk enhances our common humanity, our sense of
solidarity with others, and thus builds the framework for addressing so many
threats whether relating to religious differences, environmental destruction,
poverty, or human rights.

Realizing that our destiny is common or not at all is new. We are the first
generation that must decide whether to be ancestors or not. Let us revere the
mystery that placed us here to learn. Let our work exemplify our Reverence for
Life. As Dr. Schweitzer stated: "I don't know what your destiny will be, but one
thing I know: the only ones among you who will be really happy are those who
will have sought and found how to serve."

Let us be amongst those who know the secret of service. Life's blessings

abound upon those who serve life's blessings. May we be so blessed.

Nuclear Weapons and Reverence for Life

Appendix

Below are several issues that require a new level of global cooperation. This sense of
human solidarity informed Dr. Schweitzer's spiritual comprehension; it now informs our
most practical.

Poverty
*In 1999, some 2.8 billion people—two in every five humans on the planet—lived on
less than $2 a day.8
*There are 1.1 billion people in the world living on less than one dollar per day (PPP
US$).9
*In 2000, one in five people in the developing world—1.1 billion total—did not have
"reasonable access" to safe drinking water.10
*2.4 billion people worldwide—two out of every five—live without basic sanitation.11
*In 46 countries people are poorer today than in 1990.12
*11 million children under the age of 5 die every year.13
*The global economy has grown sevenfold since 1950. Meanwhile, the disparity in per
capita gross domestic product between the 20 richest and 20 poorest nations more than
doubled between 1960 and 1995. Of all high-income nations, the United States has the
most unequal distribution of income, with over 30 percent of income in the hands of the
richest 10 percent and only 1.8 percent going to the poorest 10 percent. 14
*104 million children are not in school, 59 million of them girls.15
*44 million primary-aged children in sub-Saharan Africa are not in school, 23 million are
girls. 16
*At current rates of progress, Sub-Saharan Africa would not achieve universal primary
education until 2129 – another 125 years.17
*In 25 countries more people go to bed hungry today than a decade ago.18
*There are 831 million people who go to bed hungry across the world today.19
*Rich countries' aid budgets are half what they were in 1960 and poor countries are
paying back a staggering $100 million a day in debt repayments. 20
*Oxfam calculates that 97 million more children will be out of school by 2015 unless
urgent action is taken. 21

Religion & Terrorism
*In 2003, one in five (13 of 65) groups who engaged in terrorism could be identified as
seeking religious domination or ethnic cleansing.22
*Over 3,000 people died in the 9/11 attacks on the United States. This loss of human
lives is tragic. When evaluating the threat posed by terrorism, however, one must keep in
mind that more than 1.2 million people are killed on the road annually (more than 3,000
people per day). Millions more are injured or disabled. In some low and middle-income
countries, road crash victims occupy up to 10% of hospital beds. Deaths from all types of
injuries are projected to rise from 5.1 million in 1990 to 8.4 million in 2020 - with road
traffic injuries as a major cause for this increase. 23
*Globally, estimates suggest that the economic costs of road traffic injuries amount to
US$518 billion per year. In developing countries, the costs are estimated to be US$100
billion, twice the annual amount of development assistance to developing countries.24

Chapter Twelve

Military Spending
*World military expenditures in 2001 were conservatively estimated at $839 billion—
almost $100 million every hour or $2.3 billion each day. 25
*The United States is now the world's sole military colossus, accounting for 36 percent
of all military spending, or $302 billion. U.S. spending is now projected to rise to $414
billion (in 2001 dollars) by 2009. 26
* Every hour, the world spends more than $100 million on soldiers, weapons, and
ammunition.27
*Expenditures for United Nations peacekeeping operations from July 2002 to July 2003
equaled roughly $2.6 billion. As of late December 2002, U.N. members still owed the
organization $1.34 billion for peacekeeping operations. The United States accounted for
40 percent of unpaid dues, or $536 million.28
*High-income countries, home to only 16 percent of the world's people, account for
$662 billion, or 75 percent, of global military expenditures. 29
*Military budgets of high-income countries are roughly 10 times larger than their
combined development assistance. 30
*Traditional military deployments abroad dwarf peacekeeping efforts. Some 530,000
soldiers (70 percent of them from the U.S.) in military operations overshadow the
125,000 peacekeepers worldwide. 31
*James Wolfensohn, President of the World Bank said on May 23, 2004 at his
commencement address at Brandeis University: "You young people should understand
that the way the world is spending its money today. We are spending $900 billion a year
on military expenditures, $350 billion a year on agricultural subsidies, which affect
developing countries adversely, and maybe $50 to $60 billion a year on development.
We should be spending $900 billion on development, and then we would only need $50
billion on military expenditures."32
*Of the roughly $148 billion America spends a year on new weapons, almost 20% is
hidden from public view, in the classified "black budget."33
*Research and development spending on new weapons in America rose 77% between
2000-2005, to $69 billion / yr. The price of buying new weapons is scheduled to rise
nearly 50%, to almost $118 billion annually, in 2011, from $78 billion today. 34

Resource Wars
*Resource-related conflicts during the 1990s killed more than 5 million people and
displaced 17 to 21 million.
*Roughly one-quarter of the world's 50 wars and armed conflicts of recent years have
involved a struggle for control of natural resources like gemstones, timber, and oil—all
of which are highly valued by wealthy consumers in the developed world. Virtually all of
these conflicts have occurred in poor countries where a particular ethnic group or
economic elite has gained control of resources at the expense of the poor majority.35
* The U.N. Environment Programme projects 50 million environmental refugees
worldwide by 2010. 36

Nuclear Weapons and Reverence for Life

Death by Preventable Causes (For Perspective)
*Almost 11 million children die every year from preventable and treatable causes,
according to the World Health Organization (WHO) and the United Nations Children's
Fund (UNICEF).37
*A report from international agency Oxfam today reveals that 45 million more children
will die needlessly by 2015, because rich countries are failing to provide the necessary
resources they promised to overcome poverty. 38
*Chronic hunger plagues 852 million people worldwide. Hunger and malnutrition cause
tremendous human suffering, kill more than five million children every year, and cost
developing countries billions of dollars in lost productivity and national income.39
*More than 20 million low birth-weight babies are born in the developing world every
year. These babies faced increased risk of dying in infancy, while those who survive
often suffer lifelong physical and cognitive disabilities.40
*The report says that without the direct costs of dealing with the damage caused by
hunger, more funds would be available to combat other social problems. "A very rough
estimate suggests that these direct costs add up to around $30 billion per year - over five
times the amount committed so far to the Global Fund to Fight AIDS, Tuberculosis and
Malaria." 41
*Tolerating the current levels of child malnutrition will result in productivity and income
losses over their lifetimes of between $500 billion to $1 trillion at present value. 42
*The resources needed to deal with the problem of hunger are small in comparison to the
potential benefits. Every dollar invested in reducing hunger can yield from five, to over
20 times as much in benefits. 43
* Between 2001 and 2020, some 590 thousand people a year in China are projected to
suffer premature deaths due to urban air pollution—nearly one third of the projected
world total.44
* Programs to provide clean water and sewage systems would cost roughly $37 billion
annually. 45
*Programs to provide immunization for every child in the developing world would cost
roughly $3 billion annually. 46
* A 2000 World Bank study projected that on average 1.8 million people would die
prematurely each year between 2001 and 2020 because of air pollution. 47

HIV / AIDS Epidemic
*The number of people living with HIV/AIDS rose to 42 million at the end of 2002. Five
million people became infected with HIV in 2002, and another 3.1 million died of AIDSrelated causes.
*For the first time, women account for half the people living with HIV/AIDS.
Heterosexual transmission, particularly in Africa and the Caribbean, is the primary cause
of infection among women, who are two to four times more likely than men to become
infected during unprotected vaginal sex.48
* The number of people living with HIV in East Asia jumped nearly 50 percent between
2002 and 2004, to 1.1 million.49
* Without adequate prevention programs, as many as 650,000 Russians could be dying
from HIV/AIDS annually by 2010—more people than have died of AIDS in the U.S.
since 1981. 50

Chapter Twelve

Over-Fishing & Species Extinction
*Nearly 75% of the world's fisheries are classified as overexploited, depleted or in a state
of collapse:

Fishery Statistics: State of stocks in 1999 51

*China alone harvested 46 million tons of fish in 2002, more than one third of the global
total.52
*The world's fishers harvested a record 133 million tons of fish and shellfish from
streams, oceans, and other water bodies in 2002—nearly seven times the global harvest
in 1950.53
* Nearly one in four mammal species is in serious decline, mainly due to human
activities. 54

Global Warming
*Since the late 1970s, an ozone hole has formed over Antarctica each austral spring
(September / October), in which up to 66 percent of the total ozone is depleted.55
*Scientists have linked the warming trend that accelerated in the twentieth century to the
buildup of carbon dioxide and other heat-trapping gasses. By burning fossil fuels, people
released some 6.44 billion tons of carbon into the atmosphere in 2002. With less than
five percent of the world's population, the United States is the single largest source of
carbon from fossil fuels—emitting 24 percent of the world's total.56
*With less than five percent of the world's population, the United States is responsible
for a large share of the world's fossil fuel burden, accounting for 26 percent of global oil
use, 25 percent of coal consumption, and 27 percent of natural gas use. U.S.
automobiles—totaling more than 128 million, or one quarter of the world's cars—emit
roughly as much carbon as the entire Japanese economy, the world's fourth largest
carbon emitter.57
*The United States produces almost 40% of the world's ozone layer-depleting
chemicals.58
*23 percent of the world's Carbon Dioxide Emissions come from the United States
alone. Per capita that's 19.8 metric tons.59

Nuclear Weapons and Reverence for Life

* Carbon emissions from U.S. motor gasoline use in 2002—at 1,139 million tons—
surpassed those of the entire Japanese economy. 60
*The average carbon dioxide concentration has increased more than 19 percent since
measurements began in Hawaii in 1959—and has gone up 35 percent since the dawn of
the industrial age. 61
*By one estimate, the world's glaciers lose at least 90 cubic kilometers of ice annually—
as much water as all U.S. homes, factories, and farms use every four months. Scientists
suspect that the enhanced melting is related to the unprecedented release of greenhouse
gases by humans during the past century. 62
*Desertification puts some 135 million people worldwide at risk of being driven from
their lands. As climate change translates into more intense storms, flooding, heat waves,
and droughts, more and more communities will likely be affected. 63

Deforestation
*Overall rates of flora and fauna extinction are now estimated to be between 1,000 and
10,000 times greater than in the recent past.64
*Tropical forests are being destroyed at the rate of 0.8% to 2.0% per year.65
*Rainforests cover 6 % of all the earth's land but contain 40% of its plant and animal
species.66
* Global forest cover stands at approximately half the original extent of 8,000 years
ago.67

Resource Consumption
*China increased its oil consumption by 11 percent in 2004, cementing its position as the
world's number two user (after the U.S.) at 6.6 million barrels per day.68
*World oil consumption surged by 3.4 percent in 2004, the fastest rate of increase in 16
years. 69
*Production is falling in 33 of the 48 largest oil-producing countries, including 6 of 11
OPEC members. 70
*In the continental U.S., oil production peaked at 8 million barrels per day in 1970 and
fell to just 2.9 million barrels a day in 2004. 71

CHAPTER THIRTEEN

CONTROLLING THE ARMS TRADE

LUIS ALBERTO CORDERO

I think my favorite thing Albert Schweitzer ever said is, "Do something

wonderful, people may imitate it."

When Dr. Oscar Arias, upon becoming president of Costa Rica in 1986,
began to insist upon disarmament, free elections and aid to refugees in Central
America, critics said the region's other presidents would never come around,
that decade-old civil wars would continue to rage.

But sure enough, other Central American leaders soon began to imitate Dr.
Arias's stance. They signed the Esquipulas II Accords in Guatemala on August
7, 1987, and through the process outlined in the accords, every civil war in the
region was brought to an end. Dr. Arias won the Noble Peace Prize for his
efforts.

What troubled Dr. Arias so much in 1987 and what is still so troubling in
individuals and

the wonderful work benevolent

2005,
organizations do, militaries seem to undo so much of it.

that for all

is

World military expenditures and the value of the arms trade in 2004 were
estimated to be over $1 trillion dollars. But this article will not go into detail
about how only $7 billion dollars per year could provide clean drinking water to
the 2.6 billion people on the planet who do not have it, or how only $5 billion
dollars per year spent on basic medicines could save 6 million children from
dying in the developing world.1

Although many nations have twisted spending priorities, what I would like

to focus on is the importance of controlling the international arms trade.

The total of arms sales and transfer agreements to industrialized and
developing nations in 2004 was nearly $37 billion dollars, with an increase in
budget spending that is the highest level since 2000.2 The United States still
dominates the market, while Britain, France, Germany, and Italy significantly
increased their collective share of arms sales with developing nations to $4.8
billion dollars between 2003 and 2004.3

Chapter Thirteen

The comment Oscar Arias made about U.S. and Russian arms sales to
Central America during the 1970s and 80s still describes the relationship
between developed and developing nations today. In the former president's
chilling words, "they provide the weapons, we provide the dead."

In 2004, Russia sold over half a billion dollars in arms to Sudan and 1.6
billion dollars worth of arms to Iran. France sent 125 million dollars worth of
conventional weapons to Indonesia, a country on which the U.S. has placed
restrictions on arms transfers because of human rights abuses in East Timor.4

We all know what happens when weapons get into the hands of shady
buyers: ethnic conflict, murder, genocide, a cycle of underdevelopment and
poverty, violence and insecurity. Conventional arms kill hundreds of thousands
of people every year, and leave about 1.5 million disabled. They have been
responsible for the death of over 2 million children since 1990. And they are the
driving force behind the astronomical number of refugees in the world right
now, which is about 35 million.5

You would think that with a trade this dangerous, the world would make
regulating it a top priority. After all, since the end of World War II the
international community has developed a number of binding agreements
concerning human rights, humanitarian law, and peaceful co-existence.

Almost every country in the world has signed the Geneva Conventions of
1949, a code of conduct for the protection of wounded soldiers, prisoners of
war, and civilians. But the conventions did not include provisions to govern
arms transfers.

The Uruguay Round of trade negotiations that led to the formal creation of
the World Trade Organization in 1995 resulted in over 60 agreements. And not
one of them regulated the arms trade.

There are a host of international conventions regulating commerce over the
oceans. And yet the closest thing to a conventional weapon whose transfer is
regulated by international law when it crosses the high seas is the swordfish.

Why have we been smart enough to create a convention regulating the
hunting of whales but not one regulating the transfer of weapons that are used to
hunt people?

The time for action has come.
In 1997, led by Dr. Arias, eight Nobel laureates came together in New York
City and launched their International Code of Conduct on Arms Transfers.
(They thought to themselves, "Do something wonderful, people may imitate
it."). The idea was simple: encourage countries not to transfer weapons if there
is reason to believe the weapons will be used to violate human rights or
international law.

Working with a group of NGOs, including the Arias Foundation, legal
scholars at Cambridge University in England transformed the Code of Conduct

The Arms Trade

into a draft comprehensive Arms Trade Treaty, or ATT. The ATT forbids the
transfer of arms for atrocities, genocide, or crimes against humanity. It forbids
the transfer of arms for violations of human rights or humanitarian law. It
forbids the transfer of arms if there is clear indication that they will be used to
disrupt sustainable development.

Aside from the ATT, the United Nations is developing something called the
"Programme of Action to Prevent, Combat and Eradicate the Illicit Traffic of
Small Arms and Light Weapons in All Its Aspects," usually just called the
Programme of Action or PoA. It is a forum for discussion and identification of
action in the UN.

Because it only deals with small arms and light weapons, the PoA departs
from the original call of the Nobel laureates to regulate the international trade of
all conventional arms. While the goals of the PoA are noble, helicopter gunships
and Humvees can be just as deadly as pistols and rifles. As much as the PoA is a
valuable forum, the problem of the global arms trade demands a broader
solution.

The Arias Foundation will continue to support a comprehensive, legally
binding ATT for all conventional arms, large and small, both through the PoA
and beyond its scope.

So who doesn't support the ATT?
First of all, defense contractors. Sadly the only bottom line they seem to see

is measured in dollars, not bodies.

Then there are the special interest groups. The National Rifle Association
has offered knee-jerk opposition to the treaty. I say "knee-jerk" because if you
look at the treaty, components of it are actually compatible with the
organization's mission. According to its website, the NRA seeks to promote
"safe, responsible ownership and use of firearms." This is completely in line
with the ATT. It is the unsafe, irresponsible ownership of firearms that this
treaty was designed to prevent.

Then there are the governments who are not in favor. One might think these
would only include places like Sudan, Indonesia and the Congo, countries
whose supply of arms would be cut off because of human rights abuses.
Unfortunately they are not alone. Joining Sudan and the Congo on the list of
nations who have not supported the ATT is the United States.

Ironically, the U.S. actually has national legislation on the control of
conventional weapons that is even more restrictive than the ATT. The Gun
Control Act of 1968, the National Firearms Act, and the Arms Export Control
Act are all far more demanding than the ATT.

Then why hasn't the U.S. signed the ATT? One simple, tragic answer is
money. Defense contractors in the U.S. sold about $8 billion dollars in

Chapter Thirteen

conventional weapons last year, and the U.S. government feels the need to
protect American business.6

But money is not the only motivator. A lot of it has to do with attitude.
There is the shortsighted attitude that going it alone makes more sense for
the United States than working together with other nations. From shunning the
Kyoto Protocol on the environment to sniping at the International Criminal
Court to withdrawing from the Anti-Ballistic Missile Treaty with Russia, it
often seems that the only way this current administration will engage the world
is with military force. Calling the Bush Administration's policies "unilateral"
makes them sound more intelligent than calling them what they are: "uncooperative."

But most dangerous of all, there is the attitude among policy makers that

weapons are necessary to security rather than poisonous to it.

This was the attitude that led the United States to give weapons to Saddam
Hussein during the Iran-Iraq war in the 1980s. This was the attitude that led the
U.S. to send munitions to Osama Bin Laden to fight the Soviet Union in
Afghanistan.

Even in the face of this gruesome and embarrassing history, if a government
is convinced that legitimate security interests require sending arms abroad, so be
it. But make those transfers responsible. Make the parties accountable. Let's
make sure the war on terrorism does not mean helping terrible people make war.
Fortunately, even in the face of this opposition, international support for the
ATT is growing. Along with respected international organizations such as the
Arias Foundation, the Schweitzer Institute, Amnesty International, Oxfam and
IANSA, among others, as of July 2005 over thirty governments have also
expressed their support for the ATT, including Costa Rica, Mali, Cambodia,
Finland, Kenya and the United Kingdom.

British foreign secretary Jack Straw made a powerful appeal for the ATT on
the floor of the UN after the July bombings in London that killed 52 people. He
remarked: "the threat from terrorists and the political instability they bring is
made worse by the easy availability of weapons in what has become an
anarchic, unregulated trade."

Leading experts around the world agree. Mohamed ElBaradei, the Director
General of the International Atomic Energy Agency and the winner of the 2005
Nobel Peace Prize, recently called the existing regimen for controlling arms
exports "completely busted." The solution, in his words, is this: "universalize
the export control system, remove loopholes, and enact binding, treaty-based
controls."7

The ATT may not be the answer to ending the world's conflicts, but it's a
step in the right direction. And it needs the support not just of politicians but,
perhaps even more importantly, of ordinary citizens as well.

The Arms Trade

If you are interested in getting informed about this issue, please go to the
Arms Trade Treaty website. The address is www.armstradetreaty.org. It has
critical information about the ATT, and if you find yourself convinced, follow
the link to the online petition. Petitions are so important. Because nothing drives
leaders to action like seeing that their people have taken action themselves. If so
many people do something wonderful, who knows. Their governments may just
imitate it.

CHAPTER FOURTEEN

SCHWEITZER'S LEGACY IN PUBLIC HEALTH

DENNIS J. RICHARDSON

Dr. Albert Schweitzer was one of the most important figures of the 20th
He was an eminent musician, musical historian, theologian,
century.
philosopher and physician. As we gather to celebrate the life of Dr. Schweitzer,
my task is to examine and analyze his legacy in the field of public health,
particularly tropical medicine. Within the arena of medicine, Schweitzer was
best known for his role as "jungle physician" and founder of his famous hospital
at Lambaréné on the Ogowe1 River in western Gabon, a facility that operates to
this day. The hospital has improved the lives of hundreds of thousands of
Africans. For example, between 1924 and 1966, over 137,000 patients received
treatment at the hospital and 3,805 babies were delivered.2 Schweitzer devoted
his life to medical service in response to a call for "the direct service of
humanity."3 This was an expression of his philosophical approach to
Christianity, which embodied the belief that "knowledge of the great truths only
appears in action and labor."4

Schweitzer's choice of equatorial Africa as an arena for his direct service to
humanity could not have been more fortuitous. Now as then, nowhere on Earth
can greater need and the burden of human misery be found. Diseases such as
malaria, typhoid, African trypanosomiasis, filariasis, schistosomiasis, cholera,
and now AIDS, just to name a few, are all too common. Schweitzer literally
engaged in single-minded warfare against these scourges, putting his music,
writing, scholarship, family life, and personal fortune second to the direct
service of humanity. Schweitzer was not above getting his hands dirty, and he
did so every day.

He recounts one instance in which he and his co-workers were hauling
lumber at the hospital, when he called out to a native visiting a patient. "Hullo!
Friend, won't you lend us a hand?" "I am an intellectual and don't drag wood
about," came the answer. "You're lucky,"" Schweitzer replied, "I too wanted to
become an intellectual, but I didn't succeed."5

Schweitzer and Public Health

It is easy and tempting to merely engage in a rhetorical diatribe of
overwhelming adulation concerning the life and work of Albert Schweitzer. But
in order to appreciate the full implications of Schweitzer's legacy, we must also
take a critical look at his perceived shortcomings. Some of his shortcomings
may have actually been among his greatest attributes when considered from a
public health perspective. Schweitzer was criticized for being arrogant, shorttempered and stubborn. His hospital was often characterized as being primitive
and unsanitary, even by the standards of the day. In defense of these criticisms,
Schweitzer's greatest accomplishment was not in building a hospital but rather
in creating an African village centered on a functional and successful clinic.6
Having a hospital conform to Western standards was not his goal. In order for
any public health initiative to succeed in developing areas, respect from and
acceptance by the local population is essential. Despite what outsiders might say
or perceive about his relationship to his patients, Schweitzer earned the undying
and devoted respect of the African people whom he served. As he pointed out,
"We . . . are really so overwhelmed with work that the humanity within us
cannot come out properly . . . For the present we are condemned to the trying
task of carrying on the struggle with sickness and pain, and to that everything
else has to give way."7

Schweitzer contended that he went to work in Lambaréné because he wanted
his life to be his argument. In doing so, he has left us with two enduring
legacies. First, Schweitzer gave us an example of how to work within a
community in such a way as to integrate local customs and beliefs with a spirit
of mutual respect, cooperation, and dignity. Second, Dr. Schweitzer has given
us a decisive call to action that is philosophically grounded in a fundamental
moral imperative. He put forth his life as his argument and this argument stands
the test of time. Dr. Schweitzer's religion and philosophy were tenants of action.
As the great natural historian and philosopher Thomas Henry Huxley once said,
"philosophy bakes no bread." It is only through philosophy and theology in
action that human misery is relieved, and Albert Schweitzer gave us this gift of
action. He was real. As Albert Einstein said of Schweitzer, "He simply acted out
of inner necessity."8

Schweitzer said, "We should love each other but one should never stand in
awe of another human being. . . . one does a disservice to philosophy in general
and certain philosophers in particular if one has nothing to offer them except
praise."9 The greatest way for us to celebrate the rich life and legacy of Dr.
Schweitzer is for us to follow his example and carry his dream into a new
generation through action in our own lives.

The need in equatorial Africa is as great as ever. In Africa, 1.7 million
children die of malaria each year, roughly one child every 20 seconds.10 The
prevalence of HIV infection exceeds 10% in some areas. Throughout equatorial

Chapter Fourteen

Africa, abject poverty provides the breeding ground for hunger, disease, and the
human suffering that accompany such conditions.

Dr. Schweitzer pointed out that humanity owes Africa a debt in repayment
for centuries of abuse and exploitation. "That many among those who took
possession of colonial territories committed injustice, violence, and cruelty is
only too true, and this puts a heavy burden of responsibility on us. And still
today the harm we inflict on the Africans should not be passed over in silence or
concealed…medical people must do for the suffering in far-off lands what cries
out to be done in the name of true civilization…I must insist that whatever
benefit we confer upon the peoples of our colonies is not charity, but atonement
for the terrible sufferings we white people have inflicted upon them ever since
the day our first ship found its way to their shores."11

The need in Africa remains nearly incomprehensible. Currently, my
organization, the Bawa Health Initiative, is undertaking an effort in partnership
with the Albert Schweitzer Institute at Quinnipiac University to bring modest
public health interventions to Bawa, a remote village in the West province of
Cameroon. There is no electricity in the village, no sanitation system, no clean
drinking water. Children die frequently as result of malaria, typhoid,
malnutrition and worm infection, just as they did in Schweitzer's time.

Unfortunately, there is nothing unique about Bawa, a village of 350 people.
It represents thousands of other villages containing hundreds of millions of
people throughout equatorial Africa. This village is located in volcanic
mountains. It is so easy to stand on one of those mountains and envision the
ocean of human suffering and misery extending for miles and miles across the
deceptively tranquil but unrelentingly harsh African countryside.

It is easy to see that the tremendous need that drew Albert Schweitzer to
Lambaréné exists today. It was undoubtedly a Reverence for Life that drew
Schweitzer to Africa and to take on seemingly insurmountable odds. It is my
hope that this conference celebrating Dr. Schweitzer's life will help to
perpetuate a Reverence for Life that so defined his being, philosophy, and
actions. Such a Reverence for Life seems to be sorely lacking in highly
industrialized nations today, in which 20 percent of the Earth's population
consumes 80% of the Earth's resources, much of which comes from developing
countries.12 On April 30, 1958, in an appeal broadcast from Oslo, Norway,
Schweitzer stated "In President Eisenhower's speech of November 7, 1957, a
few days after the launching of Sputnik II, he said, 'What the world needs more
than a gigantic leap into space is a gigantic leap into peace.' This gigantic leap
consists in finding the courage to hope the spirit of good sense will arise in
individuals and in peoples, a spirit sufficiently strong to overcome the insanity
and the inhumanity."13

Schweitzer and Public Health

The world continues to wait. When we finally embrace Schweitzer's
philosophy of Reverence for Life, maybe we will lay down our arms and begin
to build instead of destroy; to act generously instead of greedily; to give instead
of take. Dr. Schweitzer's legacy calls for redistribution of resources being used
for arms to health and development.14 In September 2000, the United Nations
held a Millennium Summit in which leaders of 180 nations set ambitious goals
toward promoting world health by 2015 including reducing global childhood
mortality by 66%, reversing the spread of AIDS, and cutting the number of
people who do not have access to food and clean drinking water by one half.15 It
was subsequently determined that if the wealthy nations of the world would
relegate 1/10th of 1% (one penny for every $10.00) of their gross national
product toward supporting health services to the poor, 21,000 lives could be
saved each day.16 It is estimated that an annual expenditure of 19 billion dollars
will cut global poverty in half by 2015.17 In 2004, 140 billion dollars were spent
on the Iraq war and occupation and additional 165 billion dollars went to
military contractors. Our total annual military spending stands at 419 billion
dollars.18 In 2002, President George W. Bush pledged that the contribution of
the United States toward the United Nations' Millennium Development Goals
would reach 5 billion dollars by 2006. President Bush is currently asking
Congress for 3 billion to be directed toward this end in the 2006 budget.19

It is all too apparent that we cannot wait for anyone to take on these
challenges on our behalf, especially our governments. It is up to each individual
to embrace a Reverence for Life and to assume the evident moral imperative—
and Albert Schweitzer has shown us the way. Let us honor him by taking direct
action to fight these scourges on humanity. Let us follow his lead and let our life
be our argument. In doing so, we will truly celebrate and honor the life of this
great man.

CHAPTER FIFTEEN

THE LEGACY OF ALBERT SCHWEITZER'S VIRTUE
ETHICS TO A MORAL PHILOSOPHY OF MEDICINE

JAMES GIORDANO AND JULIA PEDRONI

Introduction: What is Schweitzer's legacy?

What is a legacy? By definition it is both something that is inherited and that
which is willed.1 Within this definition lie two exegetic distinctions: the first
implies an inherent passivity, a simple passing along, while the second describes
a determinate and intentional act of agency. Inherent to both of these definitions
is that the legacy affords or provides something that is taken into the future, and
has meaning, value and perhaps effect. It is from this notion of passivity or
activity that we examine the legacy to medicine of Albert Schweitzer's life and
work.2

The contemporary practice of medicine has evolved as a consequence of
cultural change, influenced by diverse forces including the interaction of its
fundamental epistemology with philosophy, scientific discovery and sociopolitical thought. Within this context we must ask whether Schweitzer's legacy
is radically antiquarian, and is an ideal that while quaint, represents an
unrealistic impracticality in light of the current culture of medicine. In this essay
we oppugn this proposition and argue that Schweitzer's work can and should be
valued as contributory to the moral practice of medicine. Specifically we argue
that: 1) medicine, in its essence, possesses an internal morality and therefore the
clinician is both therapeutic and moral agent; 2) modern medicine has moved
from its telos (i.e.- a right and good healing; Pellegrino, Thomasma, 1981)
toward increasing technocentricism, commoditization and moral hebetude; 3)
there is a demonstrable need to re-vivify the essence of medicine, as an act of a
unique inter-personal relationship; 4) Schweitzer's work—embracing medicine
qua humanitarian act—is based upon a conviction to life as a foundational good,
and supports the moral basis of the inter-personal relationship of medicine
through infused virtues (faith, love and charity); and 5) we may empower

Chapter Fifteen

Schweitzer's ethical legacy to affect a change in medical culture through the
inclusion of other moral and intellectual virtue(s) to re-establish character as the
basis of telo-focal, moral agency in medicine. We will present counter
arguments to our thesis and offer refutation to these antitheses, and thus seek to
frame Schweitzer's work within a larger context of moral philosophy of
medicine.

The "essence" of medicine3

Medicine, in the classical definition, is the art and science of treating those
who suffer in sickness. Thus, all disciplines that are constituent to medicine
(e.g.- research, public health), while focusing upon more proximate goals or
even ends, are ultimately contributory and dedicated to the act of medicine: the
clinical encounter between the person who seeks healing due to the vulnerability
of disease, injury and illness and the person who professes to heal (Pellegrino,
1997). Yet, the life of each person is unique and the act of medicine is personal.
Therefore, while the natural characteristics of a disease or injury process may be
nosologically characterizable, the resultant illness that is incurred by such
processes is unique to the life of a specific patient.4 The physician, using
particular domains of knowledge to recognize the mechanistic bases and
signature of disease and injury, must also gain inter-personal understanding in
order to contextually apprehend the expression of illness in the life a specific
person (Leder, 1990; Svenaeus, 2000). This apprehension requires an
appreciation of the life of the patient, and in so doing, the physician comes to
appreciate the goods inherent to that life. This is the practice of medicine: a
mutual relationship in which there is commitment to achieve the good inherent
to the relationship itself.5 Thus, the end or telos of medicine is not simply a
technically right treatment, but is also a healing that strives to achieve the good
for a particular person (Pellegrino, 1983). The reverence for the good of the
patient is therefore a reverence for the life and life world of that patient. This
reverence dictates conduct that is consistent in all ways possible with the telos.6

The regnant character and culture of contemporary medicine

The character of medicine has changed. As a practice, medicine exists within,
and is influenced by society. The twentieth century saw a progressive rise in
technology as a force of both societal de-construction and re-construction. The
apologia of this increasingly technological orientation has been the facilitation
of comfort, ease, personal and professional productivity, and health. Indeed, to
support this claim there is evidence that technology has been directly
contributory to productivity in healthcare (Fielding, 2001; Hofman, 2001;

A Moral Philosophy of Medicine

Reiser, 1993). In many ways this is true; certainly technology has been used to
good effect to improve the public health by reducing mortality from disease
through enhanced hygiene, diagnostics, and therapeutics. Paradoxically, while
these means have been highly effective within a disease-based model of care,
they have increased the prevalence of chronic morbidity and progressive,
longitudinal illness (Morris, 1998).

There are many diseases that cannot be cured; at best these can be managed.
So, while the technocentricity of medicine has allowed an extended lifespan
with chronic disease, it is only nominally prepared to address the personal
consequences of illness. The reason for this inaptitude lies in the fact that the
technological model has been bastardized; the relative ease that was to be
gained through the application and use of technology was supposed to allow
greater temporal latitude for humanitarian pursuits: in medicine, technology was
to empower the physician-patient relationship. But, as matter of fact, this has not
been the case; rather, technology has transformed the patient-physician
encounter into that of a technician servicing a malfunctioning unit, with
efficiency of time as a key goal in addition to the "repair" itself. Indeed, medical
technophilia has fostered an explicit commitment to speed. Of course, there are
situations in medicine where expediency is vital, and it is undeniable that
technology has been a significant contribution to medical diagnosis and
treatment. Yet, the costs of such technology are overwhelming, and are
systematically imposed upon medicine. This burden is ubiquitous to both
patients and clinicians. This too has been inflicted upon the clinical encounter,
and has led to the de-construction of its inherent humanitarian relationship in
favor of a time-constrained exchange of financially pre-established services.
Such temporal and fiscal obsessions have created a socio-medical agenda of
cost-escalation, professional competitiveness and commodification.

This situation has been furthered by the post-modern paradigm in which
moral skepticism has questioned, if not frankly refuted the inherent morality of
medicine, viewing medicine
instead as a contractual exchange whose
parameters, meaning and conduct is dictated by social values with relativistic
importance (Morris, 1998).7 Thus, medicine becomes an instrumental good,
competing with numerous other instrumental goods as commodities within a
consumerist framework, the values of which are changeable and dictated by
social demand and market viability. This is consistent with the investiture of
medicine as a techno-economic deliverable: technology is instrumental to the
good of medicine, therefore techno-centric medicine becomes seen as an
instrumental good. As such, the practice of medicine assumes an increasingly
business ethos, and the patient assumes the role of consumer. Within this ethos,
the telos and ethics of business are applied to medicine. These now contractually
defined parameters of services and fees are subject to increasing pressures of a

Chapter Fifteen

litigious environment, and become both determined by this environment and
reactive to it. Herein, the good of the patient is instrumentalized within a context
of fiscal and legal ends. The internal morality of medicine, and perhaps the very
moral agency of the physician may be suborned to the whim(s) of the
contemporary economic and legal climates of society. It is in this context that
Schweitzer's view of medicine qua humanitarian act, and the ethics that support
it, are regarded by some as vague notions that are reflective of a different time
and are without realistic meaning and/or practicality within the Zeitgeist of
contemporary medicine.

A re-vitalization of medicine as moral, inter-personal
relationship

However, we argue that medicine is not merely the use of advanced
technology in applied biology. That is not to say that there is not need or
importance of technology in medicine. To the contrary, the growing epistemic
capital of science mandates that the physician utilize knowledge gained by and
from the use of increasingly sophisticated technological means. But it is
important to note that these are, in fact means, and thus are used to facilitate the
end of medicine: a right and good healing of a particular patient. Technology is
not an end to itself, nor is it the sole basis of medicine. How technically-derived
information is applied within the clinical encounter is reliant upon contextual
understanding (peira), experiential knowledge (empeira) and the practical
wisdom (phronesis) of the physician to recognize and interact with the needs of
a specific patient in a given circumstance (Davis, 1997). These needs are both
therapeutic and existential, and necessitate further understanding of the life
variables of each unique patient.

Frequently, these are complex, multi-dimensional and require that the
physician confront and resolve issues of ethical importance. These decisional
and executive domains are not accessible through the use of technology alone.
Certainly, technology can aid in differential diagnosis, yet even diagnosis is
more than a simple task of assessment. It is hermeneutic and an act of both
naming and framing the patient within a particular continuum of wellness and
illness that establishes a starting point for treatment that affects the trajectory of
their life (Phillips, 2004; Sadler, 2004; MacCormack, 1993). In the end, it is the
physicians' ability to use categorical types of knowledge practically employed
toward a defined end with skill and art (techne) and practical wisdom
(phronesis) that enables the decision of what is wrong and subsequently what
should be done for a particular patient. As Reiser (1993) has suggested, "true"
medicine may begin where technology leaves off. Thus, it is not the impersonal
use of technology, but techne, the art and skill of the physician, as an expression

A Moral Philosophy of Medicine

of intellectual and moral traits of character in interaction with another person,
that is the core of the act of medicine.

Additionally, we argue that the medical relationship is not simply a sociallydefined contract. While both physician and patient may be autonomous agents,
their interaction is not one of equally empowered individuals. The inequity of
power, knowledge and vulnerability within the medical relationship establish its
personal, covenantal nature, and the necessity of its internal morality
(Pellegrino, 1983). Similarly, medicine is not, and should not be a commodity.
Of course, in serving persons qua humans, medicine serves a broad social good:
the restoration or preservation of healthy life. This is a fundamental, not an
instrumental good, and is a good for the society of humankind, not society as
solely defined by political values or superficial consensus. Yet, we acknowledge
that medicine is not the sole social good, and as such must make significant
contribution along with other goods within the social milieu (May, 2000). In this
way, the intrinsic good, as consistent with its telos (i.e. technical rectitude and
beneficent good of the patient) must be maximized. Consistent with Schweitzer,
May (2000) has stated that medicine should not be exclusive, and should
embody reverence and respect for the subjects it serves. The act of profession
obligates the physician to uphold this covenantal basis of care across numerous
situations, through a practice ethos that is ethically grounded to the telos of
medicine.

Schweitzer's legacy in a re-construction of medicine

Hence, we feel that it is virtue, as the intellectual and moral basis of personal
character that forms the ethical foundation of medicine. Schweitzer's work
supports this thesis. From a theosophical perspective, Schweitzer makes
particular note of the infused virtues of faith (in a Creative Will), hope for the
possibilities of humanity and charitable action toward those who suffer. Central
to Schweitzer's ethics are empathy and beneficence, divinely ordained into a
natural order of living things. In seeking to find a meaningful basis of ethics,
Schweitzer recognized the inherent limitations of a singular explanatory
philosophy. Although he favored the Stoics, he considered Platonic and
Aristotelian orientations somewhat narrow (Brabazon, 2000; Meyer, 2002), did
not directly address the theologic ethics of Aquinas and assumed an increasingly
mystical view. Yet, while not overtly ascribing to, or developing a singular
philosophical or
to
communicate the depth of those approaches), he nonetheless advocated the
heuristic value of balancing revelation with reason: "To be truly rational is to
become ethical."8

theological perspective (and

thus perhaps failing

Chapter Fifteen

Individual moral commitment is the basis of his virtue ethics. He maintained
that personal morality is a reflection of character as the critical element in the
active promotion of the good. "Ethics is the activity of man directed to secure
the inner perfection of his own personality"9, and (in so doing) is "concerned
with all humanity."10,11 In this way, personal morality is a necessary condition
of active beneficence, insofar as it shapes the character that affords the
predisposition to both apprehend and affect the good.

A criticism of Schweitzer's virtue ethics is that while he alluded to the
necessity to order moral acts under particular circumstances (e.g.- the
permissive killing of mosquitoes in order to prevent the spread of disease), any
such ordering appears to be somewhat arbitrary (i.e.- rather than through the use
of a descriptive framework or practical judgement), thus rendering his ethics
impotent within the complexity of the medical context. While Schweitzer does
not address judgement directly (but rather implicitly), he explicitly placed
inherent responsibility upon the individual to utilize (differing types) of
knowledge in making ethical choices and decisions (Martin, 2002).

Still, the arbitrary ordering of acts suggests an arbitrariness of the lexical
hierarchy of the virtues underlying such acts. This has led to the accusation that
there is a vacuity to Schweitzer's work, a claim that in some ways is based upon
an additional criticism that his (virtue) ethics are not grounded within a
consistent philosophy or theology (Martin, 2002). Interestingly, the lack or
relative impossibility of a grounding philosophy has also been advanced as a
criticism of virtue-based ethics of medicine, in general (Beauchamp, 1996;
Veatch, 2001). It is in this light that we feel that Schweitzer's work can and
perhaps should be augmented so as to be influential in changing the negatively
valent moral spiral of contemporary medical culture.

We have described the essence of medicine and the architectonics as of the
medical relationship as being the basis for its uniqueness as a vocation and
profession.12 As the most notable proponent of such a philosophical importance,
Edmund Pellegrino (1983) maintains that a core philosophy of medicine is both
possible and necessary, and arises from and describes further the nature of the
act of medicine (see also: Stempsey, 2004 for review). For Pellegrino, this
philosophy is based upon an understanding of not just the metaphysical nature
of medicine, but that of humanity and the good. Therefore, by entering into the
vocation of medicine, the physician becomes both therapeutic and moral agent,
whose intentions and acts entail particular traits of character that enable and
obligate the physician to the good of the patient, and by extension, the
humanitarian good of helping those who suffer.

If we apply Schweitzer's work to a moral philosophy of medicine, it might
be able to stand alone as an account of those infused virtues that fulfill a
dimension of the act of medicine. Conversely, if we frame Schweitzer's virtue

A Moral Philosophy of Medicine

ethics within a larger metaphysical context of medicine as described by
Pellegrino, and relate this to other (i.e. Aristotelian and Thomist) intellectual
and moral virtues inherent to that philosophy, we develop an enhanced
rationality that allows recognition of its importance to a good that is focal to
persons as individuals, but also to a greater good of humanity and life.

Arguments against virtue ethics in medicine.13

Yet, there is equivocal refutation of the applicability of Schweitzer's work
and the viability of a virtue-based medical ethics. General argument against
virtue ethics has centered upon the proposition that a common virtue is not
achievable in a multi-cultural, globalized society (Doris, 2002; Engelhardt,
1996). In many ways, this supports Veatch's notion of a philosophy of medicine
and ethical framework that is dictated by social convention, as previously
discussed. We counter by acknowledging that there are domains of social
relativism that well serve both individual and community good in and of
medicine (Rachels, 1993). To be sure, it is this very multiplicity of values that
necessitates intellectual and moral virtue to guide the practice of medicine.
Schweitzer astutely stated that "Collapse . . . has come about because we left the
whole question of ethics to society. An ethical revival will only be possible
when ethics again becomes the business of thinking . . . and when individuals
seek to maintain themselves in the community as ethical personalities."14

Virtue predisposes the individual to consistently achieve the good (inherent
to the practice and consistent with the telos of medicine) across a range of
circumstance. We do not deny that values are important and need to be
recognized and incorporated into the ethical discourse. However, the telos of
medicine supersedes superficial attributes or instrumental values, and virtue
provides a moral compass that allows the physician to ethically navigate through
diverse socio-cultural territory to act in the patients' good.

A second, more general argument contends that Schweitzer's work, and
virtue-based medical ethics on the whole, are speculative, normative and not
concerned with acts or action. There is some truth to the notion that virtue ethics
focuses upon establishing grounding bases and norms, and in this regard
Schweitzer acknowledged that: "…of all the will for the ideal which exists in
mankind only a small part can be manifested in action".15 Still, we find that
this argument fails to recognize the components of action. Every event consists
of agents, a circumstance, an act (or acts), and consequences. Each has inherent
moral content and all reflect upon the nature of the agent(s). Specifically, the
Aristotelian notion of virtue was focal to both the underlying conditions of
character (i.e.- opinion, logos, feeling, pathe) and its habitual expression (ethos;
Aristotle, trans. Irwin, 1999). The relationship of virtue to action is direct and

Chapter Fifteen

reciprocal: actions arise from choice, and the elements of choice are disposed by
virtue (Aristotle, trans. Irwin, 1999). Thus the physician who acts as a moral
agent exercises virtue in the representation of the moral identity of medicine as
conjoined to its telos.

Summary and conclusion

The regnant form of contemporary medicine has evolved

into a
technocentric commodity, with socially dictated philosophy and ethics that are
instigating an increased distancing from its telos and internal morality. A
growing constituency within both medicine and society recognize this to be
opprobrious, question this progressive trajectory and seek to re-vitalize
medicine as a humanitarian endeavor. Toward this goal, Albert Schweitzer's
legacy to medicine need not be a passive reminder of an ideal from a time past.
If situated within a moral philosophy of medicine, such as that most
vociferously espoused by Edmund Pellegrino, the virtues Schweitzer advocated
could be conjoined to a larger virtue ethics that actively seeks to restore
humanitarian values to medical culture.
