CHAPTER SIXTEEN

ALBERT SCHWEITZER: STUDENT AND TEACHER

NIKKI LINDBERG

Albert Schweitzer took center stage in my life when I had the opportunity to
join the Albert Schweitzer Institute for the Humanities as director of programs.
This happened as I was about to retire and represented quite a departure from a
career in not-for-profit development. Work begun there has now followed me
into retirement and provided fulfillment to my later life.

The following approach, therefore, to "Albert Schweitzer: Student and
Teacher" is not academic but comes from the perspective of an enthusiastic
generalist. It is anecdotal in nature, illustrating some pivotal moments in Dr.
Schweitzer's own early education and highlighting some comments he made
about education, particularly as they are reflected in his Memoirs of Childhood
and Youth and Out of My Life and Thought. I will then move on to present an
overview of the Albert Schweitzer Educational Associates, the organization
Jenifer Grant, a seasoned educator, and I created to further efforts of introducing
Albert Schweitzer and his philosophy to children.

Albert Schweitzer was an academic, having earned advanced degrees in
philosophy, theology and music before studying medicine. He loved teaching,
came from a family of teachers on both his mother's and his father's side and
was principal of the Strasbourg Theological Seminary at the time he made his
decision to become a doctor and devote his life to the underserved in Africa. His
decision was met with dismay from his colleagues and friends. They wondered
how he could give up such a promising career. Hélène Bresslau was one of the
few friends who supported his decision. Later she became his wife, studied
nursing and was a partner to him in his work in Africa.

In talking of his own educational development Albert Schweitzer stresses
some key characteristics of successful learning. They all add up to the
inalienable fact that each of us is, to a large extent, the captain of our own
educational ship! These characteristics are:

Schweitzer as Student and Teacher

Curiosity; Reflection and reasoning; Self-reliance; Example; Spirituality.

Young children are by nature curious, but not all of them will remain so
throughout their lives. Curiosity was a hallmark of Albert Schweitzer's
learning. He took little for granted and sought the truth or the right path, or the
best way, wherever he went. When he was young, Schweitzer heard the story of
the birth of Jesus and wondered why the family was poor after the three kings
had brought so many wonderful gifts to the manger. His curiosity is shown in a
different way when, years later, he was studying philosophy and music in Paris.
He studied with three eminent music teachers in order to benefit from different
pedagogical perspectives: the famous organist Charles Marie Widor; the pianist
Marie Jaëll-Trautmann, known for her studies of touch; and the more
conventional teacher, J. Phillipp. Jaëll-Trautmann and Phillipp were unaware of
his studying with Widor; they would have dropped him had they known. From
Schweitzer's perspective, even the very best of teachers did not provide all the
answers. He recognized curiosity as an intellectual gift that he encouraged in his
students when he became a teacher.

After leaving Paris, Schweitzer continued his studies in philosophy in Berlin,
a city he vastly preferred. He found the intellectual life of Berlin was more
conducive to discussion and the sharing of ideas; his sense of curiosity was
nurtured there. Even though Paris was a world intellectual center, Schweitzer
found the professors' method of teaching "antiquated"; they tended to give
"lectures which bore solely on the examination syllabus."1 On the other hand, of
Berlin, he speaks of the heart, the spirit and the energy of the university and of
the city.

With curiosity came reflection and reasoning. When he was about 7 or 8
years old, Albert had the responsibility of keeping the family dog Phylax away
from the mailman; Phylax had a habit of attacking people in uniform. To do
this, Albert pretended that he was a wild animal tamer and wielded a whip to
keep Phylax in the corner. Eventually, Albert tells us he thought about his
actions and recognized they were not kind or considerate; he could easily and
simply have held on to Phylax's collar to restrain him. At an even younger age,
Schweitzer continued to cry long after he had been stung by a bee. It no was no
longer painful but he reasoned the crying gained him lots of attention. The
process of reasoning was not encouraged in Albert's school. Most teaching was
done by rote in those days, and most teachers wanted to hear back just what they
had imparted. When he took confirmation classes from Pastor Wennegel, he
mentioned that no questions were allowed. As a result, when Albert Schweitzer
taught confirmation classes as a young seminarian, he planned time for
questions. "Reason, I told myself, has been given to us so that we may grasp
through it all thoughts."2

Chapter Sixteen

The path from young boy to teacher was not always smooth for Albert. He
described himself as an obnoxious teenager who challenged the ideas of adults
and questioned conventional wisdom continually. His reflection and reasoning
needed outlet and testing, which they always would, but in a more tempered
fashion!

Schweitzer's self-reliance showed itself when he was very young. At about
the same time as the incident with his dog, Albert reluctantly went off into the
woods with his friend Heinrich Brasch and their newly-made slingshots. Brasch
had coaxed him to join in a hunt for birds. It was springtime and the leaves were
not yet on the trees so the birds were clearly silhouetted as they sat on the
branches. Just as he was about to shoot his slingshot, Albert heard the bells of
the village church ring out. The sound of the bell reminded Albert of the
commandment, "Thou shalt not kill". He shooed the birds away, dropped his
slingshot and ran home. As an adult he commented on this episode: "From that
day on I have dared to free myself from the fear of men, and when my
innermost conviction was at stake, I have considered the opinions of others less
important than before. I began to overcome my fear of being laughed at by my
classmates. The way in which the commandment not to kill and torture worked
on me is the great experience of my childhood and youth. Next to it, all others
pale."3

This action on young Albert's part is particularly noteworthy because Albert
was privileged as the son of the village pastor and perceived as well to-do and
an outsider by his classmates. He wanted desperately to be accepted by the other
village children. Major confrontations took place with his parents over the
clothes he wished to wear as he wanted to dress like the local boys. He suffered
repeated punishments from his father for his determination to look like them. He
definitely had a mind of his own.

Young Albert grew up to be a role model for people the world over, however
he recognizes this did not happen without the help of others. He reflects on his
gratitude to those who served him as an example in his own education. After
attending the local schools, Albert had the opportunity to attend gymnasium (a
secondary school running from the 5th through 13th grades) in Mulhouse, about
50 miles from his home in Gunsbach. This was affordable for the Schweitzer
family only because he could live with his great uncle Louis Schweitzer and
Aunt Sophie. In classes there, he still tended to daydream as he had throughout
his school years, so much so that his scholastic record was not very good. His
parents and Uncle Louis and Aunt Sophie were beside themselves when his
scholarship, available to the sons of pastors, was in jeopardy because of his
lacklustre performance.

Fortunately a new teacher, Dr. Wehmann, entered his life. Albert, who was
now in the European equivalent of the 10th grade, described Dr. Wehmann as

Schweitzer as Student and Teacher

coming into class well-prepared, completing his lesson plans and handing out
tests on time. This impressed young Albert. He does not mention what he taught
but rather how he taught. Albert admired his professionalism. The implication
was that Dr. Wehmann cared about his work and had respect for his students.
Furthermore Dr. Wehmann taught Albert how to study and gave him selfconfidence.

"I could never tell you how indebted I am to him," Schweitzer wrote in a
letter. "I and many of my schoolmates feel that we owe our sense of duty to the
mere example of his life . . . He had a rare gift for encouraging his students."4
Later he said, "Example is not the main thing in influencing others, it is the only
thing." And, of course, Schweitzer, who followed Dr. Wehmann's example,
grew up to become a major example himself. As he said to Norman Cousins, "I
will make my life my argument. I will advocate the things I believed in, in terms
of the life I lived and what I did."5

Another pedagogue Schweitzer looked up to as a fine example was the
director of the gymnasium, Dr. Wilhelm Deecke. Schweitzer felt that Director
Deecke wanted "not only to impart knowledge to us but to make real human
beings of us… He spiced up his lessons by opening vistas on all sorts of subjects
and problems that were in some way connected with the subject under
discussion."6

Finally, Spirituality was the very essence of Dr. Schweitzer's life. He often
spoke of spirit in connection with education. In a letter to his grandson
Christoph Martin he wrote: "May you find the right path in life, that is, the right
spirit that produces the right kind of behavior. May you acquire a true education,
and may your thoughts attain the noble and profound humanity that prepares us
to find the right way and to take it."7

There are a number of other similar examples: To the Albert Schweitzer
School in Berlin, he wrote: "Maintain the right spiritual relationship with your
teachers. Respect them as the people who are not only teaching you knowledge
but also trying to educate you for a proper life."8

To high school seniors in Wiesbaden, he wrote: "Maintain your ideal of
humanity in your hearts and let it guide you in your relations with other human
beings and with society. . . . The time demands not discussion from us but
spiritual work, which emanates almost automatically from our conduct and our
hearts."9

To Helen Preen, he wrote: "What we need is to hope for a new spirit so that
human responsibility toward all living creatures becomes something that is
taken for granted! Here as in all things, we have to rely on the spirit!"10

To Johann Hoostad, he wrote: "The most important among them [active
great people] are those who create ideas that spell spiritual progress for
humanity."11

Chapter Sixteen

And finally in a letter to Keith Osborn, a pioneer in childhood education who
was then the division chair at the Merrill-Palmer Institute at Wayne State
University in Detroit, he wrote: "I have regarded it as the true mission of my life
to advance the humanitarian ideal in our spiritual life. . . . Even children should
reflect on themselves and their relationship to other beings and come to realize
that reverence for life is the basic principle of goodness. Children should not
just take over goodness as a tradition that they are taught; they should reflect
and discover it within themselves as something intrinsic to them, that is theirs
for a lifetime. Many teachers have told me that children are deeply impressed by
the idea of the reverence for life because they possess it as experience and not
just as something they are taught. If mankind is not to be wiped out by wars
with dreadful weapons, we have to develop an ethical civilization dominated by
the idea of reverence for life—a civilization that can lead the nations beyond
short-sighted, bellicose nationalism and enable them to coexist in peace."12

With regard to Dr. Schweitzer's early education, it would be a disservice to
ignore the role that his parents played. Before Albert even went to school he
knew his Bible; he said prayers with his parents every evening (except he added
his own prayers for animals, not understanding why they had been omitted); he
had learned to play the piano, and he had been exposed to the nature of the
woods, farms and vineyards around him.

Many schools, particularly in the 1950s when Albert Schweitzer was known
all over the world, asked to name their schools after him and to introduce his
philosophy to the students. There are over three hundred Albert Schweitzer
Schools in Germany alone. Dr. Schweitzer was delighted about these tributes:
"The school will be the way!" he said, "From the time they start school, young
people must be imbued with the idea of reverence for all living things. Then we
will be able to develop a spirit based on ethical responsibility and one that will
stir many. Then we will be entitled to call ourselves a humanity of culture."13

And so we come to Dr. Schweitzer's major legacy: his philosophy of

Reverence for Life and how to encourage and stimulate that in children today.

When I retired from the Albert Schweitzer Institute for the Humanities, I
joined its Board of Governors and was closely involved in the effort to move the
Institute, now the Albert Schweitzer Institute, under the umbrella of Quinnipiac
University. Its primary effort today focuses on you, the adult student
community. Dr. Ives kindly encouraged Jenifer Grant and me to continue to
develop the work for children that began during my tenure at the Institute. As a
result, we formed the Albert Schweitzer Educational Associates. Its mission is
to further the philosophy of Albert Schweitzer by developing materials for
young students and to work with organizations that also strive to help students
develop into caring and ethically responsible adults. It is a practical approach to
applying Albert Schweitzer's philosophy so that children can live it too.

Schweitzer as Student and Teacher

The original work began with a group of volunteer teachers who gave
generously of their time to develop age-appropriate lessons based on Albert
Schweitzer's life, philosophy, and teachings. These teachers came from public,
private, and parochial schools; they came from urban, suburban, and rural
schools. They shared the belief in the relevance of Dr. Schweitzer's example
today and that the lessons inherent in Dr. Schweitzer's life would serve as a
springboard to a broader ethical outlook. The teachers were divided into three
groups, K-2, Grades 3–5, and Grades 6-8 based on their expertise and
experience. Four themes emerged to provide scope and sequence to the lessons:

Respect for all that lives
Caring for Others
Courage to act responsibly
Appreciating differences
For kindergarten students, ASEA reproduced a little coloring book by Vee
Guthrie. It tells the story of Albert Schweitzer and his love of animals. The text
has been translated into Haitian Creole and French. Quinnipiac students are
doing the Spanish translation so that it can go with them to schools in Latin
America. Each booklet consists of four folded standard 8 ½" x 11" sheets of
white paper. It can be easily duplicated by a teacher so that each student can
have a copy of his or her own. One page is blank for the child to fill in; it is
titled "This is my drawing of me helping living things too."

For Grade 2, we offer a publication that was done under the auspices of the
ASIH, entitled Albert Schweitzer—A Friend To All That Lives. It is a short story
of the life of Albert Schweitzer and includes many drawings of life in
Lambaréné done by sixth-grade students plus a lesson plan. This also, is easily
reproducible by teachers.

My memory of taking Dr. Schweitzer's daughter, Rhena Schweitzer Miller,
to the second-grade class of Sue Mattingly, one of our volunteer teachers in
Meriden, Connecticut, is still vivid. A poster of Dr. Schweitzer in Africa with
young children was on the wall. Albert Schweitzer—A Friend To All That Lives
was on the desk. A large mural ran the length of the classroom where the
children had drawn the African jungle, its palms, vines and assortment of
indigenous animals. Mobiles of birds hung from the ceiling. The room was
vibrant with the joy of learning. When it came time for the children to tell how
they thought they could make the world a better place, their responses were
thoughtful and enthusiastic: "I will remind people to treat animals with respect
because they are special too." "I will always work hard and do my best because
every little person can make our world a better place." "If kids keep telling
adults to use the Golden Rule, maybe they'll start using it too!"

Another resource for the early grades is Frederick Franck's My Friend in
Africa about a young patient in the hospital at Lambaréné. Frederick Franck

Chapter Sixteen

worked for many years with Dr. Schweitzer in Africa as a dentist. He is a wellknown artist, sculptor and founder of Pacem in Terris in Warwick, New York, a
spiritual retreat dedicated to peace and art. ASEA recently self-published a
revised and limited edition of this story that underscores friendship and
responsibility. A number were sent to England where it is being used by Percy
and Vrena Mark in their work with Albert Schweitzer UK.

The units appropriate for Grades 3-5 and for Grades 6-8 are still a work in
progress. The 3-5 unit includes a biography of Albert Schweitzer Albert
Schweitzer Humanitarian and a teacher's discussion guide to the biography. A
vocabulary list and map activities are provided, as are suggestions for related
activities applicable to writing, a biography study, community service, social
studies, geography, science, mathematics, music, and art.

The Grades 6-8 unit includes a short review of Schweitzer's life and consists
primarily of excerpts from Schweitzer's writings dealing with issues that are
important in the lives of students of this age. The readings are followed by
discussion questions and activities which provoke students into thinking about
issues they may not previously have confronted. They help students develop
into people who care and people who help others. They learn about the
importance of working together and the importance of being a leader. The
materials can be spread over a three-year period, working from the simpler
concepts to the more sophisticated. The concept of service is stressed at all
levels and students are encouraged to come up with their own projects.

Our work with other organizations has included a project with a new charter
school in Florida that includes as part of its charter "studying and applying
Albert Schweitzer's philosophy of Reverence for Life." The school is part of a
new community that is built on the premise that the natural environment be
respected. ASEA is collaborating with the ASPCA and representative of Jane
Goodall's Roots and Shoots program to create a Compassionate Curriculum that
is designed to help students develop into caring and ethically responsible people
who respect all that lives, who care for others, who have the courage to act
responsibly and who respect and appreciate differences. Its lessons are easily
incorporated into class studies and support state educational standards.
Developmentally appropriate themes have been agreed upon for each grade.
These are supported by lessons, projects, and service ideas submitted by the
three organizations. Frederick Franck's book My Friend in Africa is a
recommended reading resource for the second grade where the theme is
friendship. Even songs are suggested, like "Let it be" by Malvina Reynolds, as
arts are integral to a complete education and music was so important in Dr.
Schweitzer's life. As part of this project, ASEA recommends creating gardens
and observation sites for each grade, and going on nature walks. Young children
respond positively when they learn that plants all have names, belong to families

Schweitzer as Student and Teacher

and have relatives. These out-of-doors activities provide children with life long
lessons in the appreciation of wildlife and of plants, how they grow and how
they interact. Learning to 'commune with nature' may sound archaic, but it
provides a sense of belonging to something much larger than the human
community, and an understanding of the interconnectedness of all living things,
something Albert Schweitzer learned about when he was very young.
Evaluation tools are being developed to determine whether the goals of the
curriculum are being met.

Working with this charter school is a special situation. The demands of the
No Child Left Behind act have made it more difficult for teachers to introduce
lessons of their own choosing. This presents ASEA with the challenge to see
where and how Albert Schweitzer as a role model, and Reverence for Life as a
lesson for life, can best be introduced to today's children.

Dr. Schweitzer said, "The future of humanity depends on each person
striving, in whatever situation he finds himself, to manifest true humanity to
men." And educators must, "start early to instil in students that they are on this
earth to help and serve others. That is as important to pass on to them as
knowledge." 14

We have seen the joy in children growing a plant, caring for an animal,
helping an old person cross the street. We have seen children beginning to
experience the awe and wonder of life itself, enhanced through learning about
Albert Schweitzer. Hopefully they too will exercise curiosity and reason,
develop self-reliance, be spiritual, and set a good example.

CHAPTER SEVENTEEN

ALBERT SCHWEITZER'S LEGACY
FOR EDUCATIONAL THEORY AND PRACTICE

A.G. RUD

As one of the great figures of the last century, Albert Schweitzer has become
a legend. Known chiefly for his humanitarian work as a physician in the African
jungle, his journey there started much earlier, in unlikely circumstances.
Schweitzer was a noted Biblical scholar, minister, organist, and author of a
number of philosophical works, before deciding to become a medical doctor and
go to Africa.

Though Schweitzer's work has been examined and extended in numerous
fields, there has not been a thorough investigation of Schweitzer and education.
Ronald L. Abrell wrote three short overlapping articles on this topic a number of
years ago (Abrell, 1974, 1978, 1981). Abrell saw Schweitzer as deeply
concerned about education, but asserted that his educational thought had been
overlooked. Schweitzer believed that civilization was in crisis, though it could
be rescued if the ethic of Reverence for Life was learned by the young. Building
on these insights, I aim in my book on Schweitzer and education to assess more
broadly the educational value of Schweitzer's activity, by investigating his
philosophical thought and commentary on history and global developments, and
describing curriculum based on his work, schools based on his name, and
various institutes, such as at Quinnipiac and Chapman universities, and the
Albert Schweitzer Campus at the Harmony Institute in Florida.

Schweitzer sought personal salvation through his work, deciding to leave his
scholarly and artistic life at age 30 and devote himself to service. He was deeply
concerned about what he saw as a decaying European culture. His contemporary
Oswald Spengler's book, The Decline of the West, was read widely for its
historical dissection of the supposed ills of European culture, as were the more
lyrical writings of Friedrich Nietzsche. Schweitzer differed from both these
thinkers by making committed action his means of cultural and spiritual
regeneration. In my book, I examine Schweitzer's philosophical heritage, to
more fully understand whom he was following or criticizing, as I build an

Chapter Seventeen

argument for the pertinence of his thought and action for philosophy of
education and educational practice.

I investigate the genesis and rationale of his decision to go to Africa, aided
by analysis of letters between Schweitzer and his wife Helene, as well as
correspondence and an interview with his only child, Mrs. Rhena Schweitzer
Miller. I want to know what we may learn about similar, though usually less
radical, decisions made by teachers and other educators as they commit
themselves to a life of service.

Analysis of Schweitzer's life and legacy will aid my examination of the
formation of character at all levels of education, especially in teacher and
leadership preparation programs. I link Schweitzer's work to the moral
dimensions of educational practice. My situating of his key thought, Reverence
for Life, as well as his commitment to "make his life his argument" within
current educational theory should lead to a beginning and development of
Schweitzer educational scholarship. My initial research has prompted me to
think about why Schweitzer's legacy is broad, but not deep, in education. I
believe Schweitzer has been long due to be more fully discussed and located
within educational theory and practice.

I became interested in Schweitzer as a topic in education several years ago,
while serving as a consultant for the Harmony Institute, a research and outreach
center in Florida dealing with the environment and mankind's relationship to
animals. The founder of the Harmony Institute, Martha Eastman Lentz, took the
work of Albert Schweitzer as her inspiration for the future establishment of the
Albert Schweitzer Campus at the Institute. There, researchers can conduct
studies on the community of Harmony, Florida, established around the Institute,
and see if there were long-lasting and tangible benefits to a lifestyle that
emphasized natural settings amidst animals. Her model, of course, was
Schweitzer's own attention to all living things, as well as his hospital at
Lambaréné, with its many animals and hospitality toward them and their human
owners who came for medical services.

I immediately became fascinated by Schweitzer as a source for educational
theory and practice. My area of interest in education is the history and
philosophy of education, commonly called social or cultural foundations. There,
we seek to elucidate the roots of education as a human art, grounded in
humanistic inquiry, as a balance to educational "science" exemplified by
psychological investigation of educational phenomena using quantitative and
technical methods. I am particularly interested in ethics and moral development
at all levels of education, from pre-kindergarten through college, and this led me
to work in the new field of anthrozoology, the study of the relationship of
humans and animals, in particular companion animals. It was through this lens
on moral development that I became involved in the Harmony Institute.

Educational Theory and Practice

Underlying this academic or disciplinary interest was an intensely personal
interest in what I came to call Schweitzer's radical decision. Albert Schweitzer
left established careers that were full of future promise in theology, philosophy,
and music to study medicine as a mature student, and then travel to a remote
part of the world to help others. I wondered, what compelled him to do this, and
how can an investigation into how this decision came about inform the less
radical, but equally important, decisions educators make to teach, and to help
others in learning?

I began reflecting in earnest on these ideas in the wake of the terrorist attacks
of September 11, 2001. The shock of those events, coupled with a deep concern
about both my place in the world and the state of the world itself, led me to reevaluate my intellectual life as many others were doing. I was troubled that
degradation of the environment continued without the concern I thought it
merited. I was particularly saddened that global issues that underlay these
developments were seen as being solvable through armed conflict.

At that point, I intensified my investigation. I wanted to know what a man,
born two centuries earlier, could say to us and in particular what I could bring of
his work to education. I found that early on he had an interest in education and
in the well-being of animals, both of which he presents in his own words in the
several books about his childhood and youth. I was struck by how he dwelt upon
suffering, and considered its relief to be the main purpose of his life. I
discovered that he worked early on with the homeless but did not find that fully
satisfying, and that the mystery of suffering perplexed him. Why should he be
happy and others should suffer is a paraphrase of a question that Schweitzer
often asked himself. His idea to go to Africa seemed sudden, dramatic, and
final. He wanted to find a way to act, rather than simply talk about it, and it is
well known how pleased he was to be considered a physician putting what he
called the "religion of love" into practice.

I found there were important elements of his intellectual heritage that
informed this decision, and set the context for what I take to be his educational
contribution. Schweitzer believed that he was living in a time of cultural decay.
It is beyond the scope of this chapter to speak in detail about the particulars of
this issue, though I will develop these ideas in my book. In his brief book,
Albert Schweitzer: Sketches for a Portrait (1994), Jackson Lee Ice points out
that Schweitzer saw the dominance of a market-oriented life and the eroding of
human relationships. This theme had been sounded by both European and
American writers and thinkers for some time; one only has to think of the
American transcendentalists Ralph Waldo Emerson and Henry David Thoreau,
as well as Spengler and Nietzsche, right up to poets such as Rainer Maria Rilke,
who lyrically decried the manufactured objects of the new industrial order over
the handmade. Obviously too this theme continues forward with Martin

Chapter Seventeen

Heidegger's own particular brand of domesticity. Along with this dominance of
the markets come the twin burdens of nationalism and imperialism. Schweitzer
lived through two world wars, and was a prisoner of war in the first, suffering
sickness. He saw this world as being too small and limited, and the people in it
too devoted to their own profit and gain.

the
Schweitzer was also concerned about what could be called
overspecialization of thought. Indeed, we can see his response as a simple
physician in the jungle as being against such overspecialization. Schweitzer in
his lifetime witnessed an enormous growth in the natural sciences and technical
fields, such that medical science eradicated or controlled many previously
virulent diseases. Yet Schweitzer repeatedly asks us to consider some of the
most fundamental questions of life, and how we treat each and every person,
and indeed, all living things.

Schweitzer was interested in where the locus of control for human life was
headed. Ice (1994) sees that locus for Schweitzer as from the human to the
technological, the individual to the state, and nature to industry. In the first two
of these modes, the more immediate is given over to that which is larger, and
individual conscience and action are at least susceptible to subjugation. In the
second, nature has given way to industry, where again, subjugation is the mode
of operation, rather than coexistence. For Schweitzer, the way out of these
developments, at least in part, is to have Reverence for Life.

Reverence for Life is a special term for Schweitzer and perhaps his most
famous utterance. He uses it for the universal concept of ethics. He believed that
such an ethics, as circumscribed by this term, would reconcile the drives of
altruism and egoism by requiring a respect for the lives of all other beings, and
by demanding the highest development of an individual's moral resources. The
original German gives us more meaning, however. "Ehrfurcht vor dem Leben"
conveys respect at all lengths. The German ehrfurcht is akin to the English awe,
namely that which we feel at a mountaintop, a storm at sea, a tornado bearing
down upon the Midwestern cornfields. For Schweitzer, of course, the immediate
context was the African jungle, in its immensity and overwhelming power
(Brabazon, 2000, p. 268).

Furthermore, ehrfurcht is a product of a particular kind of thought. It is not
step by step rational, linear, thinking, but rather the dwelling of thought, a
meditative consideration, a taking in, that is conveyed by the German verb
Denken. It is this kind of thought that the English novelist and poet D. H.
Lawrence had in mind in the following poem (cited in Brabazon, 2000, p. 269-
70):

Educational Theory and Practice

Thought, I love thought.
But not the juggling, and twisting of already existent ideas,
I despise that self-important game
Thought is the welling up of unknown life into consciousness,
Thought is the testing of statements on the touchstone of conscience.
Thought is gazing into the face of life, and reading what can be read,
Thought is pondering over experience, and coming to conclusion.
Thought is not a trick, or an exercise, or a set of dodges.
Thought is a man in his wholeness wholly attending.

Schweitzer's Reverence for Life has been criticized as vague and
impractical, or simply a motto. Some critics state that Schweitzer had not
developed a strict ethical system using the dictum as a foundation. I am
interested in how Schweitzer's formulation can be used to ground moral
thinking in our schools, where I see that the lack of such a foundation enables
different and competing ethical claims to not be fully examined. I see Reverence
for Life as more a way of being in the world, and an insight into the nature of
life itself, as part of his world view. I see Schweitzer as not so much successful
in creating a new ethical code, as in searching for a way that would bind us all
together.

Yet there are ways that we may use Reverence for Life in the schools that
take advantage of some different meanings. Ice (1994) has striven to give an
analysis of the term to show how it may be understood if one wants to counter
the critique that it is a vague and imprecise motto. Here I examine Ice's analysis
and discuss its usefulness in educational settings.

Ice first says that Reverence for Life can be interpreted as having a
biological or instinctive function, that we respect the will to live. I don't see this
as being particularly compelling in a school setting, other than as an admonition
as part of an overall educational aim. A second formulation would be one of
self-affirmation, in the case of my own life: "Thus Reverence for Life
characterizes the discovery of what it means to respect myself and to realize that
my own life-giving potentialities. A lack of respect for ourselves makes us our
own worst enemies." (Ice, p. 39)

Though I could see some application of this to schoolchildren, the best
application of this formulation would be for teachers. Teachers are notorious for
putting others before themselves, and giving to others before they take into
account their own needs. Teachers in our public schools spend their time in
mental and physical "egg crates" (Rud, 1993) where they are isolated from
others who might provide mental and emotional support, as well as just being
able to share ideas and have adult conversations. Thus teachers, one to a room
as one egg is to its own compartment in a crate, are limited in the amount of
growth. They also expect of themselves that they will give to others first, before

Chapter Seventeen

their own needs have been taken care of. Of course this does not apply to all
teachers, as there are saints and knaves in teaching as much as there is in other
areas, but for the most part, in my experience of working with schoolteachers in
several states, this is their mindset.

This blends well into the third of Ice's ideas of how to interpret Reverence
for Life. The dictum can be interpreted as being about a proclivity toward love
and concern. Ice believes we need to acknowledge the joining of the ego and the
other, as the two elements of our psyche that need to be brought together. Much
of what education is all about today in industrialized nations is an education for
instrumentality, to gain better jobs, to perform tasks better, to gain technical and
useful knowledge to use in the workplace, to get ahead.

This formulation of a meaning of Reverence for Life focuses not only on an
inner development, that is, an acknowledgement of the ego and its limitations,
but to see that the ego itself needs to be nested in a development of that which is
outer, namely the other, or the social context. It is close to what the philosopher
John Dewey saw as important as not just a joining of the two, inner and outer,
ego and other, individual and society, but recognition of the basic union of the
two. The lone individual does not make sense ontologically apart from a social
context. They are together originally, and have been separated only by thinkers
who have got it wrong according to Dewey and others. Reverence for Life
acknowledges this union.

Ice also sees Reverence for Life as calling for a display of concern and
attention for all life whatsoever. It is not for humans, but for all organisms.
Schweitzer is, of course, well known for the lengths and troubles he went to in
order to preserve living organisms at Lambaréné. This kind of dictum would be
what we could begin to enact in our schools. Research into the way that children
learn about nurturing behavior has shown that boys, in particular, can be
encouraged to be caring and generous human beings by tending to companion
animals at an early age (Melson, 2001). Yet by far the most important meaning
of Reverence for Life in an educational context is its prescriptive function,
declaring what we ought to do. Here it can be enacted as a question for
curricular planning.

Another important element of Schweitzer's legacy for education is, of
course, his fateful decision to go to Africa and start a hospital, and to also do so
as an act of devotion, captured in his famous statement that he wanted to "make
his life his argument." Individual responsibility is paramount as one goes about
using one's own life to make a statement, not only to oneself, but to others, and
if so inclined as Schweitzer was, as testament to one's religious faith. I shall
develop this idea further in the book.

Schweitzer's emphasis upon service and enacting what one believes is
related to Johann Pestalozzi's educational aim of the development of the whole

Educational Theory and Practice

person—not merely intellectual growth, but what Pestalozzi calls the hand, heart
and head. Pestalozzi was quoted by his biographer: "I wish to wrest education
from the outworn order of doddering old teaching hacks as well as from the
new-fangled order of cheap, artificial teaching tricks, and entrust it to the eternal
powers of nature herself, to the light which God has kindled and kept alive in
the hearts of fathers and mothers, to the interests of parents who desire their
children grow up in favour with God and with men" (Silber, 1965).

Pestalozzi goes beyond Rousseau's view of individual, unencumbered
learning to set out concrete ways based upon research. He reconciled the
tension, recognized by Rousseau, between the education of the individual (for
freedom) and that of the citizen (for responsibility and use). Schweitzer's own
work at his hospital and the surrounding villages can be seen in this tradition,
too. It is an environmental education that is based on an appreciation of the
natural world. Part of this task is to educate so that we understand that no person
has the right to receive only benefits from nature and society, but that each has a
responsibility to give back some part of ourselves to serve the greater good of
the natural environment and the community of man.

Educational theory without practice is hollow, and Schweitzer's influence
has been noticed in schools. However, I will argue that what is taken from
Schweitzer has not resulted in profound changes in educational practice. I am
concerned about how educational ideas and ideals become disseminated, and see
how the slight influence of his work upon current schooling is ironic, given that
he believed that "the school will be the way."

His Nobel Peace Prize speech promotes a curriculum based on problems,
rather than subjects, certainly a way of looking at schooling that resonates with
current curriculum theory. Schools that bear Schweitzer's name abound, as do
institutes, centers, and initiatives. I have been intimately involved in several
such initiatives. While I have no reason to doubt the sincerity of intention and
purpose, the world of education turns a slightly deaf ear toward these efforts.
Why? I will explore what I believe are two particular reason for this, but there
may be many more.

Toward the end of his life, Schweitzer became the target of withering
criticism of his efforts in Lambaréné. His image, of the kindly white doctor clad
all in white amidst the "natives," promoted throughout the world in the wake of
his visit to the United States in 1949 and his subsequent Nobel Peace Prize a
few years later, raised him to the level of celebrity. Celebrity begets attention
and, of course, those who doubt sincerity of purpose. Schweitzer was attacked
for his supposed colonialist practices born of a nationalism that he himself
despised. His hospital was roundly criticized for what many Western observers
saw as backward practices and unsanitary conditions. These lingering images at

Chapter Seventeen

the end of his life have contributed to what may be an eclipse of his influence,
and a questioning of his legacy.

tyrannical,

No human being is perfect, and it is certainly important to investigate the
work of any person with an open mind. Yet, the characterization of Schweitzer
as a
racist, colonialist European absorbed by his own
megalomaniacal needs that reaches its apogee in the film "Le Grand Blanc de
Lambaréné" (1995) is unwarranted. While I don't believe the film is widely
known, its damage to Schweitzer's legacy is certainly real enough for many
intellectuals. Yet if we are to learn from Schweitzer's own work in Africa, then
we must ask one of the questions raised bluntly and clumsily by this film: What
did the indigenous people of Lambaréné learn from him, and what did he learn
from them? How was he changed by his time in Africa? I will continue to
investigate this issue as I develop further and deeper an understanding of the
educational legacy of Schweitzer, and I welcome comments from others.

Even if we were able to construct a vibrant educational theory and
curriculum around these ideas, can we see Schweitzer as inspiration for us as we
move forward in this already turbulent century? What more can he give us as an
example that can be useful in the schools? We can look to his devoted life of
service, and how he articulated a philosophy of ethical conduct, as partial
guidance as we address environmental and global policy issues today.
Rushworth Kidder, head of the Institute for Global Ethics, declares that we will
not survive the new century using the ethics of the last, but I believe that what
Schweitzer articulates can be a foundation for such a new ethics, and can be
enacted more forcefully and systematically in our schools. We can look to what
Schweitzer says about all life being interconnected and interdependent as we
make the argument that life itself must be held in reverential awe. Individual
responsibility is important, too, as one makes one's life one's argument for what
one stands. Individual action is always important, even in an age of large
organizations and bureaucratic emphases around the world.

In conclusion, I want to raise two topics that bear further scrutiny. In
Socrates, Buddha, Confucius, Jesus (1962), Karl Jaspers provides a concise but
illuminating account of the thought and lives of unique individuals who came at
the start of many of the great spiritual and philosophic traditions of the world.
These individuals all took as part of their mission to guide the human spirit
toward more basic and ultimate values, and they continue to influence the world
today. Jaspers called them paradigmatic individuals. There has been a concern
about Jaspers's selection of these four individuals, begging the question "Why
these men? Why only four?" Jaspers never fully explains his choice, beyond
stating that: "these paradigmatic individuals have exerted a historical influence
of incomparable scope and depth." (1962, p. 3). As part of assessing
Schweitzer's educational legacy, I am asking if we can consider him such a

Educational Theory and Practice

paradigmatic individual for our own times, as someone who exerted such a
historical influence. Though this would require more careful argumentation,
which I intend to do in my book, I raise it as a topic for consideration now.

In addition to considering Schweitzer as a possible paradigmatic individual,
I also would argue that Schweitzer may be considered an educational prophet. I
draw upon the theoretical work of David Purpel (1989) to claim that
Schweitzer's value may not be in any curriculum that he possibly could have
proposed, but in his example to us of moving us to a vision of what might be in
our educational lives. The value of an educational prophet is in awakening
others to what might be, and to warn others of what paths down which we are
headed.

Schweitzer's intellectual and spiritual journey continues to influence many
people in a number of fields. One of the most profound legacies of Schweitzer
has not yet reached fruition. That legacy, signaled by his statement "the school
will be the way" is in educational thought, and more importantly, in the
enactment of this thought in the lives of teachers and students in the schools of
today and tomorrow.

CONTRIBUTORS

Dr. Edward D'Angelo is an adjunct professor of philosophy at Quinnipiac
University. His book The Teaching of Critical Thinking was a pioneering work
in critical thinking instruction. He organized the first conference between Cuban
and North American philosophers in 1982 and currently specializes in
contemporary Cuban philosophy.

Luis Alberto Cordero is the executive director of the Arias Foundation and
Ambassador in Special Mission for the Ministry of Foreign Affairs of Costa
Rica.

Timothy Dansdill is an Associate Professor of English in the College of Liberal
Arts at Quinnipiac University. His teaching interests are composition (personal
essay, poetry) and the history of rhetoric.

Suzanne S. Hudd is an Associate Professor of Sociology at Quinnipiac
University. She studies the influence of social and cultural factors and
contemporary character education practices on the development of character in
young people. She teaches courses on Social Stratification and Research
Methods in which she incorporates community-based experiences for her
students.

James Giordano, Ph.D., M.Phil., is Scholar in Residence at the Center for
Clinical Bioethics, and Associate Professor of Palliative Medicine in the
Department of Medicine, Georgetown University Medical Center, Washington,
D.C.. He is a Fellow in Medical Philosophy and Ethics of the Institute Trentino
di Cultura, Trento, Italy, and Visiting Fellow of the John P. McGovern M.D.
Center for Health, Humanities and the Human Spirit, University of Texas Health
Sciences Center, in Houston. His books include Complementary and Integrative
Approaches
to Chronic Pain (Jones-Bartlett) and (with Bert Gordijn)
Neuroethics: Scientific, Philosophical and Ethical Dimensions (Cambridge
University Press). His work focuses upon the philosophical basis of pain and
pain medicine, agent-based virtue ethics in medical practice and neuroethics.

Contributors

Dr. Jane Goodall began her landmark study of chimpanzees in Tanzania in
June 1960, under the mentorship of anthropologist and paleontologist Dr. Louis
Leakey. Her work in the Gombe National Park became the foundation of future
primatological research and redefined the relationship between humans and
animals. In 1977, Goodall founded the Jane Goodall Institute (JGI) for Wildlife
Research, Education, and Conservation to continue field research on wild
chimpanzees and to advance the power of individuals to take informed and
compassionate action to improve the environment of all living things. JGI is
active in efforts to protect chimpanzees and their habitats, and is widely
recognized for establishing innovative community-centered conservation and
development programs in Africa. Under the umbrella of JGI the Roots & Shoots
education program has been established in more than 90 countries. Goodall's
scores of honors include the Medal of Tanzania, the National Geographic
Society's Hubbard Medal, Japan's Kyoto Prize, the Prince of Asturias Award for
Technical and Scientific Research, the Benjamin Franklin Medal in Life
Science, and the Gandhi/King Award for Nonviolence. Goodall is a Messenger
of Peace for the United Nations and a Dame of the British Empire.

Jonathan Granoff is an author, attorney, and international peace activist whose
life's work is dedicated to the total elimination of nuclear weapons worldwide.
To that end, he is the current president of the Global Security Institute, a
nonprofit organization committed to the elimination of nuclear weapons. He
also serves as the Co-Chair of the American Bar Association's Committee on
Arms Control and National Security, and as the Vice President of the NGO
Committee on Disarmament, Peace, and Security at the UN, which is regarded
as a primary ally of the international movement for arms control, peace, and
disarmament.

Jenifer Grant is the daughter of Gwen Grant Mellon and stepdaughter of Dr.
William Larimer Mellon, Jr., who in 1956 built Hôpital Albert Schweitzer in
Deschapelles, Haiti, which has provided health care for 50 years. A longtime
educator, she is a founder of the Albert Schweitzer Educational Associates.

Sergey A. Kizima is a professor at the Academy of Public Administration under
the aegis of the President of the Republic of Belarus. He previously served as a
lecturer at the Minsk Institute of Management and a research fellow at the
Belarusian State University in the History Department.

Reverence for Life Revisited: Albert Schweitzer's Relevance Today

Nikki Lindberg is the former director of programs for the Albert Schweitzer
Institute for the Humanities and a founder of the Albert Schweitzer Educational
Associates, an organization devoted to teaching children about Schweitzer's life
and philosophy.

Claudine and Damien Mougin are the Directors of Albert Schweitzer Hospital,
Lambaréné, Gabon.

Benjamin Page has taught philosophy at Quinnipiac for the past three decades.
His professional interests include intercultural dialogue and understanding,
peace and justice, spirituality and science, ethics, Marxism, and philosophies of
health; he was one of the earliest faculty members involved in the development
of Service Learning at Quinnipiac and with David Ives developed and coteaches the seminar on the life and thought of Albert Schweitzer. In addition to
articles in these areas, he has published a study of the mid-1960s Czechoslovak
attempt to reform socialism, an anthology on Marxism and Spirituality, and a
translation of The Consolation of Ontology, by Czech poet-philosopher
and erstwhile underground rock singer Egon Bondy.

Julia Pedroni is a Visiting Assistant Professor of Philosophy, Williams
College, Williamstown, MA, Adjunct Assistant Professor and Fellow of the
Center for Bioethics and Clinical Leadership, Union College and Graduate
College of Union University, and Visiting Fellow of the John P. McGovern
M.D. Center for Health, Humanities and the Human Spirit, University of Texas
Health Sciences Center, Houston. Her ongoing work addresses feminist
philosophy and ethics in healthcare, moral agency, and virtue ethics in clinical
contexts.

John D. Radigan is a professor of philosophy at Paul Smith's College in
upstate New York. He has taught courses in ethics, composition, contemporary
poetry, speech, and Western culture.

Dennis J. Richardson is an Associate Professor of Biology at Quinnipiac
University and co-founder of the Bawa Health Initiative.

A. G. Rud is an Associate Professor in the Department of Educational Studies,
College of Education, at Purdue University. He is currently writing a book on
Albert Schweitzer's legacy of educational thought and practice.

Contributors

René Wadlow is the editor of www.transnational-perspectives.org, an online
journal of world politics, and an NGO representative to the United Nations, in
Geneva. He was formerly a professor and director of research at the Graduate
Institute of Development Studies, University of Geneva. From 1961 through
1963, he was an advisor in the Ministry of National Education of Gabon.

Christopher Weaver is a senior at Quinnipiac University studying biology and
philosophy with aspirations to go to medical school and also obtain a degree in
public health. His experiences in Nicaragua with the Albert Schweitzer Institute
drove him to explore Schweitzer's work and philosophy and, in particular, how
Schweitzer's thought applies to our world today. This work was put into
practice when he completed a Health Internship in Tanzania, Africa during the
summer of 2006.
